Blood and Silk

Blood and Silk

A novel — first draft

Part One The house in the Dye Yards

Chapter 1 The part that goes backward

The lock worked perfectly while the door was open.

Sariel had demonstrated this twice. She had turned the key, drawn the bolt, released it, and watched the little tongue return to its place with a click that any reasonable person would have accepted. Then Hest had shut the door. The key had turned halfway and stopped.

“Don’t force it,” Hest said.

“I wasn’t.”

“You were about to.”

Sariel let go of the key. The door belonged to a cabinet in which they kept bowls. There was no reason for a cabinet containing bowls to have a lock, except that Hest had bought the cabinet cheaply because its lock was broken and had considered this a useful coincidence.

“Someone’s changed the hanging.”

“Someone has put the spring in backward.”

“It works when it’s open.”

Hest leaned over her shoulder. She smelled of soap, metal, and the pear she had been eating. A brown drip had dried on the front of her work apron. She set half the pear on the windowsill, where Sariel could reach it, and placed a finger against the cabinet door.

“This pushes here when it’s shut. Your spring lifts the tail against the case. Open, there’s nothing touching it. You could put a dead beetle in there and admire how well it worked.”

“You could just leave it unlocked.”

“Yes. And then I’d have to find you something else to do.”

Below the window, a barge was coming into the narrow canal with so much wool on it that its steersman appeared to be standing in a hedge. He was arguing with a woman on the landing. Sariel had been listening to the argument and watching the cabinet at the same time. The woman maintained that the far mooring belonged to her. The man maintained that the current did not respect her property. Sariel thought he was going to lose a bale before the matter was settled.

Hest took the key and got the door open without seeming to do anything. That was particularly irritating.

“How?”

“Lifted it. You were right about the hanging. It wasn’t the only thing wrong.”

She left Sariel with the cabinet and went to wash her hands. Sariel pulled the hinge pin, laid the door flat, and removed the lock. With the lid off, the mistake looked obvious. The long end of the spring lay where the short end should bear. She had put it in as she remembered seeing Hest do it. Hest must have been looking at the mechanism from the other side.

She turned the spring around. It took three attempts to get it beneath the little bridge without sending it into the bowls.

“There’s a pear behind you,” Hest called.

“I know.”

“It’s getting old.”

Sariel ate it before testing the lock again.

Across the room, Thane had taken the legs off a table. There had been nothing wrong with the table that Sariel had noticed, but it now lay on its back with its contents on the floor around it, and Thane was looking for a piece he had put somewhere safe. Kestra, whose copying work had formerly been on the table, sat on a crate beside him holding an ink bottle.

“Under your foot,” she said.

“That isn’t it.”

“The other foot.”

Thane looked down.

“It was going to rock,” he explained.

“It has stopped.”

“If you let me finish—”

“I have a page drying on the stairs.”

He moved his foot carefully.

At the far end of the room, Benjamin was practicing the same step beside the long bench. Step back, turn, bring the wooden sword across. Orren watched from a chair, one leg out before him, working a leather thong through a buckle. Benjamin completed the movement and looked at him.

“Again,” Orren said.

Benjamin did it again. This time his heel struck the bench.

“Move the bench.”

“It won’t be a bench.”

“Then it’ll be something else. Look before you go.”

Benjamin moved the bench, smiling as though the instruction had been a joke. Orren gave him a look over the buckle and went back to threading it.

Sariel liked this hour in the house. Everyone was still pretending there would be time to finish what they had started. The kitchen door was open. From within came the smell of onions and a quarrel about the good lamp. Elena wanted it for a boy’s finger; whoever was cooking wanted to see what was in the pot. The boy was arguing that the finger did not hurt. Elena told him to take it out of his mouth, then.

Sariel tested the lock with the door standing on its edge. She pressed against the bolt with her thumb and turned the key. It moved.

She tried harder. It still moved.

“Hest.”

Hest came back, took the door, and tried it herself.

“Good. Put it on.”

“It was the spring.”

“Yes.”

“And the hanging.”

“Yes.”

Sariel waited. Hest reached into her apron and handed her a thin strip of brass.

“For the lower hinge.”

She was very difficult to please conspicuously. Later, if someone asked who had repaired the cabinet, she would say Sariel as though there had never been any possibility of another answer.

The Green Crane kept its dye in a storeroom above the waterline. That was sensible. Its owner kept the key on a hook behind the weighing desk. That was less sensible, except that the desk was occupied from sunrise until the yard shut, and the yard’s two gates were watched.

Sariel stood outside the landward gate with a bundle of torn blue cloth and tried to look as though she had already had a bad day.

This was not difficult. The bundle was heavier than Hest had said it would be. Blue dust had worked into the crease of her elbow. The man at the gate had asked the same question three times.

“I don’t know his name,” she said. “He said bring it back if it bled.”

“Everything bleeds if you scrub it.”

“I didn’t scrub it. I hung it by the stove. The floor’s blue. My mistress says someone here can come and look at the floor if you don’t believe me.”

He looked behind her, as though hoping to find a more senior person to insult. Sariel shifted the bundle and let a damp corner fall against his clean threshold.

“All right. You can wait there. Don’t go into the yard.”

He pointed to the space by the desk. Sariel thanked him in the voice of someone who had forgotten why she had once wanted this job. Inside, a clerk was adding a column, moving his lips silently. He had a cup with no handle and a fresh cut on his chin.

She waited through the end of the column. The yard smelled of vinegar and wet wood. Two men rolled an empty cask toward the water gate. One wore a red cloth round his hair; the other walked with his right boot turned out. Neither looked at her. Beyond them, a third man sat on a bale eating something out of paper.

Hest had asked for three things. How the water gate was fastened. Who remained after the evening bell. Whether the crate marked with a double black line had been moved.

Sariel could see the gate. A chain, looped twice through an iron staple, hung down on the yard side. It was secured by a padlock large enough to be visible from where she stood. The chain’s slack would permit a hand to go through, but a hand on its own would be useless. The staple passed through the timber and was peened over on the other face. Cutting the wood would make noise.

She set the blue bundle on the edge of the weighing platform.

“Not there,” the clerk said.

“Sorry.”

She lifted it, caught a loose strand on the platform, and let the bundle unravel. Blue strips slid over the clerk’s shoes.

He stood up so quickly that his stool hit the desk. Sariel dropped to her knees, apologizing. While he pulled his foot out of the cloth, she looked beneath the desk. The key hung on the back of the right leg. A square head, three cuts, one tooth shorter than the others. She could have taken it. He would notice before she got out of the gate. Hest had not asked her to take it.

“Who sold you this?” he demanded.

“A man with a wart.”

He shut his eyes. There were evidently several candidates.

One of the yard men laughed. The clerk looked at him, and he stopped.

Sariel gathered the cloth slowly. From the floor she could see through the legs of a cart into the storeroom. Four crates stood against the wall. The marked one was at the front. A woman came out carrying a little shovel, wiped it on her apron, and hung it on a peg. When she turned to pull the door shut, she used both hands and lifted.

The door sagged. Sariel watched the place where its lower corner scraped the ground.

“If you come back in the morning,” the clerk said, “the dyer will be here.”

“I can’t take all this home.”

“You brought it.”

“She’ll think I didn’t ask.”

He looked at her face for the first time. She let him. In her old guild, she had been taught to show either anger or fear because those were the feelings people knew what to do with. Hest had suggested, once, that most people recognized tiredness too.

“I’ll write you a note.”

“Thank you.”

While he wrote, she asked whether someone would be there early. He said the night porter would let her wait under the arch until the yard opened. She asked whether that was the man at the gate. It was not. The night porter was old and could not lift the weighing stones, so the clerk would prefer that she bring only a sample.

Sariel folded the note with great care. She thanked him again and took the cloth away.

At the next corner she stopped to ease her arms. The note said, in a large impatient hand, that a complaint had been received concerning cloth which might not have been bought at the Green Crane at all. She liked the clerk a little for putting it that way. She hoped he would not be blamed for what happened that night.

“The chain’s new,” she told Hest. “The gate isn’t. The staple’s good. You won’t pull it out.”

“I wasn’t planning to.”

“You said yesterday—”

“That was before someone had looked.”

They sat at the repaired table. It no longer rocked. Kestra had reclaimed one end and was copying with her left hand shielding the page from the rest of them. Tavia sat beside Zara, reading a bill she had been asked to interpret. Finn was under the table trying to recover a piece of sausage from the fox, who had found somewhere too narrow for Finn’s shoulders.

Sariel described the storeroom door. Hest asked her how far it scraped, and on which side. Orren drew the yard with a finger in the dust on the tabletop. Thane wiped the dust away before realizing what he had done.

“Sorry.”

Orren drew it on a scrap of paper instead.

“If I lift here,” Hest said, “does the bolt come free of the keep?”

“Not enough. But the gap at the top gets wider.”

Hest looked at her. Sariel moved a bowl, took a spoon, and showed the angle with the spoon’s handle.

“A strip round the back. Like the cabinet, except the spring’s the right way.”

“Likely.”

“I can do it.”

“You can try. I’ll be beside you.”

Benjamin came over from the bench. “What am I doing?”

“Keeping the boat where we leave it,” Orren said.

“I can do more than that.”

“Can you keep a loaded boat still with a barge passing?”

Benjamin considered. “If there’s a ring.”

“There isn’t.”

Orren moved the drawing so that Benjamin could see it. They began discussing a line around the landing’s lower post. After a while Benjamin fetched a length of rope and asked Thane how much strain the particular knot would take.

Kes came in while they were making the knot. He carried a bag of potatoes beneath one arm and a little paper packet in his good hand.

“Who has broken the table?”

“It’s better,” Thane said.

Kes set the potatoes down. The table remained level. He looked disappointed.

“What are those?” Finn asked from the floor.

“Medicine.”

“They’re sweets.”

“For the disposition. You don’t need one. Your disposition is excellent.”

Finn got out from under the table, leaving the fox to finish the sausage. Kes gave him a sweet and asked whether he intended to leave the animal’s dinner bill in his own name or the guild’s. Finn said the fox had caught two rats. Kes said he had paid for neither rat and could see no reason to thank it.

Elena emerged from the kitchen with the lamp. There was a little blood on her cuff and a boy following her whose finger had been wrapped into an indignity almost as large as his fist. Kes gave him a sweet too. The boy tried to take a second, was caught, and looked so wounded that Kes gave him the smallest remaining one on the understanding that this concluded their arrangement.

“He’s not staying,” the cook called.

“Of course not,” Kes said. “Put another bowl out.”

The boy slipped into the place beside Finn. Sariel watched him look around the table, trying to work out which adult he had to please. She nudged the bread within his reach.

Orren was asking Hest about a job in the southern warehouses. She wanted to wait until the owner traveled; he wanted to go before the rains made the roof approach treacherous. They argued with the easy persistence of people who had already done so twice and expected another opportunity. Hest said she had promised herself a week away afterward. Orren asked who would mend everyone’s dreadful locks. She said that was what apprentices were for, and nodded at Sariel without looking at her.

Sariel took a larger piece of bread than she had meant to.

The old night porter had a cough and a comfortable chair. Sariel could hear the cough from the water. The chair she inferred from the time he took to get up when Orren knocked at the landward gate.

Their boat lay below the landing, where the canal wall hid it from anyone standing in the yard. Benjamin braced the rope around the lower post with both hands. Thane waited at the stern. Hest touched Sariel’s shoulder and pointed up.

Sariel went first.

The chain had been drawn through the staple as she had seen it that afternoon. She passed the narrow strip through the gap beside the gate, worked it around the padlock’s shackle, and lifted. Nothing moved. The chain was carrying the gate’s weight.

Hest put her shoulder against the timber and took that weight. Sariel felt the difference in the strip at once. She lifted again, held, and slipped a pick into the lock. The first plate moved. The second did not. Her hands wanted to hurry because a sound had come from the landward arch.

Hest’s fingers closed briefly on her wrist.

Sariel let the tension ease and tried the second plate again. It lifted. The padlock opened in her palm.

They were inside before the porter finished telling Orren that the Green Crane did not take deliveries after dark. Orren apologized. He had the wrong yard, then. Did the porter know whether the man selling lampblack had moved? It had been here when his brother came last year.

At the storeroom Hest lifted the door as the dyer had done. Sariel fed the strip through the wider gap at its upper edge. She had to feel for the back of the latch. The first thing she touched was a cobweb thick enough to mistake for cloth. She pulled back, wiped the strip on her sleeve, and went in lower.

The latch lifted.

She caught the door before it swung into a stacked cask.

Hest smiled. It was a quick expression, almost all on one side, and it warmed Sariel more than praise would have. Then they were at the crate, getting the sling beneath it.

It was heavier than the practice weight. Hest made no comment. They moved it in stages, one end and then the other, stopping when the porter coughed. At the water gate, Thane came up to take the near corner. Together they lowered it to the boat.

A barge was passing.

Benjamin’s arms shook as the wash moved beneath the hull. The rope creaked against the post. Sariel held the corner of the crate away from the wall while Thane shifted it inward, and Hest kept the sling from catching. Benjamin made a small, angry sound through his teeth. The boat steadied.

“There,” Thane whispered. “It’s down.”

Benjamin let out his breath and looked at his hands as though he had been introduced to them.

Hest locked the water gate behind them. The porter would find nothing broken when he made his morning round. Orren was still apologizing for his brother’s poor directions.

They pushed away. At the next landing, Orren came down the steps and joined them, and the five bent over their work as the canal carried them beneath a bridge. Sariel sat on the crate and felt, beneath the wood, the weight she had helped remove from a locked room.

On the return, Benjamin showed her the raw place the rope had made across his palm.

“You kept it still,” she said.

“I noticed.”

He was trying not to smile. She nudged his boot with hers and he stopped trying.

By breakfast the crate was gone to its buyer. Zara had counted the payment twice. Kes put aside the amount the factor had paid for the dye, which Sariel thought unusually considerate until Hest told her whose protection the factor had purchased.

“Serafine’s?”

Hest was washing blue from the crease of her thumb. It had got there through a split in the crate.

“She’ll send someone.”

Sariel remembered the woman sitting at their table in gloves she had taken off because Kes’s daughter teased her. She had brought files wrapped in oiled cloth, and paper so smooth that Kestra had stroked it before writing on it. She had laughed at Finn’s fox taking a place on the bench.

“Will she be angry?”

Hest looked at the blue water.

“She might come herself.”

Chapter 2 People who come to the kitchen

Elena was trying to thread a needle when Serafine arrived.

The eye was wide enough. The thread was too soft, a strand pulled from a length of linen that had already been washed until the weave opened. She wetted its end, twisted it, and tried again. Across the table the boy whose finger she had dressed was eating an apple with his injured hand held out to one side. His mother had brought the apple. She had also brought six small onions, as payment, and was apologizing for their size.

“They go in the pot the same,” Elena told her.

“They won’t keep.”

“Then we’ll eat them before they have to.”

The thread went through. Elena drew it carefully and made a knot. She was repairing the mouth of a medicine bag. The last sound linen she had put aside had gone round the boy’s finger, which he was now inspecting with concern.

“Does it hurt?”

“There’s apple on it.”

“That will be all right.”

“You said to keep it clean.”

“I meant don’t put it in the canal.”

His mother touched the back of his head. He leaned against her without looking up.

From the front room came the sound of Kes greeting a visitor. Elena recognized the answering voice before she could make out the words. She looked through the kitchen doorway and saw Serafine standing beside the repaired table, taking one glove off finger by finger.

Her dress was a brown so dark that it seemed black until the light touched the folds. She had worn blue when Elena last saw her. Elena remembered because blue lint from the hem had caught on a splinter beneath the table, and she had put it between the leaves of her handbook as a marker. The same evening Serafine had brought a little case of needles and a stoppered bottle of tincture. Elena had never owned needles so fine. She had used the last unbent one on a cut above a bargeman’s eye and sent him home with the instruction to return if the lid swelled shut. He had returned to show her that it had not.

Kes drew out a chair. Serafine laid her gloves beside it but did not sit.

“Have you come for supper?”

“I hoped we could settle a small matter first.”

There was something formal in the way she said it. Elena put the needle into the bag’s hem. She told the boy’s mother that he could use the finger gently and that she wanted to look at it again in two days. The woman thanked her and began manoeuvring him toward the outer door. Kes stepped aside to let them pass. Serafine smiled at the boy. He stared at her until his mother pulled his sleeve.

When they had gone, Kes left the door open.

Elena could not hear all of the visitor’s explanation. A cart rattled over the bridge, and someone upstairs began playing a passage on a reed pipe which had been attempted several times that week. She heard the factor’s name, then the word protection. Kes listened with his palms on the table. One hand had all its fingers. The other rested at an angle, its two remaining fingers curled beneath the edge.

He fetched the money he had set aside and pushed it toward her.

“You don’t get to sell him safety from us.”

Elena stopped stitching.

“Someone has to settle these things,” Serafine said.

“We have settled it.”

Hest, who was sharpening a little blade near the window, laid it down. Orren stood by the stair with a cup and a drying cloth in his hands. Serafine glanced toward them, then back at Kes. Elena wondered whether she had expected him to take her into his small office, where a curtain could be pulled across the doorway.

They talked for several minutes. The money remained between them. Serafine said that others would ask what her word was worth. Kes asked what he was supposed to tell the people who came to him. She said that certain arrangements could be made. He asked who would make them. Her patience sounded almost affectionate, which made Elena want him to answer more gently.

Then Kes rubbed his eyes with the heel of his hand.

“Serafine. Some people are allowed to tell you no.”

The pipe upstairs stopped. Elena could hear water knocking against the landing.

Serafine looked at Kes for a long time. She picked up the money, put on her gloves, and kissed his cheek. She thanked him for asking her to supper and said she could not stay. At the doorway she paused to let a man carrying a basket go past her. She held the door while he turned the basket sideways.

After she left, Kes went into the kitchen and took one of the small onions from the table.

“These are good.”

“They won’t keep,” Elena said.

“Nothing does if you eat it.”

He bit into it raw. She watched his eyes water and gave him a cup.

Serafine’s first offer came in writing.

Tavia read it aloud after supper. There were good terms. The Coin could keep the warehouse, choose much of its work, and bring disputes to people who would be obliged to hear them. There were provisions for the infirm and the injured. Elena listened harder at that part. She imagined the cupboard full, the linen bought before anyone needed it, a patient who could be kept three more days because there was a bed available.

Then Tavia reached the requirements concerning protected cargo and information.

“How much information?” Thane asked.

“It doesn’t say.”

“About our own work?”

“It doesn’t say that either.”

Through the kitchen doorway Elena could see the boy whose finger she had dressed. He had come back with his mother and was helping Finn coax the fox out from under a chair. Elena wondered whether their names counted as information.

Hest wanted to know who would decide when a dispute had been settled. Orren wanted to know what happened if the Court’s settlement was one they could not accept. Zara said they should ask for the boundaries to be written more clearly. She had lost a business once through trusting that an agreement meant what everyone in the room had said it meant. Kes listened without interrupting her.

“If they write it properly,” Elena said, “could we accept?”

The room became still enough that she could hear the boy pushing something across the floor in the kitchen.

Kes looked at her. “What would you need it to say?”

She had expected a refusal. The question left her without a prepared answer.

“That people can leave. That we aren’t required to give someone back because somebody paid to have them. That we can treat anyone.”

“Write those down,” Kes told Tavia.

“And the money,” Zara said. “What each of us is paid. Not simply what the house receives.”

Tavia wrote that too. Benjamin asked whether the Court would send people to help defend the warehouse if someone attacked it. Orren asked who Benjamin thought might attack it. Benjamin became red and said that he was asking a sensible question. Orren reached across and squeezed his shoulder.

They sent back the offer unsigned, with their questions. An answer came after two days. Certain sums improved. Certain phrases grew longer. The part about people who wished to leave did not change.

At the next meeting Elena said they could not accept. She found herself looking at the medicine cupboard while she said it.

Kes asked everyone in the room. He did not ask them to vote on whether the Court was wicked. He asked whether they could do their work under the proposed terms. Some answered at once. Zara took a long time. The boy’s mother said she had no right to answer, and Hest asked who had told her that.

In the end the reply went back unsigned except by Kes. He told Tavia he would not send the Court a convenient list of everyone who had refused it.

Afterward Elena found the boy asleep with his head in his mother’s lap. The mother looked up as she approached.

“Are we making trouble?”

“No.”

The woman continued looking at her.

Elena pulled out a chair. “I don’t think you should walk home alone tonight.”

They arranged for Benjamin to go with them. He was pleased to be asked, and brought back the bowl the woman had borrowed the week before.

The first person Elena understood to have come because of the dispute was a runner with a torn boot. He had slept in the open for two nights and was shivering too hard to hold the bowl Kes gave him. He kept asking who had been told he was there.

“No one who needs telling,” Kes said.

“That isn’t the same.”

“No. It isn’t.”

Elena put a folded cloth around the bowl so he could grip it. He had a blister that had broken beneath his heel. Getting the boot off was painful, and he made her stop twice before he would let her continue. He apologized each time. On the third occasion she told him he could ask her to stop without apologizing, and he looked so suspicious that she wished she had said nothing.

He stayed three days. Sariel brought him another pair of boots, though Elena did not ask from where. On the third evening Kes told him that there was a barge leaving before dawn. He could work his passage if he was willing to spend it among hides.

“How much?” the runner asked.

Kes named a sum.

“I haven’t got it.”

“I know. You have a brother at the other end.”

The runner looked down at his bowl.

“He won’t want to pay.”

“Then you can work for him until he does.”

“And after?”

“After what?”

“After that.”

Kes considered the question. “I suggest you buy boots that fit.”

Elena saw the young man’s mouth move. For a moment she thought he would cry. Instead he asked whether the hides were wet or dry. Kes said he had forgotten to ask, and the runner began laughing. It was not a pleasant laugh, but when it stopped he could eat.

The kitchen became crowded. Two brothers who knew a route through the Dye Yards slept upstairs. One spent an afternoon repairing a shutter so he would have something useful to do. People did not all arrive at once, and they were not all grateful. A woman who had been offered work at private suppers came with two bundles and an opinion of every chair in the house. She had sewn for years. She knew the value of linen better than anyone there and objected to the way they dried it above the stove. By the second day Elena was taking her mending to her. By the fourth, the woman had made a list of everything in the medicine cupboard which needed a proper bag.

She was also afraid to go into the street.

Elena noticed because the woman found a different reason each time someone asked her to fetch something. On one occasion she was busy. On another she had no clean stockings. When Elena offered to lend her some, she became angry and accused the household of thinking it had bought her labor for a bowl of soup.

Elena sat with the rejected stockings in her lap until Tavia came and took them away.

“She knows you meant well.”

“It didn’t sound like it.”

“You were trying to solve the thing she said instead of the thing she couldn’t say.”

“And you know what that is?”

“No. But I know the stockings weren’t very important.”

The next morning the woman asked Elena for them. She said nothing about the previous evening. Elena handed them over, then said that she had to go to the apothecary and would like company. They walked there together. The woman kept to the inside of the pavement, touching the walls with her fingertips when they passed a doorway.

At the apothecary Elena asked for a preparation she had used under her former teacher. The man behind the counter asked who had prescribed it. She said she knew how to use it. He waited. She named her teacher. The man’s expression changed.

“You aren’t working there now.”

“No.”

“I’ll need his instruction.”

“He didn’t instruct me to buy bandages either.”

“Bandages aren’t that preparation.”

She could have argued. She knew the quantity she needed, the danger of too much, the signs that would make her stop. She also knew that the man would tell someone she had argued. Her hands became cold. She bought what she could and went outside carrying the lighter parcel.

The seamstress waited until they had turned the corner.

“Was it your fault?”

Elena looked at her.

“Whatever they say you did.”

The answer she ordinarily gave was ready. Her master had refused a treatment because the patient’s family could not pay. She had tried it without his permission. It had helped for a while. Then the fever had returned, and by the time he saw the patient there had been little he could do. He had said that Elena’s intervention had concealed the danger. She did not know whether he believed that. She did not know, on some nights, whether he was right.

“I don’t know all of it,” she said.

The woman nodded. “That’s a tiresome answer to have.”

They went home by the market. The seamstress bought two needles with money she had sewn into her cuff, then gave one to Elena without making a ceremony of it.

Kes’s daughter wrote about a cook who could make a whole house miserable by standing quietly beside a stove.

Elena read that letter to Kes while he sorted receipts. He could read it himself. He said that if he did he would have to look for his spectacles, and they were both aware that the spectacles lay beside his hand. Elena read it anyway. His daughter had been at the Pillow long enough to discover errors in the flour accounts and to stop finding the private room strange. She liked having a door which no one expected her to leave open. She had bought a little plant and was uncertain whether it was meant to live indoors.

“Ask Thane,” Kes said.

“About the plant?”

“He’ll know someone who can make a shelf for it. She’ll put it on something foolish.”

The letter ended with a description of someone learning to sign her own name. Elena read that portion twice because Kes asked her to. Then he took the page, smoothed its crease, and placed it among the papers he kept in the top drawer.

Elena asked whether he had been to see the room.

“She’s employed, not imprisoned. She can invite me when she’s ready.”

He said it a little too firmly. Elena smiled and asked whether he had told her that.

“She told me.”

He laughed then, and she did too.

Over the following days the house grew harder to keep orderly. The two brothers left by separate routes. The seamstress found work at a sail loft beyond the northern locks and came back on her first free evening with a great bag of clean scraps. The boy’s finger healed enough for him to complain about being asked to help his mother. A man who had accepted the Coin’s help decided to return to his creditor under revised terms, and Kes let him go although Elena thought the terms sounded dreadful. She argued afterward until Kes asked her what she proposed to do with a man who wanted to walk out of the door.

She thought of the runner asking what happened after he had paid. She went to put away the linen.

There was no letter the following week. Kes said his daughter was busy. He asked Elena whether she thought it improper to send the plant a pot. Elena said she did not think plants were sensitive to that sort of thing. He went out and bought one, then decided it was the wrong size.

Three days later a messenger he had sent returned without an answer. The house, the messenger said, had been busy. He had left the message with someone who said she would see to it.

Kes sat at his desk for a while after the boy went. He put on his spectacles, took them off, and rubbed one lens with the edge of his sleeve.

“I’m going to call this afternoon,” he told Elena.

“Shall I come?”

“No need.”

“I haven’t been that way for a while.”

He looked at her, grateful and annoyed. “I can go and see my own daughter.”

She nodded. He stood with a little effort and put on the good coat he wore when he meant to make a complaint politely. At the kitchen door he stopped to ask what time they would eat. Elena told him. He said he would try to be back.

She watched him cross the bridge. Halfway across he turned toward the little sweet shop beneath the bell-house. He had bought the same sort for his daughter for years. Once she had told Elena she no longer particularly liked them, but would not have hurt him by saying so.

He came out carrying a small paper packet in his good hand. Then the street bent and Elena could no longer see him.

Chapter 3 One night

Kestra had put the same date on three pages before she realized it was wrong.

She scraped the first error carefully. The knife caught a raised fibre and made a tiny hole. Light from the window showed through it. She put the ruined page aside, took the second, and found that she could not remember what day it actually was.

Kes had been gone for two nights.

Hest had made inquiries after the first. She had come back with very little she would say in the front room. Orren had gone out before dawn that morning and returned without taking his coat off. Kestra could hear him speaking to Hest behind the office curtain. Their voices were low. She disliked being on the wrong side of a curtain almost as much as she disliked pretending she was not listening.

She bent over the second page. The commission was a set of shipping articles, copied fairly for a man who could afford beautiful lettering but not, apparently, a sound table to keep his originals on. She could earn enough from it to buy a new penknife. If the knife were sharp, she would not have made the hole. She held that small, manageable grievance in front of everything else.

The messenger hit the outer door hard enough to make the latch jump.

Benjamin let him in. He was a small boy with a wet face and one stocking lower than the other. Kestra recognized him as someone who had eaten with Finn. He clutched a folded sheet in both hands.

“They said—”

Hest came out of the office. She knelt, not quite touching him.

“Start where you can.”

He thrust the sheet at her. There was blood on its edge. Kestra saw the dark smear before she saw anything written. Hest read it once, then stood up and gave it to Orren.

“Where did you get this?”

The boy described a woman at a bridge. He did not know her name. She had known his. She had told him that Kes was in danger and that he had to run. He had run. Now he was shaking so hard that he could not hold the cup Elena brought him.

Hest took the cup and let him drink while she held it. Orren put the note on Kestra’s table.

The Bronze Ledger. One night. Or Kes hangs.

There was no signature.

Kestra read it twice, trying to make the words require some interpretation they had not yet been given. The Bronze Ledger was not a cargo book. Everyone who had worked around the Court knew of it, although people disagreed about what it contained. Some claimed the pages themselves changed when an obligation was incurred. Hest said that was nonsense; people were perfectly capable of writing down a secret without the book offering to help.

It belonged to Serafine. It was kept in the study in her house in the Heights.

“We can’t,” Benjamin said.

He looked furious with himself as soon as the words were out.

Orren pulled the table away from the wall. “Move the copying.”

Kestra gathered the sheets. Her hands steadied as soon as she had something to do.

They did not begin with the lock.

Kestra had expected them to begin with the lock. She had thought there would be a name, a particular maker, and that Hest would either know its weakness or admit that she did not. Instead Orren asked who lived in the house and when the kitchens shut.

“The staff won’t all sleep there,” Tavia said.

“How many leave?”

“I don’t know.”

“Can you find out?”

“Yes.”

He put a mark on a scrap of board. Hest took the charcoal from him and drew a crooked rectangle.

“That’s the service court. Kitchen here. A stair, then the passage behind the reception rooms. The study’s over this end.”

“You’ve been in?” Sariel asked.

“Some years ago.”

“How many?”

Hest made a face. “Enough for you to look.”

Sariel nodded. She leaned over the sketch and asked about the slope outside the wall. Orren said there was a lane used by tradespeople. Thane asked whether the wall was dressed stone or rubble. No one knew. Kestra waited for a question she could answer and disliked herself for wanting one.

“The study’s warded,” she said.

Hest looked up. “I know.”

“It may matter which sort.”

“It does.”

“A fixed ward can be tied to a frame, an object, or the person using it. If it’s tied to the room, opening the safe may not be what wakes it.”

Hest put down the charcoal.

Kestra felt everyone attending to her. She began explaining too quickly: how a command could be renewed, how a protected threshold might distinguish between someone carrying an allowed token and someone merely carrying its impression, how a seal could preserve a silence within a room rather than prevent entry. She knew these things. For several sentences the pleasure of knowing them overcame the reason they needed to be known.

Orren interrupted gently.

“Which one is hers?”

Kestra stopped.

“I haven’t seen it.”

“Then what will let us tell?”

She thought. “A drawing. A good one. Of everything around the study door, not just the mark that looks important.”

Sariel looked at Hest. “Can I get that close?”

“Find out what you can see from the outside first.”

“And if I get in?”

“You come out.”

Hest waited until Sariel nodded.

Benjamin wanted to go with her. Sariel said he would be conspicuous. He looked down at himself, apparently surprised to discover how much room he occupied. Orren sent him to find a cart instead, with instructions about its wheels and the kind of cover it should have.

“Are we stealing the whole house?”

“We may have someone to carry.”

Benjamin’s expression changed. He took the instructions seriously after that.

By midmorning the room had emptied. Elena was arranging the back kitchen, clearing the table and putting water on. Zara was trying to learn which of the Court’s ordinary guards were on duty elsewhere. Finn had gone to carry a message. Kestra remained with the board, the note, and her own books.

The blood on the note had dried to brown. She had not noticed the change while it happened.

She opened the volume she had saved when she left the Academy. It had belonged to the library before she bought it from the clerk who disposed of damaged books. One corner had been burnt. She had given the clerk more than he asked because she did not want him to think she was taking advantage of damage she had helped cause.

Near the middle was a chapter about conditional boundaries. The example was a storeroom whose keeper could cross freely while carrying a particular brass tally. The working did not know whether the keeper had been dismissed that morning. It knew the tally. An accompanying note described an accident in which someone had changed the hinge without restoring the lower inscription.

Kestra read it carefully, then made two lists. What they could observe. What they would have to assume.

She left a wide space beneath the second heading.

Tavia returned smelling of someone else’s perfume.

“Two kitchen maids leave after the evening meal. The housekeeper stays. There are visitors expected, but not at the house in the Heights. One of the drivers thinks Serafine will be out.”

“Thinks?” Orren asked.

“He was told to prepare a carriage. He wasn’t told who would use it.”

She sat and took off a shoe. There was a red mark where its edge had rubbed her heel. Elena came out of the kitchen, saw it, and fetched a little piece of soft cloth without being asked.

“I haven’t been told to wait in a corridor for that long since I was employed,” Tavia said.

“What did you say you wanted?”

“A place copying accounts.”

“Did they offer you one?”

“They wanted references.”

Orren almost smiled.

Sariel came back later, damp to the knees. She had approached from the canal, which ran close beneath the lower gardens, and discovered that the service wall could be reached from a flight of public steps. A delivery boy had left the outer wicket open while he struggled with a hamper. She had taken one end of the hamper and acquired an opinion about the stairs from the cook.

“You went inside?” Hest asked.

“Only to the kitchen passage.”

“Only.”

“There was a tray outside the room. I took it downstairs.”

Hest looked at her for a moment, then held out her hand for the drawing.

Sariel had used the back of the dyer’s note. The study door appeared in rough proportion, with its hinges marked and a line near the floor. Above the lintel she had copied a small device: two curved marks meeting beneath a thorn.

Kestra recognized the broad shape. Livaea. That did not tell her what the ward had been instructed to do.

“This line,” she said. “Was it cut or painted?”

“Cut. Filled with something dark.”

“And these breaks?”

“There were little nails.”

“Metal?”

“Yes.”

“What color?”

Sariel shut her eyes. Kestra waited. She could see Sariel reconstructing the door in her mind, looking past the parts she had thought worth noticing.

“Red. Not rust. The heads were red.”

Kestra put the drawing beside the book. If the metal was copper, the line might be part of a return path. If it was painted iron, the purpose might be entirely different. She said so. Hest listened without impatience.

“Can you come as far as the passage?” Hest asked.

“Yes.”

“That wasn’t a question about whether you’re willing.”

Kestra swallowed. She had never entered a house in order to steal something. She had carried messages, copied things which were not hers to copy, and once distracted a clerk while Zara removed an invoice. She knew how to put a little light into a prepared bead and how to test whether a simple mark remained active. Neither skill would prevent a guard from putting his hand around her neck.

“I can tell you more if I see it.”

“And you can leave when I tell you?”

The answer she wanted to give was that it would depend on what was happening. Hest would hear that as clearly as though she said it.

“Yes.”

Hest nodded. “Then you’ll come.”

Thane made three wooden wedges and a narrow tool with a hooked end. He asked Hest to try the tool. She bent it with her hands and gave it back.

“Too soft.”

He made another. This one she could not bend. She smiled and put it in her roll.

Benjamin brought a cart with a canvas cover, a damaged basket, and a very long account of why the cart had cost more than it ought. Zara listened, asked three questions, and told him he had done fairly well. He seemed more pleased by that than he would have been by unqualified praise.

They ate before sunset because Elena insisted. Nobody wanted much until the food was in front of them. Kestra discovered that she had been hungry for hours. She ate potatoes with salt and tried to keep her hands from shaking against the bowl.

Hest went over the plan while they ate. They would enter through the service wicket after the maids left. Tavia would be at the lower steps, dressed as someone waiting for a servant she intended to complain about. Benjamin would have the cart beyond the lane. Thane would remain with it unless called. Orren, Hest, Sariel, and Kestra would go inside.

“Once we’re at the study, you look,” Hest told Kestra. “You tell us what you can tell. Then you go back to the wicket with Orren.”

Orren would return to the passage after taking her down. Sariel would hold the route while Hest opened the safe.

“If the wicket shuts?” Benjamin asked.

“It won’t,” Thane said.

Hest looked at him.

“It shouldn’t,” he corrected himself. “The wedge goes above the lower hinge. It will look shut from outside.”

“And if it does?”

Thane touched the hooked tool. “There’s a smaller door into the wash yard. Sariel saw the bolt. We can lift it from this side if we can reach it.”

“You need to show me,” Benjamin said.

Sariel showed him on the sketch. She did not make a joke about his size that time.

They agreed on a knock: three slow strokes at the wicket meant someone needed it opened from within. A shout meant come out by whatever route remained. No one was to assume that silence meant the others were safe. Orren would keep time by the little sandglass he used when the kitchen asked how long something had been boiling. Kestra had thought its presence among his equipment funny until he turned it over and made her watch how quickly it emptied.

“We don’t get all night because we were given all night,” he said.

After supper Kestra went upstairs to put away the copying. The page with the hole lay where she had left it. She looked at it with a tenderness she had not expected. A mistake that could be put aside, another sheet that could be bought. She folded the good pages between boards and left them beneath her bed.

Elena came to the doorway.

“Have you eaten enough?”

“Yes.”

“I have an apple.”

Kestra took it. They stood together in the room without finding anything more to say. Elena had been given nothing to do at the house in the Heights. She would wait here with water, linen, and whatever they brought back to her.

“I can do this part,” Kestra said.

“I know.”

“I don’t mean all of it.”

“I know that too.”

Elena held her for a moment, tight enough to make Kestra’s chin press against her shoulder. Then she let go and told her to put on the shoes with the sound soles.

The study door was warmer than the passage.

Kestra noticed it before she could see the inscription properly. The house smelled of wax and some flower she could not name. Downstairs a servant was laughing at something another had said. Their voices carried up the stair and then stopped abruptly when a door shut.

Hest stood beside her. Sariel watched the turn in the passage. Orren had his hand on the wall as though feeling the building breathe.

Kestra knelt and held a bit of fine thread near the lower line. She had worked a little responsiveness into the thread that afternoon. It would incline toward an active mark if she held her hand steady enough. It would also incline toward a draught, or a warm surface, or whatever her trembling fingers made it do. She made herself breathe out slowly.

The thread moved inward.

She repeated the test at the other side. The line returned around the frame and into the room. That meant the boundary was probably complete beyond the threshold, not at it. The small red heads were copper. Each was set within a separate mark so faint that she could only see it when Hest shaded the lamp with her hand.

“It’s active,” Kestra whispered.

Hest waited.

“The door isn’t the whole boundary. Something inside completes it.”

“The safe?”

“It could be.”

“Can we enter?”

Kestra looked at the line again. It had not changed when she approached. It did not point outward. Everything she had learned suggested a ward arranged to react to a condition within the room. She could not tell which condition.

“I think so. I can’t tell you what happens when you touch what’s inside.”

“All right.”

Hest put her hand on Kestra’s shoulder and squeezed once. She seemed pleased. Kestra had expected to feel ashamed of giving so little, but the pleasure came instead, sharp and unwelcome in the silent passage.

Orren touched her elbow. Time to go.

She looked back from the stair. Hest had opened the door. Sariel slipped through behind her, small against the dark room, and the line beneath the frame shone for a moment as though someone had poured a thin stream of oil across it.

Then Orren turned the corner, and Kestra followed him down.

Chapter 4 The empty safe

There was a bowl of fruit on Serafine’s desk.

Sariel had not expected fruit. She had imagined papers, seals, perhaps one of the little knives people laid beside their inkstands when they wanted a visitor to remember that a letter could cause pain. There were papers. There was a knife with an ivory handle. There was also a pear with a bite taken out of it, resting on a folded cloth.

Hest went to the safe. Sariel stood just inside the doorway and listened.

The study was larger than their entire kitchen. A carpet covered most of the floor. Beyond the desk, tall shutters concealed a window facing the canal. Two lamps burned, one beside the writing chair and one on a bracket near the safe. Hest looked at the second lamp before she knelt.

“Someone’s been considerate,” she murmured.

Sariel watched the passage. She could still hear Orren’s feet on the stair, then a soft sound which might have been a door below. The house had resumed its ordinary noises around them. A board shifted as something warm cooled. Water moved through a pipe behind the wall.

Hest unfolded her roll on the carpet. The safe was set into the wall at the height of a seated person’s knees. Its iron face was dark, almost purple. The lock had a narrow mouth behind a sliding plate. Above it, engraved so lightly that Sariel had mistaken the shape for a scratch, was the same thorn that Kestra had examined at the threshold.

“Look at the lamp,” Hest whispered.

Sariel did. Its bracket had been polished recently. Beneath it, on the wall, a pale rectangle showed where something had hung for a long time and been removed. The safe was clear of the room’s other furniture. There was space to kneel comfortably. There was even a small cushion under the writing chair which would have served the purpose.

“Do we go?” Sariel asked.

Hest held still.

The answer did not come quickly. Sariel wished she had not asked. She wanted Hest to know what the correct thing was without having to consider it. She wanted the note to be an ordinary threat which an ordinary theft could answer.

“Go to the shutters,” Hest said. “Quietly. See how they open.”

Sariel crossed the room along the carpet’s edge. She touched nothing on the desk. The shutters were secured by a vertical bar resting in two metal cups. She lifted it free, leaned it against the wall, and opened one shutter a finger’s breadth. Beyond the glass was a narrow balcony with a stone balustrade. The canal lay far below it. No useful stair. A vine, too thin to take anyone’s weight, climbed the adjoining wall.

She reported it. Hest nodded.

“Leave the bar out.”

She began working on the safe.

Sariel had watched her open difficult locks before. Hest usually talked while she worked, partly for the pupil and partly, Sariel suspected, because she disliked having her hands occupied while her mouth did nothing. Tonight she was silent. She tried one tool, took it out, and chose another without looking at the roll. When she reached the third, a tiny click came from within the safe.

Sariel looked back at the doorway. The copper heads in the frame had begun to glow a dull red. They had shown no light a moment before. She opened her mouth, then shut it, unwilling to startle Hest at the wrong instant.

“The door,” she said softly.

“I see it.”

Hest had not turned her head.

She drew the bolt. The safe’s door came outward with a faint sigh, as though the air inside were warmer. Hest looked into it. She remained kneeling for so long that Sariel took a step toward her.

“What?”

Hest lifted one sheet of paper.

Sariel saw the bare lead lining behind it.

“Back,” Hest said.

The door to the study shut.

Sariel reached it before she had decided to move. The latch would not rise. She put her shoulder against the wood, pulled with both hands, and felt a heat so sudden that she thought she had put her palm into water just off the boil.

Hest caught the back of her coat and dragged her away.

“Hands off.”

The sheet of paper fell between them. Sariel saw the words watched you for a year before Hest kicked it clear of their feet.

The lamp by the safe brightened. Its flame did not grow upward. It spread in a thin blue line along the bracket, entered the pale rectangle on the wall, and traveled toward the floor. Something crackled inside the plaster.

Hest took the ivory-handled knife from the desk and pushed its point beneath one of the copper heads. The handle split. She swore, quietly and with precision.

“Window,” she said.

Sariel ran back to the shutters. The catch on the inner frame was stiff. She worked it loose with the heel of her hand, using her sleeve this time. When the window opened, air swept into the room and lifted the loose papers on the desk. One slid toward the line of blue at the wall. Its edge blackened.

The balcony was barely deep enough to stand on. Sariel stepped out, leaned over, and looked down. A boat passed in the canal. The man at its stern had not looked up. Even if he did, there was no way to put three people into his boat from this height without breaking them.

Three. Orren was outside the room. He might already be coming back.

She turned to tell Hest, but Hest had gone to one knee beside the door. A fold of her coat had caught against the lower inscription. It smoked where the cloth touched the frame. She tried to pull it free and seemed unable to judge how much effort to use.

Sariel seized her arm.

“Hest.”

Hest looked up. Her eyes were clear and furious.

“Don’t touch the frame.”

Sariel drew her own knife and cut the smoking cloth. Hest got her foot beneath her. For a moment it seemed that would be enough. Then the floor beneath the carpet cracked, the door shuddered, and the heat drove them both back.

Someone struck the door from outside.

Orren.

“Away from it!”

They moved. Hest stumbled toward the desk. Sariel caught her before she hit its corner. The other woman’s arm was horribly hot through her sleeve. She had been nearer the safe’s lamp and had taken the first burst of heat across her shoulder.

Orren struck again. The latch tore free of its plate. The door opened a hand’s breadth, and smoke rushed through the gap.

“Sariel. Come.”

She looked at Hest.

“Go,” Hest said. “Keep it open.”

Sariel squeezed through. Orren had wrapped his left arm in a curtain pulled from somewhere along the passage. He put that arm against the door and forced it farther. Sariel slid Thane’s narrow wedge beneath the lower edge. It caught. She put the second wedge above it and pressed with her boot.

The wood began to smoke around the wedges.

“I can reach her,” Sariel said.

Orren was already going in.

The passage felt cold after the room, although the wall was hot against her back. Sariel pushed with both feet and held the wedge’s upper end with the narrow strip she had used at the Green Crane. It was too thin for the job. It bent. She straightened it against the floor and pushed it in again.

From within came a crash and a sound she had never heard Hest make.

She wanted to go after them. She could see part of Orren’s back through the gap. His coat was smoking at the shoulder. If she could get past him she might help lift Hest, might do something better than sit here while the wood burned against the soles of her shoes.

The door moved half an inch. The wedge spat a thin curl of black wood onto her ankle.

She pressed harder.

Orren came back carrying Hest sideways, one arm under her knees and the other across her back. Her head struck the doorframe. He swore and turned her, apologizing as though he had been clumsy helping her out of a boat. His hair had singed. There was a bright patch on his neck where the skin had gone red and wet.

“All right,” he said to Sariel. “All right. Let it.”

She pulled her feet away. The door slammed so hard that one wedge snapped in two.

For a moment none of them moved. Orren lowered Hest onto the passage floor and leaned over her, coughing. Sariel stood because she was afraid that if she stayed sitting she would not be able to get up. Her legs shook. Hest tried to say something and coughed instead.

“Can you walk?” Orren asked her.

Hest lifted one hand in an obscene answer. He laughed, a short harsh sound which turned into another cough.

“Good. I was hoping.”

At the lower end of the passage a door opened. A servant stood staring at them. Behind her came a man holding a short staff.

“Fire,” Sariel said.

The servant looked past her to the smoke emerging around the study door. Her face changed. She went back through the doorway. The man with the staff did not.

Orren stood in front of Hest. Sariel moved to the wall, trying to get an angle. The man watched both of them. He was older than Benjamin, younger than Orren. He looked as though this was a great deal more than he had expected from the evening.

“You can let us go,” Sariel said.

He tightened his hands on the staff.

Orren took a step forward. The man struck too soon. Orren caught the staff against the wall with his wrapped arm and hit him once in the stomach. The man folded over it. Orren took the staff, laid it down beyond his reach, and pushed him into the open room.

Sariel shut the door and turned the key which, by an extraordinary kindness of ordinary household arrangements, had been left in the lock.

“Come on,” Orren said.

She helped Hest stand. The older woman was trying to hold her burnt shoulder away from her clothing. She smelled of singed wool and something beneath it that Sariel did not want to recognize.

They went down the passage with Hest between them.

At the stair, Hest stopped.

“Those.”

Sariel looked where she was pointing. A small writing table stood in an alcove, with three books and a bundle of papers on it. They might have been kitchen accounts. They might have been a list of guests. Sariel could not tell.

“Take them.”

“We need—”

“Take something.”

Sariel put the books inside her coat. The papers would not fit, so she passed them to Orren, who pushed them beneath his belt with the indifferent economy of a man who had acquired more urgent discomforts. Hest was looking back up the passage. Sariel put an arm around her waist and made her continue.

The service wicket was shut.

Not merely shut. A second bar had been dropped across it. Thane’s wedge lay on the paving, unburned and useless. Someone had found it.

Sariel knocked three slow strokes. She could hear movement beyond the wood. Benjamin answered once, then tried to lift the gate. The bar remained where it was.

“Wash yard,” she called through the gap. “The little door.”

Orren had put Hest down against the kitchen wall. He stood over her, breathing through his mouth. The band of cloth on his arm had stuck to the skin. Sariel looked away quickly. He had seen her see it, and she hated that.

The wash-yard door was reached through a covered passage with tubs against one wall. Water lay in the channels between the paving stones. Above, an open window spilled yellow light. Someone was ringing a handbell inside the house. Sariel could not tell whether it was an alarm or an instruction for servants to come.

The bolt on the little door was exactly where she had drawn it. That was a relief so great that she nearly laughed. She lifted it, pulled, and found the door secured from the other side as well.

“Benjamin.”

“I’m here.”

“There’s something outside.”

“A hasp. Hold.”

Metal scraped. Thane’s voice, low and irritated, told him to move his hand. A moment later the hasp lifted. Sariel pulled the door inward and found Benjamin standing so close that he nearly fell through.

He looked past her.

“Where’s Orren?”

“Help me carry Hest.”

For an instant he seemed not to understand. Then he went past her at a run.

They got Hest through the small doorway with great difficulty. Her burnt shoulder struck the jamb and she cried out. Sariel had never felt so angry with a building. Orren came after them, took two steps into the lane, and bent over the gutter, coughing until his whole body shook.

Tavia stood at the turn in the lane. She looked once toward the house and waved them forward.

“Now.”

Thane brought the cart. Benjamin climbed into it and reached down. He and Sariel lifted Hest while Orren steadied her legs. Kestra appeared from the shadow of the wall carrying the smaller wedge and looking as though she had been left outside the world. Sariel wanted to shout at her, although she had done exactly what she had been told.

“Take the books,” she said instead.

Kestra took them. She looked at the first cover, then at Sariel.

Sariel shook her head.

They put Orren beside Hest, though he protested that he could walk. Tavia came back from the corner and sat by the cart’s opening with a basket in her lap, hiding as much of them as she could. Thane took the shafts. Benjamin pushed from behind. The wheels, for which he had paid too much, moved without a sound.

Elena had put the good lamp on the kitchen table.

Sariel remembered this absurdly clearly afterward. The flame had been trimmed. There were clean bowls beside it and a stack of cloths. A pot of water steamed on the stove. Elena was standing in the doorway before the cart stopped, and when she saw Hest she ceased looking at anyone else.

They carried Hest inside. Orren came under his own power until Elena told him to sit. He began to argue. She pointed at the chair, and he sat.

“What happened?”

“Ward,” Kestra said. “Heat. Smoke.”

“How long?”

No one answered.

Elena looked at Sariel. “Help me with her coat.”

Hest said they would have to cut it. Elena did. She cut along the seam, slowly where the cloth had stuck, and asked for water which had been allowed to cool. Sariel fetched it. She was glad of the distance to the stove. She was ashamed of being glad.

Benjamin stood beside Orren holding a cup. Orren could not take a full breath without coughing. He tried to say something to Benjamin and had to stop. Benjamin waited with the cup steady between both hands.

Thane went out and came back with another lamp. Zara cleared the far end of the table. Tavia took the frightened messenger upstairs; he had woken when they arrived and was asking whether Kes was coming.

Sariel held the bowl while Elena worked. Hest’s hand closed around her wrist, hard enough to hurt.

“You kept the door,” Hest said.

Sariel looked at her face. She had been waiting, without knowing it, for Hest to tell her what she should have done differently.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

Elena needed the wrist free. Hest let go. Sariel moved the bowl nearer and stayed until the water was gray and Elena asked for fresh.

Later, in the front room, she found Kestra examining the books they had taken. There were receipts, a list of payments to people Sariel did not know, and a thin volume of appointments. None was bound in bronze. None mentioned Kes on the page Kestra had open.

Sariel sat on the floor beside the table. Her shoes had blackened at the toes. A piece of burnt wedge remained caught in one sole. She picked at it with her fingernail and could not get it out.

“I thought the door would let you through,” Kestra said.

“It did.”

“I didn’t know what it would do afterward.”

Sariel looked up. Kestra was staring at the page as though its writing had become too small to read.

“You told us.”

Kestra put a hand over her mouth. Sariel sat with her until she took it away.

From the kitchen came Elena’s voice, asking Orren to try another small breath. The house smelled of smoke. Sariel had thought, on the canal, that coming home would put that smell behind them.

Chapter 5 What Kes can still do

By morning the first fence had been arrested.

Sariel learned it from his wife, who arrived at the kitchen door carrying a child too young to understand why he had been dressed before daylight. The woman had put her own dress on inside out. She kept pulling at the seam beneath her arm as she described the men who had come.

“There was a warrant.”

Orren sat upright on the bench because lying down made him cough. He asked who had signed it. The woman did not know. She had tried to read it, but one of the men had folded it before she could finish.

“He said it wasn’t for me.”

Sariel brought her a stool. The woman sat without letting go of the child. Hest listened from the kitchen table, where Elena was changing the dressing on her shoulder. The skin beneath was red and blistered. Sariel watched Hest’s face rather than look at the burn.

“Which court?” Hest asked.

“They wouldn’t say.”

“Did anyone you know see them?”

“The man across the lane. But he shut his shop.”

Zara went to find someone who would take an inquiry to the courts. Tavia wrote the fence’s full name, his trade, and the hour he had been taken. Each question slowed the woman’s speech enough that she could answer. When they had finished, she asked whether she should go home.

Nobody wanted to tell her to stay. Nobody wanted to send her back.

“You can sit here until we’ve heard something,” Elena said.

The child looked at the bowls on the shelf. Sariel got one down. The cabinet lock worked. She found herself resenting that small success with an intensity which made her feel foolish.

Hest sent Sariel to warn a woman who kept spare clothes and tools for them in a room above a dye shop. She gave her a message for the woman and another for the shopkeeper, who was not supposed to know that there was anything upstairs except his lodger’s possessions.

“Don’t take your usual bridge.”

“I don’t have a usual bridge.”

“You have three. Take another.”

Sariel went by the ropewalk. It added time and gave her a long view behind her. A porter pushing a handcart followed her as far as the fish stairs, then turned away without looking at her. She let him turn before she continued. In the old guild she had been praised for caution until it made her late. Hest usually wanted to know what had made her late before she decided what to call it.

The dye shop was closed.

Its shutters were fastened from inside, but smoke came from the little upper window. At first Sariel thought the woman was cooking. Then the smoke thickened, and someone in the adjoining house shouted.

Sariel ran down the side passage. The door at its far end had been secured with a length of wire through the staple. She cut it with the heavier blade Hest made her carry, working the edge back and forth while the handle hurt her palm. The wire broke. She hit the door with her shoulder and went in.

The woman stood on the stair holding a bundle against her chest. Smoke rolled down from above her. She had stopped halfway because the steps below were burning along one edge.

“Come down,” Sariel said.

“My box—”

“Come down.”

The woman took one step. A burning strip of cloth fell from above and lay across the next. She stopped again.

Sariel pulled off her coat, wrapped it around her forearm, and swept the cloth aside. It stuck to the wool. She flung the coat through the doorway, reached up, and took the woman’s free hand. They came down together. At the bottom the woman stumbled, and Sariel had to put her shoulder beneath her arm to keep her moving.

Outside, neighbors were passing buckets from the canal. The shopkeeper stood in his nightshirt shouting about a barrel of something which must be moved away from the heat. No one was listening. Sariel took one end of the barrel. A man from the next house took the other. They rolled it into the lane and went back for a second.

The woman sat beside Sariel’s smoking coat. Her bundle had opened. It contained a child’s small shirt, a pair of shoes, and a wooden comb. The clothes were old, carefully mended, far too small for anyone Sariel had seen visiting her.

Sariel wanted to ask about the box. She did not.

“There’s a place you can go,” she said.

“They know that place.”

The woman looked at her with a clarity Sariel could not answer. Behind them, a portion of the upper floor fell. Someone cheered because the collapse had opened the fire to the buckets, and someone else swore at him.

Sariel took the woman to the seamstress’s new workplace instead. It was farther away. The seamstress came to the loft door with a needle between her lips, saw them, and took it out.

“Up here.”

She made room without asking whose fault it was. Sariel stayed long enough to explain what she could. Then she went back to the guildhouse with a burnt sleeve and no spare tools.

Hest was standing when she returned. Elena was telling her that she had no business standing.

“Sitting won’t make this stop,” Hest said.

“It will stop you tearing that dressing.”

Hest sat. She listened to Sariel’s account with her injured arm resting across her lap. When Sariel reached the wire on the door, Hest’s face changed. It was a small change, a tightening at one corner of the mouth.

“Did anyone see you go in?”

“Half the lane.”

“All right.”

Sariel waited to be told what she should have done differently.

“Good that you got her out,” Hest said.

“She didn’t want to come here.”

“Sensible woman.”

Zara had returned. The first advocate she approached was unavailable. The second said the matter did not fall within his practice. The third asked for a fee so large that she could only stare at him. He had not looked embarrassed.

The fence’s wife listened. She took her child from Elena, stood, and said she knew someone else she could ask. Orren offered to go with her. She shook her head at once.

“You’d make it worse.”

After she left, Orren sat back down. Benjamin was looking at him as though the refusal had been a blow he should have prevented.

“She’s right,” Orren said.

A message arrived in the early afternoon. The messenger did not come to the door. He stood on the far side of the canal until Finn noticed him, then left a folded scrap beneath a mooring stone and went away. Sariel fetched it.

The writing was Kes’s.

Hest read it with the paper laid flat, because lifting her hand too high hurt her. She read it again. Then she told Sariel to put on another coat.

“Where?”

“The Pillow.”

Orren started to get up. Hest stopped him with a look.

“You can’t climb the stair.”

“I can.”

“And everyone will hear you trying.”

He remained seated. His face became older while Sariel watched.

Hest told Elena where they were going. She did not ask permission to leave. Elena did not tell her that she was allowed. They looked at one another, then Elena fetched a clean strip of cloth and secured the injured arm against Hest’s body.

“If you have to run, don’t try to use that side.”

Hest nodded. “I know.”

“I mean it.”

“So do I.”

They entered beneath the laundry rooms, where the Pillow’s fine linen arrived in baskets and left in carts. A woman at a table looked at Hest’s bandage, then at Sariel’s shoes. She said they should not be there. Hest showed her the folded scrap.

The woman read it without touching it.

“The stair at the back. No noise.”

Sariel could hear music above them. Someone was practicing a song, stopping and beginning the same phrase. The air smelled of warm soap and rosewater. A servant came down carrying an empty jug and passed so close that Sariel had to turn sideways. The servant did not look at them.

The stair descended into older stone. The plaster ended after the first turning. Below that, the walls were damp and the air had a cold mineral smell. Hest held the rail with her good hand. Sariel went ahead so that she would not have to watch how much effort each step cost her.

Kes waited beside a shallow niche where someone had once kept a lamp.

He was wearing the good coat. One sleeve was creased across the elbow as though he had slept in it. He had no spectacles. He looked first at Hest’s bandage, then at Sariel.

“I hoped you wouldn’t bring her.”

“I needed someone who could get me home,” Hest said.

Kes put a hand against the wall. He was breathing quickly.

Sariel had imagined him tied to a chair. She had imagined bars, a locked room, a guard with a knife. Seeing him standing here made her angry before she understood why.

“Where were you?”

“Here.”

“All this time?”

“Yes.”

Hest asked whether there was somewhere he could sit. He shook his head. Perhaps there was not. Perhaps he knew how little time they had.

“My daughter came to work,” he said. “The work was real. Then her letters stopped. I came to see her. They let me see her.”

His voice had acquired the careful rhythm of someone trying not to leave out a fact which might change how he was judged.

“She was alive. She wasn’t hurt. She wasn’t allowed to leave. Celise made an offer.”

Hest closed her eyes briefly.

“The rite?”

He nodded.

Sariel knew of such things. She had heard boasts, threats, stories told by people who had never been in the room. The word offer had always seemed part of the ugliness. An offer could be declined. She looked at Kes and did not see someone who had been permitted to decline anything.

“I said no,” he told them. “Twice.”

He put his good hand against his chest.

“Then I said what she gave me to say.”

“What?” Sariel asked.

Hest opened her eyes. She did not stop her.

“That I would assist in bringing the Coin into the Court’s hands. My daughter would leave alive. Free of the house. No one following her. No later claim.”

“And the note?”

“They told me what it would say.”

Sariel could see the empty lead-lined safe. Hest’s coat burning at the frame. Orren bent over the gutter, unable to breathe.

“You knew.”

“I knew they meant you to go there.”

“You could have—”

She stopped because she did not know how to finish. Warned them. Sent another note. Refused. Each seemed so simple as a sentence. Kes stood with his hand pressed to his chest, waiting for her to say it.

Hest asked, “Are you still bound?”

“Yes.”

“Then why can you tell us this?”

“They required my assistance. They did not tell me I had to stop caring what happened to you.”

Sariel hated the distinction. She could see Hest examining it as she would examine a difficult lock, searching for whatever small movement remained possible. It was useful. It had not kept the study door open.

Kes reached inside his coat and took out a folded paper. His fingers had trouble with the lining. Hest started to help him, then stopped when the motion pulled her shoulder. Sariel took the paper from him instead.

“The back safe,” he said. “In the warehouse. That opens it.”

“What’s in it?”

“Money. Introductions. The receipts for what we paid outright, in case someone tells you you owe it again. Enough for a road.”

“You should come,” Sariel said.

It emerged before she had decided whether she wanted it. Kes looked at her with such sudden tenderness that she wished she could take it back.

“Not yet.”

“When?”

He did not answer.

Hest took the paper from Sariel and read the numbers. She asked Kes where the patrols had been placed. He told her what he knew. She asked about the canal side. He said the upper landings were watched, but they had not yet sealed every water passage beneath the warehouses.

“Get the young ones out,” he said. “Don’t wait for an answer from anyone. They’re closing the roads.”

“We have others to move too.”

“Move who you can.”

Footsteps sounded above. Kes looked toward the stair.

Hest touched his cheek with her good hand. She was angry. Sariel could see it in the set of her jaw, in the way she did not draw him close. She touched him anyway.

“You old fool,” she said softly.

He gave a little nod, as though accepting a description he had already considered.

“Tell her,” Sariel said, and could not finish the sentence.

Kes waited. She did not know whether she meant to tell his daughter that they understood, that she was to blame for nothing, or that there would be so much to explain if they ever met again.

“Get out,” he said. “Please.”

They left him beside the empty niche.

On the way home Hest had to stop twice.

The first time she pretended to look at a shop. The second time she sat on a low wall and let Sariel stand in front of her so people would not stare at the bandage. Sariel wanted to run back to the Pillow and bring Kes with them. She wanted never to see him again. The two desires changed places so quickly that she could not settle on either long enough to be ashamed.

“Did he have a choice?” she asked.

Hest looked up from the paving.

“Before they had her? All sorts, probably. After? I wasn’t in that room.”

“He said he was bound.”

“I believe him.”

“Then why are you angry?”

Hest moved her injured arm and winced.

Sariel looked away.

“Help me up,” Hest said.

At the warehouse they found Zara and Tavia back from the river gate. A carrier who had agreed to take them across the city now claimed not to know them. Tavia’s papers had been examined and returned with the suggestion that she inquire at another office. Two men had followed them as far as the ropewalk.

Orren was at the window. He pointed without turning. On the far side of the canal, a man stood by the mooring stone where the note had been left. Another waited on the bridge. They had the stillness of people who had been paid to remain somewhere.

Hest told them about the lower passage.

Thane went at once to inspect it. Finn followed with a lamp. Benjamin went to the stairs, then stopped and looked at Orren. Orren nodded, and he went down after the others.

Elena asked what Kes had said.

Sariel began badly. She said he had agreed to betray them. Elena put a hand on the table and asked her to begin earlier. So Sariel told it again: the job, the stopped letters, the visit, the daughter who could not leave, the words Celise gave him, the command that still held him. It took longer. When she finished she felt less certain of what she was entitled to say about any of it.

“And his daughter?” Tavia asked.

“He says she’ll be let go.”

“Has she been?”

“Not yet.”

They sat without speaking until Thane called from below.

The old canal passage was partly blocked. There was a service hatch beneath a neighboring landing, but the water stood too high to reach it safely while carrying packs. The tide would fall toward evening. They could clear the interior now, wait for enough space, and go out below the level of the watched landings.

“How long?” Orren asked.

“A few hours.”

Hest looked at the men across the water. She handed Sariel the key to the workshop cupboard.

“Bring the good rope.”

Sariel went to get it. In the kitchen, the small onions had been cut into the pot. Someone had put a lid over the food to keep it warm.

Chapter 6 The canal

Kestra could lift the end of the table if she stood on a crate and put her shoulder beneath it. On the floor she could only pull, which hurt her fingers and accomplished very little.

“Wait,” Thane said.

He slid a length of timber under the near leg. Benjamin moved to the other end. At Thane’s nod, they lifted together, and the table rose until Kestra could take its weight against her shoulder. They turned it onto its side and pushed it into the front passage.

The repaired legs pointed at the door. Thane ran his hand over one joint.

“It’ll hold.”

“Then stop admiring it,” Orren said.

He was trying to move the long bench on his own. Benjamin went back to help. Kestra stepped down from the crate, flexing her fingers. She had copied at that table for months. The pale rectangle where her board had shielded the wood from dust was still visible, turned sideways toward the street.

Beyond the open doorway a man waited on the bridge. He had taken off his helmet and held it beneath his arm. There was nothing threatening in the way he stood. That seemed worse to Kestra than if he had shouted.
