Hest went out to speak to him. Orren remained just inside the door. Kestra could not hear what was said at first. Hest listened for a long time, her injured arm held against her ribs.

“And the others?” she asked at last.

The man said something about names being considered individually.

Hest laughed once. It was not a laugh Kestra had heard at supper.

“You’ll put it in writing.”

He looked toward the far end of the bridge. Another man stood there, wearing a sash over his armour. The first man went to him. Hest came back inside.

“How long?” Orren asked.

“As long as it takes them to write it.”

“Will it help?”

She glanced toward the cellar stair. “It might help them finish.”

Kestra understood then that Hest was not waiting for good terms. She was making them spend time producing bad ones. The knowledge did not make the man on the bridge look less reasonable.

Below, someone struck stone with a hammer. Thane swore. Water sloshed against something hollow.

Kestra went to help.

The passage had once been used to bring spices into the warehouse before a new landing was built above it. Kestra knew this because Kes had told her when she asked why the cellar smelled sweet even in the damp. He had known the name of the man who changed the landing and the amount he had overcharged. She could remember the overcharge more easily than the name.

The lower steps ended at water. Beyond them a narrow tunnel ran beneath the kitchen and turned toward the adjoining property. A grating had been set across the bend. Its lower bolts were under water, and one of the upper bolts had rusted into the stone.

Thane was working on it with a cold chisel. Finn held the lamp. Sariel crouched beside the water with the rope coiled over one arm.

“We could take the lamp lower,” Kestra said.

“Then you’ll drop it,” Thane answered without looking round.

“I could hang it.”

“From what?”

She looked above him. An old iron hook protruded from the wall, too high to reach from the lower step. She went back for the crate.

Once the lamp was hung, Finn had both hands free. He took the hammer when Thane’s arm began to tire. His first blow missed. The sound made everyone flinch. Thane repositioned the chisel and showed him where to stand. The next blow struck squarely. A scale of rust fell into the water.

“Again,” Thane said.

The word brought Orren and the wooden sword into Kestra’s mind with such force that she had to turn away. She looked at the water instead. The level had dropped below a pale line on the stone. Not far enough yet.

Sariel passed a rope round a bracket in the wall and leaned on it with her whole weight. The bracket held. She tied the line and passed the free end along the passage. Kestra went as far as she could on the steps to help feed it. The stones were slippery, but each had an edge she could find with her shoe.

At the bend, the grating shifted.

“There,” Finn said, delighted.

Thane told him to put down the hammer and take the other side before it fell on his foot. They lifted together. It came free with a long scrape and an astonishing weight of dirty water. Sariel caught it before it struck the stair. Kestra reached to help and felt one rough edge tear across her palm.

She drew her hand back. A thin red line opened on the skin.

Elena was beside her almost at once.

“Let me see.”

“It’s nothing.”

“Then it won’t take long.”

She washed it from a cup, bound it lightly, and told Kestra to use the other hand on the rope. The other palm was scratched too. Kestra did not mention it until Elena saw the blood and gave her a look.

They got the grating onto the steps. The opening beyond was black and narrow, with enough air beneath the roof for a person to pass if the water fell a little more.

“The outside hatch?” Sariel asked.

“You can reach it from the next shelf,” Thane said. “If it opens.”

No one answered that.

Upstairs, Hest sat with a sheet of paper beneath her good hand.

She was drawing something small. Kestra went past carrying a stack of bowls from the kitchen, then stopped because the bowls were absurd. There would be no room for them. She put them back on the shelf.

“Are you writing to someone?”

“Yes.”

“I can take it down for you.”

Hest shook her head. “This one’s mine.”

She bent over the paper again. The point of the pen made a little scratch. Kestra saw a shape like the inside of the cabinet lock, then looked away because it seemed private.

Orren had put a sword on the bench. He was winding a strip of leather around its hilt. Benjamin watched him.

“It shifts,” Orren said. “When you can, have it seen to properly. Don’t just keep adding leather.”

“Why are you telling me?”

Orren held it out.

Benjamin did not take it.

“I have a sword.”

“You have something with an edge which you’ve persuaded yourself is a sword. Take this.”

Benjamin took it. He knew its weight already. Kestra had seen Orren make him practice with it when the wooden one no longer taught what he needed to learn.

“What will you use?”

Orren nodded toward the boarding pike beside the stair. “At this distance? That.”

Benjamin looked at the narrow passage, the barricade, the place where a person would have to come through it. He understood the answer. He understood something else too, and Kestra watched him refusing to say it.

Zara came from the office with a purse, a sheaf of receipts, and an expression which had become very calm. Tavia carried the papers wrapped in oilcloth. Finn had the fox inside a sling made from a torn apron. The fox did not approve and was expressing its view with all four feet.

“Leave him loose,” Thane said.

“In the water?”

“He can swim.”

“So can you. I’ll put you in a sack.”

Thane laughed. It came out suddenly and made several people look up. Finn began laughing too, still trying to keep the fox’s head clear of the cloth. For a moment they were merely people with too much to carry and an uncooperative animal.

Kestra went to her sleeping place. She had packed the handbook, the scroll case, two changes of clothing, and every page she had written that season. The bag was too heavy. She sat on the bed and took out the shipping articles, the exercise copies, and the damaged book she had not finished reading. She could not decide which of her own notes mattered most. She put them all back, then took them out again.

Sariel appeared in the doorway.

“We have to carry it through water.”

“I know.”

“Let me feel.”

Kestra held the bag away from her. Sariel stood waiting.

After a while Kestra put it down. She kept the scroll case, a thin sheaf of notes, and one change of clothes. Sariel tried the weight and nodded. She did not look at the pages left on the bed, which Kestra appreciated more than any consolation she might have offered.

Downstairs something struck the outer door.

The man on the bridge had brought the terms. Orren had refused to move the barricade to receive them. Someone had pushed the folded sheet beneath the door.

Hest read it. She passed it to Tavia, who read the same paragraph twice and then set it down.

“They can detain anyone they decide has information.”

“Yes.”

“That could be all of us.”

“Yes.”

There was another blow against the door, more a demand for attention than an attempt to break it.

Hest called that the paper needed clarification. The answer came that no further clarification would be given. She asked whom they should hold responsible for the wording. A man outside laughed.

Orren turned to Benjamin. “Go down.”

“I’m staying here.”

“You’re taking the others through.”

“Sariel can do that.”

Sariel stood by the cellar door with Hest’s tool case beneath her coat. Kestra had seen Hest fold the letter and slip it inside the case.

“She’ll find the way,” Orren said. “You’ll get them along it.”

Benjamin shook his head.

Kestra wanted to stay too. The desire frightened her because it had so little to do with what she could accomplish. She wanted someone to remember that she had not run. She wanted the people who had been kind to her not to die in a room she was leaving. She had no working which could keep the door upright and no sword she could use against the men outside it.

“Benjamin,” Elena said.

He did not look at her.

The first real attack came against the lower hinge. The whole barricade moved. Thane caught one of the projecting table legs and pushed it back into place. Above them a crossbow sounded from the upper window. Someone in the street cried out.

Orren took Benjamin’s face between his hands.

He had done it too quickly and his burned arm shook. Benjamin reached to support it, then stopped.

“Listen to me,” Orren said. “They need your hands. I need you to go and use them.”

Benjamin’s mouth moved. He nodded once, sharply, and turned away.

Hest gave Sariel a brief embrace with her good arm. Sariel held her much more tightly than seemed wise. Hest endured it. When they separated, she touched the side of Sariel’s head with two fingers.

Kestra wanted to say something. Thank you was hopelessly small. Goodbye was impossible. Hest looked at her and said, “Take the lamp.”

Kestra took it.

The cellar filled with the sounds of the house above it. Every movement seemed louder beneath the floor: feet, furniture shifting, an impact that sent dust down between the boards. Thane went first to the bend. Sariel followed him, carrying the free end of the line. Kestra stood on the steps with the lamp while the others brought down their packs.

Water lapped at the second stair now. The tide was falling. It had begun to leave a narrow ledge along one side of the passage, slippery and uneven but wide enough for a foot.

Sariel tested it. She went around the bend and out of sight.

Kestra waited. She could hear Sariel’s breathing, then another sound: a wooden surface being pushed from beneath.

“Fastened,” Sariel called.

Thane went to help. Kestra raised the lamp. Its light reached the bend and no farther.

Above them, something broke. Benjamin looked toward the cellar stair. Elena caught his sleeve before he moved. He looked down at her hand, then back toward the passage.

“What do you need?” he called.

“Wait,” Sariel answered.

Tavia put the papers inside her coat. Zara checked the knot on the common purse. Finn spoke to the fox as though the animal had deliberately arranged all of this and might yet be persuaded to stop.

A bright fragment fell through the open cellar door. It struck the stairs, bounced, and landed against Tavia’s arm. She had put the arm out to steady Finn. The fragment caught in her sleeve.

She made a small surprised noise. Elena knocked it away and pulled the cloth clear. There was a red patch above the wrist which grew darker as Kestra watched. Tavia looked at it with bewilderment.

“Water,” Elena said.

They had a little clean water in a flask. Elena used it. Tavia protested that they might need it to drink. Elena continued until the flask was nearly empty, then put a clean covering over the burn and tied it loosely.

Benjamin went two steps up toward the kitchen. A splinter struck his left shoulder when something hit the door above him. He pulled it out, stared at it, and came back down. Blood ran into the collar of his shirt.

“Sit,” Elena said.

“There isn’t time.”

“Then stand still.”

She pressed cloth against the cut. Kestra held the lamp close enough for her to see.

From around the bend came the sound of a bolt moving.

Then Sariel said, very quietly, “Who are you?”

The person beyond the hatch wore a hood and had a lamp shaded with blue glass. Kestra could not see their face at first. They stood on a little service landing beneath the neighboring warehouse, half in the water, holding the hatch open.

“Mareth,” they said. “I knew your teachers. This way.”

Sariel did not move at once.

Mareth looked past her toward the light in Kestra’s hand, the smoke lowering over the stair, the people waiting to come through. Then they stepped aside and lifted the lamp so Sariel could see the ledge beyond them. It led beneath the adjoining landing and out toward a channel between warehouses. A rope had been fixed along the wall.

Sariel went through. She tested the rope before she put her weight on it.

“One at a time,” she called back.

Thane took Finn’s pack. Benjamin took the heavier bundle from Elena. Tavia held her injured arm against her body and used the other hand on the line. They moved with a slowness that made Kestra want to push them and a care she knew was necessary.

She went after Zara.

The ledge beneath the water felt sound until she reached the turn. There her foot found nothing. She put her weight down before she understood the absence. Her other foot slid. The lamp struck the wall, went out, and disappeared.

Cold water closed over her face.

For an instant she could see light above it, broken into pieces by the surface. She tried to stand. There was no floor close enough. Her bag caught against something behind her, pulling her sideways. She reached for the rope and touched stone instead.

She had never understood how quickly a person could use up a breath.

Something caught her coat. She pulled toward it and felt the bag hold her back. A voice came through the water, a sound with no words in it. Then she was at the surface and someone was saying, “Let the strap go.”

She could not find it.

Hands moved beneath her arm. The strap loosened. She felt the bag leave her, and then her chest struck the edge of the landing so hard that all the air she had been trying to keep came out at once.

Mareth pulled her higher. Benjamin reached over their shoulder and caught the back of her coat. Between them they dragged her onto the boards.

She coughed water onto the wood. It hurt her ribs. It hurt her throat. She tried to ask about the bag and could only cough again.

Finn crouched beside her with the scroll case.

“This floated.”

She took it with both scraped hands. The relief was so great that she began crying, which made it even harder to breathe.

“Up,” Mareth said, gently. “We have to go.”

She got her feet beneath her. Elena was there, supporting her on the uninjured side. The blue lamp moved ahead. Benjamin put one hand against the wall and followed them with the bundles.

Behind them, the guildhouse made a deep, tearing sound.

Kestra turned. She could see nothing of the stairwell, nothing of the front room, nothing of the people she had left there. The view back through the hatch contained only smoke above black water. She waited for another person to emerge.

Sariel came back and took her free hand.

“Come on.”

They counted at the far end of the channel.

Sariel, Benjamin, Elena, Thane, Tavia, Finn, Zara. Kestra counted herself last because she kept forgetting to include the person doing the counting. Eight. She tried again. Eight.

Mareth stood farther along the narrow lane, watching the opening onto the road. They had taken the blue glass off the lamp. In ordinary light their face looked tired and unremarkable, except for a faint silver brightness in one eye when they turned.

“West,” they said. “Keep off the main bridge. There’s a towpath beyond the tanner’s wall. It joins the road after the toll station.”

“Are you coming?” Elena asked.

“For a little way.”

They walked while the city went on around them. A woman at an upper window complained about the smoke. A boatman shouted for someone to move a barge. Bells began ringing, first nearby and then farther off, never together.

At the corner Kestra looked back. The light over the Dye Yards was high and unsteady. Sparks went above the roofs in a long bright stream. She thought she could hear Orren coughing, then understood that the sound was coming from her own chest.

They passed the last houses. Mareth took them through a gap in a wall and pointed out the towpath, then stayed at the gap while the eight went on. When Kestra looked again, the lamp was no longer visible.

Elena stopped them under a stand of trees to look at Benjamin’s shoulder and Tavia’s arm. She made Kestra take small sips from what remained in the flask. Thane opened his tool roll and checked it without saying anything. Zara had the purse. Finn held the fox against his chest, its single good ear flattened beneath his chin.

Sariel sat a little apart with the lockpick case open on her knees. Inside it was a folded sheet. She unfolded it, looked, and folded it again so carefully that Kestra knew she would remember the movement.

Nobody asked what it said.

When they began walking again, Benjamin went to the back. He did not tell the others he was taking that place. He simply waited until the last of them had passed and followed with Orren’s sword at his side.

Kestra kept the scroll case against her ribs. The pages inside were wet at the edges. She would have to open them somewhere dry, separate them carefully, and find out what could be saved.

Part Two The road to Millhaven

Chapter 7 What fits in the bag

By the second morning, Elena could tell who was awake by the things they were trying not to do.

Kestra held a cough in until it shook her. Benjamin reached for his coat without moving his left shoulder. Tavia lay on her unburned arm and looked at the wall of the cattle shelter as though the rough stones required all her attention. Elena herself had been keeping her eyes closed because opening them would begin the day.

Something damp touched her mouth.

“Finn.”

“What?”

“Your fox.”

“What about him?”

She opened her eyes. The one-eared animal had his front paws on the rolled coat beneath her head. His whiskers were wet. He looked directly into her face with an interest she would have preferred from something carrying breakfast.

Finn lifted him. Fox became wonderfully limp, as though nobody had ever asked him to do anything so difficult as be carried.

“He’s checking you’re alive.”

“Tell him to take your word for it.”

“He doesn’t.”

Elena sat up. Her neck objected. The shelter had a roof over two thirds of it, which they had considered a remarkable piece of good fortune the previous evening. Now light came through the missing third onto wet dung and a white feather. Beyond the low doorway, the hills held a thin morning mist. She could see neither the city nor the road that had brought them here.

For a few moments she could not remember where she had put her bag. Then she found it between her knees, where she had slept with it.

“I’ll look at the arm first,” she told Tavia.

“Good morning to you too.”

“Good morning. Let me look at your arm.”

Tavia smiled, but the smile went as soon as Elena began unwinding the bandage. The dressing had shifted during the night. A strip stuck to the raw patch above her right wrist.

“Don’t pull,” Tavia said quickly.

“I won’t.”

Elena reached for the little water flask, shook it, then looked at its open mouth. Dry. She remembered wetting Kestra’s lips in the night and giving Benjamin the last swallow because he had said his head hurt. She had not remembered to keep any for this.

“We need water.”

“There’s a run below the trees,” Finn said.

“Clean water. Heated.”

“I haven’t taught the trees that.”

He put down Fox and took the cooking pot. Elena wanted to tell him not to make jokes, although she had laughed at him a moment before. She rubbed her eyes instead.

Tavia kept her arm extended between them. “How long?”

“Not long.”

Elena heard how readily she said it. She began to open the medicine bag so she would not have to look at Tavia’s face.

The kit had seemed substantial at the Coin. It had contained enough small, familiar objects to make her feel that she was arriving at an injury with a household behind her: cloth, thread, two needles, wax, a thin spatula, wrapped salves, a packet of dried leaves, a little spirit, a knife. Laid on her lap, with the damp removed from the wrapping and the used things set aside, it looked like the contents of a drawer somebody had forgotten to empty.

She had enough clean linen for Benjamin’s shoulder and Tavia’s burn today. Kestra’s palms were less raw than they had been, but the left one had opened again when she caught herself on a stone. Tomorrow there would be the cloth they could wash today, if it dried, if nobody fell, if none of the wounds needed a second change.

She folded the smallest piece over her finger and then unfolded it. It was hardly bigger than the burn.

“I have a shirt,” Benjamin said.

“You’re wearing your shirt.”

“Another.”

He took a bundle from his pack. The shirt inside was gray, with a careful darn near the collar. Elena knew it. He had worn it on days when Orren asked him to look respectable rather than dangerous, a distinction Orren had enjoyed explaining in front of visitors.

“That isn’t clean,” she said.

“It can be.”

“Then you won’t have another.”

Benjamin held it out until she took it.

Zara came through the doorway carrying a flat stone. “There’s no fire yet. Finn’s found water and three reasons the first place was wrong. Give me the cloth.”

“I’ll do it.”

“With what hand? You’re holding Tavia.”

Elena looked down. She was still supporting the injured arm, although Tavia had shifted onto the bench and no longer needed her to.

“Keep the used pieces separate.”

“I can recognize the ones with blood on them.”

“And don’t put the clean ones on the ground.”

Zara set the stone on a ledge, spread a corner of her own skirt across it, and waited.

“All right,” Elena said. “Thank you.”

Outside, Thane and Finn began disagreeing about firewood. Sariel was gone. Elena could not recall when she had left, and the thought of finding out made her feel tired enough to lie down beside Tavia and leave them all to heal by themselves.

Tavia touched her knee with her sound hand.

“I’m not going anywhere,” she said.

“I know.”

“Then stop holding my sleeve as if I am.”

Elena let go. They waited for the water together.

Sariel returned while the strips of Benjamin’s shirt were drying over a branch. She had two turnips, a twist of salt, and a narrow loaf tucked beneath her coat.

Benjamin was on his feet before she reached the doorway.

“Where were you?”

“Out.”

“We noticed.”

“There are farms along the lower road. No riders. A woman with geese sold me this.”

She put the food beside the pot. Her hair was wet at the ends. There was mud nearly to her knees.

“You could have said,” Benjamin told her.

“You were asleep.”

“Somebody was awake.”

Sariel looked at Elena, who shook her head. She had only woken when the fox had climbed onto her.

“I wasn’t long.”

“We didn’t know that.”

“You know it now.”

Elena had been watching Tavia. The older woman was sitting with her newly dressed arm against her chest, eyes half shut. She had borne the washing without a sound, then cried suddenly when Zara tried to feed her a spoonful of thin porridge. Zara had set the spoon down and stayed beside her without asking what was wrong. Elena would have asked. She would have asked three different ways and tried to repair each answer.

“Sariel,” Elena said, “next time, tell me where you’re going.”

“So I need leave?”

“So I know when to start worrying.”

Sariel pulled the loaf from its wrapping. “The goose woman says the lower road joins the west road beyond the stone sheds. Fewer tolls than the main way.”

“How much fewer?” Zara asked.

“She didn’t give me a schedule.”

“Then we’re about to eat the only certain thing she sold us.”

Zara took the loaf and divided it. She gave Sariel an end piece with a thick knob of crust. Sariel accepted it and sat down, a little of the tightness leaving her shoulders.

Elena ate slowly. The bread had seeds in it. She picked one from her tooth and found herself thinking of the Coin’s kitchen floor, where seeds lodged between the tiles no matter how thoroughly somebody swept. She had spent whole mornings there wishing everyone would go away and let her work.

Benjamin began repacking before she had finished. He took Tavia’s bundle, then Kestra’s, then the pot, which still contained the morning’s wet ashes because Thane had used it to carry the fire away from the shelter. When he stood, the pot struck his knee. He caught it, shifted the straps, and found that the sword at his hip now pointed into the earth behind him.

Finn watched with his mouth open.

“Don’t,” Benjamin said.

“I haven’t.”

“You’re going to.”

“I was wondering where we fit the wheels.”

Thane bent to inspect the arrangements. “Through his ankles. The shoulder would never bear an axle.”

“I can carry it.”

“You can stand in it,” Zara said. “There’s a distinction.”

Benjamin attempted to step over the threshold. The pot caught the stone, his right bundle swung forward, and he had to take three small, furious steps to prevent himself sitting in the hearth. Finn made a noise like a goose trying to cough politely. Tavia laughed first, then held her arm against herself and laughed harder.

Benjamin looked back at them. For an instant Elena thought he would be angry. Instead his face changed, and he began laughing too.

“Help, then,” he said.

They took him apart. Thane rescued the pot. Finn shifted the food into his own bag. Zara gave Kestra back her scroll case and took the spare clothing. Elena let Benjamin carry the unused linen but kept the things she might need quickly. Tavia protested until Sariel showed her that she could carry the bread against her left hip without rubbing the burn.

Benjamin ended with his own pack and one extra blanket. He rolled his shoulder to test it, then stopped.

Elena saw.

“Let me look.”

“It’s the strap.”

“Then let me look at the strap.”

He unfastened the pack with an exaggerated patience that made her want to slap his sound shoulder. Beneath the shirt, the edge of the dressing had reddened. The cut itself was shallow, but it ran exactly where a load would work against it. She had known that when she dressed it. She had told herself he would favor the other side.

She made a pad from a folded scrap and tried the pack again. He raised his arm and lowered it.

“Better?”

“Yes.”

“Actually?”

“Actually.”

They walked out into the mist with the last damp strips of shirt tied to Thane’s pack. They waved behind him like little flags. Finn said they could announce themselves as a company of laundresses, and Zara said laundresses would have more sense than to travel with one shirt between them. Elena laughed until she had to stop to get her breath.

That day they learned how far a hill could be from the foot of a hill.

Elena had agreed to the stone sheds because Sariel said they were near. Sariel meant near to someone walking alone, unburdened, who did not stop to look at every footfall. By midday Kestra’s cough had returned. She tried to disguise it by asking questions about the road. When nobody answered, she asked the questions of herself.

“Those blocks weren’t laid at the same time. You can see the difference in the cutting.”

“Can I?” Thane said.

“Under the moss.”

“That would be why I can’t.”

“The old surface must continue beneath the bank. There was probably a wider approach.”

Elena listened to the spaces between the sentences. Kestra was leaving herself room to breathe without appearing to. She had done the same in the shelter when Elena pressed gently along her bruised ribs.

“We should stop.”

“At the sheds,” Benjamin said.

“Here.”

There was no useful shade where they stood. The mist had burned off, and the old paving held heat. Finn went ahead to a patch of thorn. Sariel came back from a bend and said the sheds were closer than they had been. Nobody found it funny except Sariel, and even she seemed sorry afterward.

Kestra sat with one leg stretched in front of her. Elena asked her to take off her left shoe.

“It’s my ribs.”

“You’re stepping on the outside of that foot.”

“Everyone has a way of walking.”

“Show me yours.”

The heel of the stocking had stuck. Elena eased it away and found a blister torn open, with grit pressed into the raw skin. Nothing terrible. Nothing that would kill Kestra if they stopped and tended it. Another thing that would become worse under a day’s walking.

“Since when?”

“This morning.”

“Why didn’t you say?”

Kestra stared at her. “Which part should I have said first?”

Elena put the stocking down. Around them the others had fallen quiet. She wanted to explain that she needed information, that being told would help, that she had asked because she cared. Kestra’s scraped hands were resting palm upward on her knees. Elena took one of them, avoiding the raw places, and held it for a moment.

“The heel is worse than it needed to be,” she said. “We’ll clean it.”

“Will I be all right tomorrow?”

“You’ll still have a sore heel tomorrow.”

Kestra blinked. “That wasn’t an encouraging answer.”

“I’m trying another kind.”

Zara brought water and crouched beside them. She began talking about a man she had once seen purchase six identical blue hats because he believed the price would rise. Kestra objected to the economics. Zara defended the man with increasing absurdity while Elena worked. When the grit came free, Kestra’s fingers tightened on her wrist, but she kept arguing about the hats.

Later, Elena asked Zara where she had learned to do that.

“At a stall. People stop watching your hands if you give them something to disagree with.”

“I meant sitting with someone.”

Zara shrugged. “People get ill at stalls too.”

She took the dirty cloth before Elena could reach for it. Elena watched her carry it away, ashamed of how readily she had expected cleverness to be useless at this particular work.

They reached the sheds with enough light to see the holes in the roof. One was occupied by a carter, his wife, and their donkey. The wife offered them space in the other in exchange for half the turnips. Zara negotiated it down to one turnip and help watering the animal. Finn said the donkey should have asked for both, as they would be terrible at sleeping outdoors.

Elena was still smiling when she sat down, and then she could not make herself stand again.

Thane knelt beside her and slid the medicine bag off her shoulder. Its strap had left a deep line in her skin.

“Where do you want this?”

“By my hand.”

He put it there. He brought her a cup before she remembered to ask for one.

They stayed another day at the sheds.

It took most of the evening to agree to it. Benjamin wanted the distance. Sariel wanted to know who else used the road. Zara disliked paying for shelter when they had already paid for the previous night, as if the morning should have been included. Tavia said she could walk. Kestra said she could also walk, but nobody had offered to put that statement to a useful test.

Elena sat with her back against a sack of chaff and listened until they began repeating themselves.

“I can keep the burn covered,” she said. “I can clean Benjamin’s shoulder. I can’t make either of them finish healing before you need them to. Kestra’s heel needs less walking than today.”

“How much less?” Sariel asked.

“I don’t know.”

It was easier to say once she had begun. She told them what she could see and what she wanted to see in the morning. She was not certain the cough would clear. She did not like the way Tavia’s dressing had rubbed. They needed dry cloth, sleep, and food that was not being eaten while standing up because somebody thought he had heard a horse.

Benjamin looked toward the road. “I did hear one.”

“So did the donkey,” Finn said. “He didn’t enlist.”

They settled on one day. Sariel would watch the road and ask about the western turn. The carter’s wife had a wash pot they could use if they fetched wood. Thane could mend the loose handle on her bucket. Tavia could write a receipt the carter needed. Zara would discover whether any of these services could be made to pay for their supper before they offered them free.

For the first time since the canal, they chose who would stay awake and who was allowed to sleep. Elena tried to put herself into the first watch. Tavia crossed out her name with a burned stick.

“You were awake half last night.”

“So were you.”

“Then neither of us.”

Sariel pointed to the hedge. “If I go along there, I come back here. If I’m not back before the sky changes, wake Finn.”

Benjamin began to say something and stopped. Finn nodded.

In the morning the carter’s wife lent Elena a clean apron while her own dried. It had a pocket deep enough to lose a hand in. Elena stood in the sunshine, putting small things into it and discovering that they stayed where she had put them.

Zara found her there and held out half a cooked apple.

“The bucket,” she explained. “He’s very pleased with his bucket.”

They ate beside the washing. Kestra was asleep on a blanket in the shade. Tavia sat near her, reading the carter’s receipt aloud while he corrected the spelling of his own name for the third time. At the water trough, Benjamin was trying to persuade the donkey to move one foot.

Elena licked apple from her thumb. She could smell the linen drying. When Zara offered to fetch the other half, she said yes.

Chapter 8 The same bridge

Kestra recognized the milestone by the rabbit.

They had been nine days out of Silkstrand. She knew because she had kept the count on the back of a sheet rescued from the scroll case, beside the names of the farms where they had worked for meals. The marks were straight for the first three days and increasingly cramped after that. She had thought the remaining space would be sufficient.

Somebody had scratched the rabbit into the stone below the old lettering: two long ears, a round body, a pair of absurdly large back feet. One ear followed a natural crack. The first time they passed, she had thought it rather good. The second time, she stopped so abruptly that Thane walked into her.

“Sorry,” he said. “I was watching the road.”

“We’ve been here.”

“There are a great many stones.”

“With that rabbit?”

He looked. She waited for him to produce a sensible objection. The day had been going well enough to deserve one. Her heel had new skin across it, the cough no longer hurt every time, and a farm woman had sold them cheese wrapped in leaves. They had eaten it sitting on a wall, with no immediate need to move. Finn had found a feather that he said belonged to an owl and Sariel said belonged to a hen. The argument had lasted half a mile.

Thane bent over the milestone. A drop of water fell from his hair onto the rabbit’s head.

“Same scratch,” he said.

Behind them, the bridge crossed a shallow river between low banks. They had approached it from the east, passed between square pillars, and gone over the worn crown. On the far side the road curved through hazels. Beyond the hazels, it had brought them here.

Kestra turned slowly. The bridge lay ahead now, exactly where it had lain before. She could see the pale scar on the nearest pillar. She had touched it while trying to read the name beneath.

“No,” she said.

Benjamin had already walked back to the bend. He stood there with his hands on his hips, staring into the hazels as though somebody had moved a substantial object while he was not looking.

Sariel put down her pack.

“Stay here.”

“Where are you going?” Elena asked.

“Across. Then I’ll come back.”

“We just did that.”

“Exactly.”

Kestra wanted to go with her. Instead she knelt at the milestone. There were letters above the rabbit, half erased beneath three successive claims to ownership. The uppermost inscription named someone who had styled himself Protector of the Western Approaches. Another hand had cut across his name with a crude line. Beneath both, an older inscription followed the stone’s curved shoulder.

She cleared it with the corner of her sleeve. The words were familiar forms in an unfamiliar arrangement. She had read examples at the Academy, in a room with tall windows and a tutor who would never have permitted a rabbit in his copy.

Thane squatted beside her. “What does it say?”

“It says somebody did not believe the Protector was protecting them.”

“Useful.”

“The older part might be.”

She took out the little notebook she had made from the recovered sheets, stitching them into a folded wrapper with thread Elena had spared. Her palms were stiff where the scrapes had closed, and the damp made the paper curl. She flattened it against the case and copied the first line.

Sariel returned before she finished the second.

She came out of the hazels behind them. She was walking quickly. She stopped when she saw their faces, then looked over her shoulder.

“I didn’t turn,” she said.

Nobody answered.

“There isn’t a turning. You can see the way through.”

Finn caught Fox before he followed a scent into the undergrowth. The animal wriggled in his arms, offended by a restraint he could not attribute to anything interesting.

“We could go back,” Tavia said.

Sariel laughed once. It was an unpleasant sound.

“We have.”

They spent the next hour finding out what did not help.

Benjamin walked to the middle of the bridge and called to them. His voice came plainly across the water. He could see them. They could see him. When he came back by the same half of the road, he arrived at the near end as he should.

Thane tied a strip of cloth to the pillar and watched from the riverbank. The cloth did not move except in the wind. Sariel went over again, this time keeping one hand against the parapet until it ended. For a few moments Kestra could see her beyond the western pillar. Then she passed behind the hazels and returned from the east.

“No more,” Elena said when Benjamin began unfastening his pack.

“I thought if we went together—”

“We did.”

Kestra had found a word she believed meant landing. The next might mean winter, or lower, depending on whether the cut beside it belonged to the letter or the damage. She told them this. Nobody appeared improved by the information.

Rain began in little spits. The river showed each drop as a small dark hollow before smoothing over it. Upstream, the bank rose into reeds. Downstream, stones broke the current, but the channel beyond them was deeper than it looked. Finn had tested it with a branch and pulled the branch out dripping above his shoulder.

“We might find another crossing,” he said.

“How far?” Zara asked.

He spread his hands. She went back to dividing the remains of the bread.

Kestra kept copying. If she could recover the whole inscription, there might be an instruction hidden beneath the claims and complaints. Some old road works had been maintained by rites. She knew that. She knew several of the signs used to identify them. None appeared here, but the absence of a sign did not prove the absence of a working.

She scraped moss from the lower edge with a thin piece of wood.

“Kestra,” Elena said.

“A moment.”

The last two letters had been worn nearly away. She put her face close to the stone and changed the angle of her head. A footbridge, perhaps. Or a landing used when the main span was being repaired. The word before it was a direction, certainly a direction, if she could identify the abbreviated ending.

“Kestra.”

“I have almost—”

“Your hand.”

Blood had spotted the paper. The edge of her left palm had opened against the rough stone. She stared at it with more anger than the injury deserved.

Elena took the scrap of wood away.

“Sit on your case. I’ll hold the page.”

“I can hold it.”

“Then you can tell me what it says while I do your hand.”

Kestra sat. Above the hazels, the sky had darkened. She had expected to finish the inscription and find that the road was still waiting in the same afternoon. Instead the air had cooled, and the shadows had joined beneath the trees.

A cart rattled on the eastern road.

They all turned. The driver saw them gathered about the milestone and reined in with an expression of weary recognition.

“First time?” he asked.

He was broad through the shoulders, with a reddish beard trimmed short enough to show the pale skin beneath. His cart held baskets, two empty sacks, and a covered bundle tied down more carefully than the rest. A patient brown mare stood between the shafts.

Benjamin moved a little in front of the others.

The driver looked at the sword, then at the rabbit.

“That won’t persuade it,” he said.

“We weren’t trying to persuade it.”

“Good. Saves you an argument.”

His name was Roven. He carried between several settlements west of the river and the larger farms to the east. He had crossed that morning without difficulty and had hoped to cross again before the mist thickened. He looked at the bridge, sighed, and climbed down.

“Can you get over?” Tavia asked.

“Not there, apparently.”

He took a small pinch of salt from a cloth in his pocket and put it into the hollow at the base of the milestone. Kestra watched closely.

“What does that do?”

“Keeps me from being the man who didn’t.”

“Does it change the crossing?”

“Not that I’ve noticed.”

He went to inspect the river. Kestra got up and followed before Elena could object.

“There’s an older landing,” she said. “The inscription mentions one. Below the bridge, I think.”

Roven stopped. “Can you read that?”

“Some of it.”

“My grandfather said they brought stone across below. Nobody brings stone now. Look there.”

He pointed to a line of dark humps under the water, downstream from the central pier. Kestra had taken them for fallen masonry. Now she saw that the line continued toward a notch in the opposite bank.

“That’s not safe for your cart.”

“No. I can wait. I have dry oats and no reason to explain myself to you before supper.”

She liked him better for saying it. She would have liked him still more if he had pointed out the stones an hour earlier.

Roven knew where the lower path began. Kestra’s copy told them where it had once ended.

The notch on the opposite bank was misleading. It opened into a soft shelf where the river had deposited silt. The old landing lay a little farther north, hidden under willow roots. Kestra found the mark in the inscription at last: not a distance, as she had thought, but the name of the upstream bank. Roven knew the name in a shorter form. Together they argued themselves into agreement while the others ate.

“I could have used the hour,” he said when she showed him her first reading.

“So could I.”

He grinned. “Come on, then.”

Sariel took off her boots. Finn went a little way ahead of her along the bank, testing the first stones with the branch. Thane found a sound willow and tied their rope around it, then measured out enough to reach the first shallow shelf. He put a knot at the point where the rope should meet the far edge.

“If it comes back longer,” he said, “I want somebody to tell me.”

“It won’t,” Kestra said, before remembering what she had already seen that day.

He glanced at her. She closed her mouth.

The plan was small enough that she could keep all of it in mind. Sariel would cross without a pack, following the stones Finn could identify from this bank. Thane would hold the rope clear of the water. At the first shelf, she would stop, fasten the rope where they could see it, and check whether she could reach the root-bound landing. If she could not, she would return while there was still light.

Benjamin wanted to go beside her. The stones were too narrow for two. He stood with his pack at his feet, watching each place where she set a foot.

The first stretch was easier than it looked. Sariel bent her knees and moved with a slow, deliberate confidence that Kestra had never seen her use in a room. At the fourth stone she stopped. She tested the next one, drew her foot back, and pointed.

“Loose.”

Finn pushed the branch farther out. Water took the end and pulled. He braced it beneath his arm and found a larger stone half a pace to the side.

“There.”

Sariel looked, nodded, and stepped.

When she reached the shelf, Thane’s knot lay where it should. Kestra let out a breath she had been saving without noticing.

Sariel tied the rope around a projecting root. Then she disappeared beneath the willows.

They waited.

Kestra could hear the cart horse chewing. Roven had taken a handful of oats to her and was standing with one hand against her neck. He looked toward the river, but he did not call encouragement. Kestra was grateful. She was afraid of what a voice might interrupt.

Sariel appeared on the far bank, north of the notch, and raised both arms.

“There is a field,” she called. “And a wall. And the road goes west.”

“Does it stay there?” Thane called back.

“Come and find out.”

Elena made them sit before they began. Tavia’s bandage had loosened; Kestra needed a fresh wrap over the palm. Benjamin and Thane divided Kestra’s heavier things, leaving her the scroll case. She tried to refuse until Thane opened his mouth. She could see a joke coming and chose to spare herself.

Finn carried Fox inside his coat. Fox disliked the water but liked the smell of Roven’s food bundle enough to keep trying to climb toward it. When he finally settled, his tail protruded below Finn’s belt.

Kestra crossed after Elena. The rope felt rough in her right hand. At the loose stone she looked down and saw water moving where her foot ought to go. For a moment the difference between knowing a thing and doing it became so large she could not step.

“Left,” Elena said from ahead.

“I know.”

“Then look at my foot.”

Kestra looked. Elena’s boot rested on a broad dark surface. She could reach the same place. She did, and the stone held.

On the shelf she began to laugh. It escaped before she could prevent it, a high breathless noise that made Elena smile.

“Wait until we’re up,” Elena said.

But Kestra could not quite stop. She was across the part she had feared, and ahead of her Sariel had cut a narrow place through the willow growth. She climbed, scraping one knee, and found herself standing in a wet field full of ordinary sheep.

The sheep watched without admiration. She admired them anyway.

Roven stayed with the cart. He would shelter under its cover and try the bridge again in the morning. They offered to help him unload and carry the goods over. He said the mare could not follow a rope through willow roots, and she was worth more to him than an evening saved.

“Millhaven,” he called when they were all on the far side. “If you come that way, ask for me. I may even be home.”

Zara shouted back that he should check the food bundle before blaming the river for anything missing. Finn examined the inside of his coat. Fox had acquired a piece of dried meat.

“How?” Finn demanded.

The animal chewed.

They made camp beside a low wall, too far from the river to hear it. The lost afternoon meant no purchased supper and no roof. Kestra’s feet were wet again. She set her shoes where the fire could warm them without hardening the leather and ate porridge with the feeling that it was an earned and excellent meal.

Thane drew the crossing in the dirt. He made Sariel show him the position of the willow roots, then rubbed out the first version and drew them again. Benjamin asked how they would cross if someone could not walk. Kestra and Elena joined them over the little plan. For a few minutes they moved twigs and argued about the rope. Elena laid a finger across the shallow shelf.

“You’d need to stop there. You couldn’t carry someone straight through.”

“Two people could,” Benjamin said.

“Two people couldn’t fit,” Sariel reminded him.

He studied it, then moved a twig.

Kestra went for her notebook. There was still enough light to write beside the fire. She put the bridge account on one page, including the mistaken reading. On the next she wrote Hest’s name.

Her pen stopped.

“What are you doing?” Tavia asked.

“Making an account.”

Tavia settled beside her. For a while she said nothing.

“Of the house,” Kestra added.

“I thought so.”

Kestra wrote that Hest had given Sariel the case before the last preparations. Sariel looked over from the far side of the fire.

“After,” she said.

“She had it before we went down.”

“Yes. After the preparations. She was still using the tools.”

Kestra crossed out the words. Benjamin said Orren had spoken to them in the upper room. Elena said the lower. They argued for several breaths before realizing he had spoken in both.

Kestra had thought gathering their memories would make one room visible. Instead doors moved. A voice she remembered on her left had been on somebody else’s right. Finn had seen a face at the passage that she had never seen. Tavia recalled a joke; nobody remembered who had made it.

“Was it funny?” Zara asked.

“At the time.”

“Then it was probably mine.”

They laughed, uncertainly at first. Kestra put the joke down as Tavia remembered it and left a space for the speaker.

When she reached the end of the passage, her hand tightened on the pen. She could still feel water over her face. Mareth’s grip. The helpless fury of being pulled away when she wanted to turn back.

She wrote that the house had burned. Then she set the pen across the book.

Benjamin reached over and put another piece of wood on the fire. The light grew, catching the edge of Orren’s sword where it lay beside his blanket.

“I remember what he had for breakfast,” he said.

Nobody asked what use that was.

Kestra picked up her pen again.

Chapter 9 Finn stays

At Alderfold, Fox stole a glove and was offered employment before any of the people were.

They had entered their third week on the road. Sariel had stopped asking how far the next town was unless the person answering had actually been there. Two days of rain had kept them under a cartwright’s lean-to, and another had gone into helping him move timber in payment. The road beyond his yard had seemed no shorter for being free to use.

Fox had taken the glove from a stump where a woman had left it while cutting hazel rods. Finn called after him, then apologized to the woman, then went into the bushes to recover it. The woman watched the shaking leaves.

“If he can do that with rats, I’ll feed him.”

“He has strong opinions about rats,” Finn said from inside the hedge.

“So have I.”

Sariel stood on the road with the others and counted the visible doors. Six cottages close together, another beyond the drying racks, a long low building with animals in the shaded end. Behind it the forest rose toward a bare shoulder of hill. There was no enclosing wall. A child sat in the road scraping mud from a wheel with a spoon.

The woman picked up another rod. She was square and gray-haired, with a scar that pulled one eyebrow higher than the other. Her remaining glove had a hole at the thumb.

“What are you looking for?” she asked.

Tavia began to answer, but the woman pointed at Sariel.

“Her.”

“Somewhere to sleep.”

“Then look at the roofs.”

Finn emerged with the glove. It had acquired a wet fringe at the wrist. He offered it with both hands. The woman inspected the damage, looked at him, and sighed as though somebody had confirmed an old suspicion.

“Dena,” she said. “You’ve come at the wrong time for idle visitors.”

“We can work,” Tavia told her.

“Most people can. Whether they do is what costs the supper.”

Dena had a fence to mend, a cartload of split wood to stack, and a mule that had decided the shaded end of the long building belonged to it exclusively. Tavia asked about a place for eight. Dena considered their packs, Benjamin’s sword, and the way Kestra kept her left foot a little forward when standing.

“Three nights in the hay shed. Food while you work. The fence before the wood. Don’t go into the western coppice unless someone shows you where the pits are.”

“Whose food?” Zara asked.

“Mine.”

“And whose fence?”

“Also mine. You’re welcome to meet it before deciding.”

They met the fence. A small pig was already passing through it, with the unhurried manner of an animal attending to a longstanding arrangement. Finn stepped into its path and held out his arms. The pig considered him, turned, and went through a different hole.

Benjamin laughed. Finn gave him a patient look.

“You can guard that one.”

Within half an hour Benjamin was stationed beside the second hole, Tavia watched the lane, and Finn had persuaded the pig into a small enclosure behind the house. Sariel helped Thane cut new stakes. Kestra carried the lighter rods and asked Dena about charcoal pits, though not from any desire to fall into one. Elena found a pump with clear water and began negotiating for the use of a wash pot.

There was so much ordinary work that Sariel nearly forgot to watch the road.

On their second afternoon, Finn caught the mule without anyone being bitten.

Sariel had gone to the long building because she could no longer endure Benjamin’s efforts to sharpen Orren’s sword with a stone not suited to the task. Thane was trying to explain the problem. Benjamin agreed with everything he said and continued rubbing. Sariel left before she was asked to take a side.

The mule stood with its hindquarters toward the doorway. One ear turned at the sound of her steps. Finn was several paces away, sitting on an upturned bucket with a halter across his knees.

“Is this the work?” she asked.

“Part of it.”

“How do they know you’re doing it?”

“The mule tells them.”

She leaned against the doorpost. Finn watched the animal. He had been given an old hat to keep the insects from his face, and its brim had collapsed on one side. Sariel had expected him to look ridiculous. Instead he looked as though he had been here before they arrived.

“He knows how to be caught,” Finn said. “He just doesn’t want to.”

“Sensible.”

“Dena pulls the rope straight when she’s tired. He’s learned how to put his weight against it.”

“And you don’t?”

“I haven’t been tired here as long.”

He rose slowly and went toward the animal at an angle. Sariel expected him to offer food. He did not. He spoke in the same mild voice he used to complain about Fox, touched the mule high on its neck, and waited. The mule moved its head. Finn let it. When he lifted the halter, it stood.

Sariel was disappointed by how little there was to watch. Then Finn led the animal into the yard, and Dena, coming from the woodpile, stopped with both hands on her hips.

“You little bastard,” she told the mule affectionately.

“He’s not little,” Finn said.

“I’ve known him longer.”

She asked Finn to look at a place where the harness had rubbed. He knelt and examined it, then fetched Elena. Together they shifted the offending strap and padded it. Dena listened, squinting into the light, and made Finn show her how he had put the halter on.

Sariel went back to the hay shed. Benjamin had stopped sharpening. Thane was binding a strip around the loose hilt, his expression grave with concentration.

“This is temporary,” he said.

“You’ve said.”

“I know. You keep looking pleased.”

Benjamin smiled at him. “It no longer tries to turn in my hand.”

Sariel sat in the doorway and took out Hest’s case. The leather had dried badly after the canal. One fold had gone stiff. She worked oil into it with her thumb, careful around the place where the letter lay.

Hest would have liked Thane’s annoyance. She would have found something inadequate in the binding, then improved it without being asked. Sariel imagined telling her about the mule and receiving a lengthy account of why a lock was preferable to an animal. She kept rubbing after the leather could take no more oil.

“What?” Benjamin asked.

“Nothing.”

He did not press. That was one of the things she liked about him, although she would not have chosen the doorway of a hay shed to tell him so.

Finn came to supper late. Dena had asked him to help move two goats before the light went. He sat beside Sariel with hay in his hair and a scrape of dirt across his cheek. Fox immediately climbed onto his lap, putting muddy paws against the borrowed shirt Dena had given him while his own dried.

“He’s been asleep all afternoon,” Finn told them.

“Keeping the rats nervous,” Zara said.

“Has he caught any?” Tavia asked.

“No. But they must wonder.”

They ate bean stew with thick slices of bread. Dena had put a bowl of onions in vinegar beside the pot. Finn took one, bit into it, and shut his eyes with pleasure. Sariel watched him take another before the first was swallowed.

“You like them?” Dena asked.

He nodded, unable to answer.

“Good. Nobody else will eat them.”

Benjamin reached for the bowl. A moment later he was coughing so hard that Elena had to take the bread out of his hand. Finn laughed with both hands over his mouth. Dena poured more water and looked deeply contented.

The offer came on the third morning.

Sariel heard it while carrying the last of the firewood around the back of the house. Dena stood by the fence with Finn, showing him the line of the western trees. She was talking about work for autumn: bringing the animals closer, checking the coppice boundaries, cutting poles while the ground still allowed a cart. There would be meals and a bed in the small room above the stores. A little money each week. More if the winter wood sold well, but she would not put that part into a promise.

“I’d teach you the woodland work properly,” she said. “You know enough to make a good beginning and enough to get yourself in trouble.”

Finn looked at the trees. “I worked under a forester once.”

“So you told me.”

“I wasn’t very old.”

“You aren’t very old now.”

He smiled. Then he saw Sariel.

She carried the wood inside without speaking.

By midday everyone knew. Benjamin took it worst. He did not shout. He began finding practical objections, one after another, so quickly that Finn could not answer before the next arrived.

There were only eight of them. They did not know the western road. Dena could change her mind. Winter work was different from summer. A person could be trapped by owing for a bed. Fox would kill the hens. If something happened, Finn would have nobody.

“She has no hens,” Finn said.

“That’s the one you answer?”

“It’s the one I can finish before you begin again.”

They were sitting in the hay shed. Elena had gone still beside the wash basket. Tavia looked at the floor. Zara was turning a coin between her fingers, over and over.

Sariel said, “Have you seen the room?”

Finn looked at her. “Yes.”

“Does it lock?”

“Yes.”

“From inside?”

“It’s a room, Sariel.”

“That doesn’t answer.”

“It locks from inside. The window opens. The stairs are ordinary stairs. There’s a bed and a cupboard with no back.”

She disliked his tone. She disliked still more that it made her want to find something wrong with the room.

Benjamin rubbed both hands over his face.

“We said we’d stay together.”

“When?” Finn asked.

“After the canal.”

“We said we’d keep going.”

“You know what I mean.”

Finn looked toward the doorway. Fox lay in a patch of sun with all four paws extended. Nothing about the conversation disturbed him.

“I don’t want to keep going,” Finn said.

Benjamin opened his mouth, then closed it.

“I like it here,” Finn continued. “I like knowing what to do when I get up. I like the animals. I even like the fence. It stays where I mend it.”

“For now,” Benjamin said.

Finn’s face tightened. “Yes. For now. I haven’t found a place that can give me every year before I start.”

Nobody had an answer ready. Sariel looked down at Hest’s case beside her knee and wanted, with sudden force, to be somewhere else.

She spent the afternoon asking questions.

Dena was not the only person in Alderfold who used the animals or the woodland. Two other households shared rights in the coppice, and the settlement’s carts carried wood together when the larger buyers came. An older boy had helped Dena through the previous winter. Sariel found him mending a roof with his father. He had left because his father’s knee had worsened and the house needed another pair of hands.

“Did she pay you?”

He looked down at her. “What?”

“Dena.”

“On the day.”

“All of it?”

His father said something she could not hear. The boy laughed.

“He says ask her about the spoon.”

“What spoon?”

“I took one home by mistake. She says I still owe it.”

Sariel asked at the wood store, at the pump, and at a cottage where a woman was hanging little bundles of herbs beneath the eaves. She learned that Dena was impatient, that she undercharged for cart hire and complained about the result, that the mule had been difficult for eleven years, and that the cupboard in Finn’s offered room truly had no back because damp had spoiled it.

Nobody gave her the answer she had prepared herself to use.

She examined the room while Dena was out. The latch worked. The window opened onto the yard, with a low roof beneath it that a person could reach without a ladder. There were no chains, no paper waiting to bind a newcomer to years of service, no hidden shelf full of unpaid accounts.

The bed was narrow. Someone had put a folded blanket on it. On the sill stood a chipped cup with a blue flower painted inside.

Sariel picked it up. It was an ugly flower, with uneven petals and a stem too thick for it. She put it back exactly where she had found it.

Finn was waiting at the foot of the stairs.

“Well?” he asked.

She could have been angry that he had known where to find her. Instead she said, “The shutter catches at the bottom.”

“Thane said he’d plane it.”

“The latch is sound.”

“Good.”

She leaned against the stair rail. From the yard came the sound of Benjamin splitting wood, each blow harder than necessary.

“What if you want to leave?” she asked.

“I’ll leave.”

“If you haven’t been paid?”

“Then I’ll ask for my pay.”

“Finn.”

He came a step nearer. “I know you can find a way out of places. I’m glad of it. But I need you to look at this one while I’m still in it.”

She looked through the open door into the yard. The fence they had repaired stood straight enough. Dena was showing Kestra how to distinguish two trees by the buds. Fox had moved into the shade beneath a bench. Beyond them the forest rose green and dense under the late-summer light.

“The cup is dreadful,” she said.

Finn smiled. “I like the cup.”

She pressed her lips together. Then she held out her hand, and he took it without making her explain why.

They stayed one more night because none of them was ready to leave in the morning.

Tavia wrote the arrangement for Dena and Finn. She read it aloud twice: bed, meals, the weekly money, what work was expected, how either could end it. Dena asked for notice when possible because livestock did not stop needing food during a quarrel. Finn agreed. Tavia put “when possible” into the written line and showed them both.

Zara counted the common purse into eight equal shares on a clean cloth. The sums looked smaller separated. Sariel watched Finn’s portion gather near his hand and remembered Kes’s safe without wanting to. The seven remaining portions went back together after everyone had seen them. Finn put his into the little inner pocket of his old coat.

“You’ll need the blanket,” Elena said.

“There’s one upstairs.”

“You’ll need another.”

They gave him the blanket. He kept his knife, his own small things, and Fox, whose ownership had never depended on anybody’s approval. He tried to give Elena back a piece of linen. She told him not to be an idiot. He kissed her cheek, and she cried angrily enough that he apologized before she could say she was not angry with him.

Benjamin took him outside after supper. Sariel watched them through the doorway. They walked to the repaired fence and stood there, the two dark shapes close together. Benjamin spoke for some time. Finn answered. Then Benjamin put both arms around him and held on.

Sariel looked away.

That night nobody slept early. They passed a bowl of Dena’s terrible onions and told stories about things that had gone wrong before the fire. Tavia described a letter in which she had accidentally promised a man six oxen instead of six measures of oats. Zara said the man should have accepted promptly. Finn wanted to know whether the oxen had arrived. Tavia threw a piece of straw at him.

At dawn the packs were lighter and the hay shed looked too large.

Finn walked with them to the western turn. Fox came too, then found something in the hedge that interested him more than farewell. Finn called him twice and gave up.

“If he follows us,” Zara said, “we’re charging for his meals.”

“Send the account to Dena.”

“I wouldn’t dare.”

They hugged him one at a time, awkward with straps between them. Kestra promised to write. Tavia explained which traders were likely to pass. Thane reminded him about the shutter. Benjamin said nothing, but squeezed the back of his neck before stepping away.

Sariel was last. She had thought of several things to say and disliked all of them now.

“You can write even if you don’t need anything,” Finn told her.

She nodded against his shoulder.

He stayed at the turn while they walked on. After a little way she looked back. Fox had emerged from the hedge with something small and unidentifiable in his mouth. Finn bent to inspect it, laughing, then waved without straightening.

Sariel lifted her hand. She kept it raised until the trees hid him.

Chapter 10 A place until morning

For four days after leaving Alderfold, Elena kept turning to ask Finn about things.

Once it was a bird calling from a hedge. Once it was whether a sheep caught by its fleece would let them approach. Sariel helped free the sheep, which immediately tried to run through the same gap again. They blocked the gap with branches and walked on, laughing. Elena began composing the story she would put into a letter.

The last night before Millhaven they slept beside a farm’s empty threshing floor. Benjamin found a smooth place to roll their bedclothes; Thane made a hook to keep the cooking pot from tipping; Zara acquired eggs by persuading the farmer’s wife that Tavia could settle a disputed letter more cheaply than the clerk at the next market. They ate the eggs with a solemnity that lasted until one slid from Benjamin’s bread onto his boot.

He picked it up, inspected it, and looked around the circle.

“I didn’t see anything,” Sariel said.

“Neither did I,” Elena told him.

Tavia covered Kestra’s eyes. Benjamin ate the egg. Afterward they sat close around the little fire while the farmer’s wife dictated a second letter, which had nothing to do with the dispute and a great deal to do with a niece who had not visited. Tavia wrote it without asking for another payment. Elena fell asleep listening to the woman remember which flowers the niece liked.

Elena smelled the bread before she saw the village.

It came to them on a warm current of air between the damp hedges, faint enough that she thought she had imagined it. Then the road turned, the trees opened, and below them lay roofs, a square, a tall store with a patched gable, and the pale movement of water along the eastern fields. Smoke stood above a chimney in a blue line. Somewhere a hammer was striking iron.

“Millhaven,” Kestra said.

She had asked twice at the last farm and once again at the fork, where the unused branch of the road went down among reeds. Nobody disputed her now.

Benjamin stopped beside Elena. His shoulder no longer needed a dressing, though he still shifted his pack before lifting it. The road had browned his face and made the sword look less like a weapon he had borrowed for an occasion. He looked at the roofs without speaking.

Elena wanted a bed. She wanted to take off her clothes without first deciding which pieces would be least uncomfortable to put on wet. She wanted a bowl somebody else would wash. These wishes had grown so large that the sight of a village almost frightened her. It was quite possible that every bed belonged to someone else.

Tavia smoothed her hair. The burned patch on her right forearm was covered by a light dressing now, and she had begun using the hand normally except when she forgot and caught the tender skin on something. She drew her sleeve down before they entered the square.

“Let me speak first.”

“I thought we could all shout our needs,” Zara said.

“After I’ve spoken.”

The square was smaller than it had looked from the hill. Two carts stood beside a covered weighing place. A boy was sweeping husks toward a drain, losing more to the breeze than he gathered. Beyond the northern houses, the mill’s wheel moved with an uneven clunk. The forge lay to the west, its open doors showing a man bent over a workbench while another person argued with him from the yard.

People looked up as the seven passed. Elena recognized the order of their attention: packs, faces, sword, packs again. She had watched strangers arrive at the Coin in much the same way.

The bakery door was propped with a brick. Inside, a woman with flour up both forearms was sliding loaves onto a shelf. Her hair had escaped its scarf in little dark curls. She saw them through the doorway and set down her board.

“Bread?”

“And perhaps advice,” Tavia said.

“The advice is to buy the bread while it’s here.”

Zara stepped forward. They bought two loaves. Elena held one because her hands were free, and its heat came through the cloth so pleasantly that she nearly closed her eyes.

“We’re looking for work and somewhere to sleep,” Tavia said. “Seven people. We can pay for food while we make an arrangement, but not many nights at an inn.”

“We haven’t an inn that would take seven without putting somebody in a cupboard.”

“A dry floor would do.”

The woman looked at them more closely. Her gaze lingered on Tavia’s wrist.

“Where from?”

“Silkstrand.”

“Why here?”

Tavia paused. “The road came here.”

The woman gave a small, unwilling smile. “Sometimes it does.”

She was Mira. She had a loft behind the bakery where spare boards and sacks were stored, and a yard that needed clearing before the next flour delivery. They could have the loft until morning if they moved the boards into the dry lean-to, swept, and helped unload when the cart came. She would provide supper in addition to the bread they had bought. After morning they would speak again.

“No fire upstairs,” she said. “No wet clothes against the flour sacks. No knives left where a child can reach them. If you hear somebody up before dawn, it’s me, and I don’t want to be rescued from my own oven.”

Benjamin opened his mouth, then shut it.

Tavia repeated the terms. Zara asked which boards needed moving and where the flour cart would stop. Mira answered both without seeming offended that they wanted to know before agreeing.

“We can do that,” Tavia said.

“Good. Eat some bread first. You look as if a gust would take you.”

She cut a heel from a third loaf and spread it with something dark from a crock. When Elena tried to count out another coin, Mira pushed the plate toward her.

“That’s mine to give.”

Elena took a piece. Plum, cooked until it was almost black, sweet and sharp against the warm crumb. She stood in the doorway eating it while the village went about its afternoon. Nobody needed her to move just yet.

The loft had mice, a low beam in exactly the wrong place, and enough room to lie flat. Elena loved it at once.

They shifted the boards carefully. Thane examined each as it came down, pronouncing some fit for use and others fit only for improving somebody’s opinion of the rest. Mira listened from the yard while kneading a second batch of dough. She told him the bad ones were meant for the oven. He apologized to the good ones for making them share accommodation.

Elena had just swept a clear space when a woman came to the bakery door holding a girl’s hand. The girl was perhaps ten, with dirt on her knees and a piece of cloth wrapped around her palm. She was trying not to cry in front of the strangers.

“Vennit’s at home?” the woman asked Mira.

“She was an hour ago.”

“Can someone watch my younger one while I take this one over? He’s asleep.”

Mira asked Benjamin to sit with the younger child. He set down the board he was carrying and nodded.

Elena put down the broom. “I can come with you. I have some medicine.”

The mother considered her, then the bag at her feet.

“You’re a healer?”

“I work with wounds.”

It was a smaller answer than the one she had nearly given. The woman accepted it. They went south from the square along a lane where kitchen gardens stood behind low fences. The girl held her injured hand away from her body as though it had recently become someone else’s.

“How did it happen?” Elena asked.

“The wood stack.”

“A splinter?”

The girl nodded.

Vennit’s cottage smelled of dried herbs, vinegar, and the cabbage cooking somewhere behind it. She was older than Elena had expected, with strong hands and a face that gave very little away while listening. She cleared a place beside the window and asked the girl to sit.

Elena offered her bag. Vennit glanced through it, then handed it back.

“Hold the bowl.”

The cloth came away to reveal a small puncture near the base of the thumb. Elena could see a dark line beneath the skin. She reached for her fine needle.

Vennit put a hand over the opening of the bag.

“What came out?” she asked the girl.

“A bit.”

“How big?”

The girl showed them with two fingers.

“And what did the end look like?”

“Broken.”

Vennit turned the hand toward the light. There was another sore place farther along the palm, almost hidden by dirt. Elena had mistaken the dark line for the whole splinter. It was only part of its track.

“Give me time to see it,” Vennit said quietly.

Heat climbed Elena’s neck. The girl’s mother was watching. She wanted to explain that she would have looked again before doing anything. She held the bowl instead.

Vennit worked slowly, talking about the wood stack and who had been meant to carry it. The girl’s answers grew longer. By the time the little piece of wood came free, they had established that her brother was a fool, that she had told him not to put the split ends toward the path, and that their mother had already heard enough of the matter to last until winter.

Elena cleaned the surrounding skin when asked. Vennit let her dress the hand, then made her loosen one turn so the girl could move her fingers comfortably.

“Tomorrow,” Vennit told the mother. “Bring her back if it looks worse before then.”

After they left, Elena washed the bowl. She could have taken her bag and gone. Instead she dried it carefully and said, “I thought I could see the end.”

“You could see something.”

“Yes.”

Vennit sat down with a small sound of relief and rubbed one knee.

“Where were you taught?”

Elena gave the name of the apothecary where she had worked, then said she had not completed the apprenticeship. Vennit did not ask why at once. She asked what Elena had done there, what she had treated since, what supplies she had used most on the road. By the time they reached Tavia’s burn, Elena had stopped standing as though awaiting dismissal.

“Bring her tomorrow,” Vennit said. “If she wishes. I have cloth you can wash, too. More hands would be useful.”

“I can help.”

“You’ve just done some.”

Elena looked at the bowl, absurdly pleased.

By the time she returned to the bakery, Thane had gone to the mill with the smith, and Benjamin had been promoted from moving boards to unloading flour. Mira was feeding him instructions as quickly as he could obey them. He seemed delighted to have work with an obvious end.

“Your friend says she can mend the gate,” Mira told Elena.

“Which friend?”

Mira pointed with a floury elbow. Sariel stood by the yard gate with Hest’s case open on a wall. She had removed the latch plate and laid its pieces in order.

“She says it doesn’t catch.”

“Doesn’t it?”

“Not unless I lift it. That’s how I wanted it.”

Sariel looked up. Mira demonstrated with both arms held as though carrying a tray.

“I push it with my hip. If it caught every time, I’d have to put the bread down.”

“You could change the latch,” Sariel said.

“I could. But I didn’t ask to.”

For a moment Sariel remained perfectly still. Then she began putting the pieces back.

“The hinge is pulling,” she said.

“Yes. That I would be grateful for.”

“I can do that.”

Mira went back to the oven. Elena joined Sariel at the wall and held the small screws while she worked. Neither spoke for a while. Then Sariel said, “She gave us the plum stuff.”

“I know.”

“It was good.”

“Very.”

Sariel fitted the last screw, tested the gate with her hip, and opened it again. Elena tried it too. They stood side by side pushing the gate until Mira called that she had already decided it worked and would like them to come inside.

There was a plate to take to the mill. Elena volunteered, partly because she wanted to see where Thane had gone and partly because the bakery had grown so warm she feared she would fall asleep standing up. Sariel came with her.

They followed the north approach. The mill stood on the west bank of the stream, its wheel throwing a steady pattern of water into the race. A short wooden crossing carried the approach over an overflow channel. Beyond the mill, higher ground rose into a lane that turned west toward a little chapel and an orchard.

Elena noticed the slope because she was tired. Sariel kept glancing into the spaces between buildings. At the crossing she stopped to look beneath the deck.

“What?” Elena asked.

“Nothing yet.”

The mill door was open. Inside, Thane and Tom were bent over a wooden fitting laid across two trestles. A younger man held a lamp, although there was still daylight outside. Flour dust covered his hair so thoroughly that Elena could not tell its color.

“You could make a proper shoe for it,” Thane was saying.

“I could make a proper new mill,” Tom answered. “The question is what we can finish before supper.”

Tom was thick through the forearms, with a singed place in one eyebrow and a voice that carried easily over the wheel. He took a piece of wood from the bench and held it against the worn fitting.

“There. Replace that when it wears. The iron lasts.”

Thane turned the piece in his hands. Elena watched him resist the suggestion, consider it, and become interested despite himself.

“It’ll need a shoulder.”

“Then give it one.”

“And if he drives it too far—”

“Halen,” Tom said to the young man with the lamp, “will you drive it too far?”

“Probably,” Halen said.

Tom spread his hands. Thane laughed and reached for his tools.

Elena put the food on a clear board. Sariel sat near the doorway. For the next little while they watched the fitting change from a problem into something that could be put back. Thane cut; Tom pared a place he had left too proud; Halen held it and reported honestly when his arms began to ache. Nobody pretended the repair would last forever. When they fitted it, the clunk in the wheel’s rhythm diminished to a sound Elena could barely distinguish from the rest of the mill.

Thane stood listening. His face opened into a smile so young that Elena felt she had not properly seen him for days.

Tom clapped him on the shoulder. “Come and explain that face to my wife. She thinks I enjoy the noise.”

Linett was waiting at the forge with a list, a basket, and very little patience for either man.

“You said a short look,” she told Tom.

“It became a longer look.”

“I noticed. The plough iron is still here.”

She turned to Thane. “Can you read figures?”

“Usually.”

“Then don’t let him say he has none to do.”

Tom kissed her cheek. She allowed it without surrendering the list. Elena liked her immediately.

On the way back to the bakery, they passed Roven unloading baskets beside the weighing place. He saw them and waved so broadly that one basket slipped from his arm. Benjamin caught it before it hit the ground.

“You crossed,” Kestra said, arriving behind them.

“Next morning. Straight over. No idea why.”

“Did you do anything differently?”

“Had breakfast first.”

Kestra looked as though she might ask whether that mattered, then laughed. Roven asked about the rest of them, and they told him where Finn had stayed. He knew Dena. He said the animals would be better for another hand and the hand would be better for learning when Dena was teasing.

“He’ll manage,” Sariel said.

Roven nodded. “I expect so.”
