Mira’s supper was thick soup, bread, and a dish of something green cooked with onions. They ate at a table behind the bakery after the last customer left. Mira sat with them for part of it, then rose to check the oven and came back carrying a small loaf she had made from the scraps.

“That one won’t sell neatly,” she said.

Benjamin cut it into eight pieces and put the largest back in front of her. She looked at him, then took it.

Later Elena climbed to the loft and found that someone had hung a blanket across one end so they could change without asking everybody else to turn away. Tavia sat behind it undoing her sleeve. The others were settling their packs in the clear spaces between the beams.

Elena looked at the place she had chosen for her bed. A dry floor, a borrowed quilt, her bag within reach. She took off both shoes and left them standing upright beside the wall.

Downstairs Mira banked the fire. The village sounds thinned. Elena lay on her back and listened to Benjamin telling Thane that the mill had sounded better from the square, until Thane began asking exactly how much better and Tavia told them both to let the wheel sleep.

Elena laughed into her blanket. Then, before she could decide what tomorrow needed, she slept.

Chapter 11 The grain collectors

On their third morning in Millhaven, Sariel was allowed to buy bread on account.

It was a small account. Mira made a mark on a board beside the oven and told her not to mistake it for an invitation to eat the stock. Still, Sariel walked out with a loaf under her arm and a quite unreasonable pleasure in owing somebody for an ordinary breakfast.

They had remained in the loft while the arrangement changed by inches. Tavia had written letters for two households. Zara helped Mira check a flour delivery whose measure had been disputed. Elena went to Vennit’s after breakfast and returned carrying less medicine but more washed linen. Thane had been invited to the forge, then sent away when he tried to use a piece of iron already promised to somebody else. He had gone back with an apology and a question about scrap, which had been better received.

Sariel fetched, carried, and looked. Nobody had asked her to look, but she found it difficult to stop.

The village had no wall. Doors opened onto lanes that met behind houses or ended in gardens. From the eastern edge, a person could reach the mill without passing through the square. The north approach narrowed at the wooden crossing over the overflow channel, then widened by the mill door. The higher lane behind it offered a way west toward the chapel. She had walked there the previous evening and watched the sun shine through the orchard.

Benjamin had joined her. They had spoken mostly about Finn. Benjamin wondered whether Dena would give him enough food. Sariel said he could eat the onions. Benjamin said that might be a punishment for something he had done in a previous life, and she had laughed so suddenly that an old woman pruning a tree looked over the wall.

Now she saw Halen outside the mill, balancing a sack against his thigh while another man tied its mouth. He grinned when he recognized her.

“The wheel’s still quiet.”

“Tell Thane. He’ll want to stand beside it.”

“He already has. Twice.”

Sariel tore a small piece from the loaf and offered it. Halen took it with his teeth because both hands were occupied. He shifted the sack, and she caught its lower corner before it slid.

“Where?”

“By the others.”

They put it beside a row of marked sacks under the eaves. Twelve, she counted without intending to. Each bore crossed mill-sails in red ochre and a tied strip at the neck.

“Going out?”

Halen’s face altered. “Collection.”

“Whose?”

He looked past her toward the square. “You’ll hear.”

She waited. He rubbed flour from one wrist with the other hand.

“Ursillo. Garrick’s people. They keep the road safe, according to them.”

“Safe from whom?”

Halen gave her a look that made the question unnecessary. She wished she had not asked it so neatly. He had been carrying the answer since before she arrived.

“Do they come often?”

“Often enough that we know which sacks to put out.”

He bent to the next one. Sariel helped him lift it, then returned to the bakery with the bread pressed more tightly beneath her arm.

The collectors came before the square had finished filling.

Four men and a cart, with a fifth man driving. Two rode ahead, one walked beside the load bed, and the last followed at a little distance with a bow wrapped against the dust. The driver looked at nobody. The man in front wore a dark coat despite the warmth and carried a short staff across his saddle.

Sariel stood in Mira’s doorway. She had been going to ask whether the gate wanted oil. She forgot the question.

Thorne met the men beside the weighing place. The elder was leaner than she had expected, with a careful way of setting his feet and eyes that moved from one person to another without hurry. He greeted the leader by no name Sariel caught.

“The grain’s at the mill.”

“How much?”

“Twelve, as agreed.”

The man in the dark coat took a folded paper from inside his breast. “Eighteen.”

Thorne did not reach for it. “That was not the agreement.”

“It is now.”

People continued moving in the square, but their movements shortened. A woman took two children through a door. Roven set down a basket and stayed beside it. Benjamin came from the bakery loft with his sword belted on. He looked at Sariel, then at the men, and went no farther.

Tavia approached Thorne. She had dressed carefully that morning for a household’s letter, and the clean collar made her look better rested than she was.

“May I see the notice?” she asked.

The collector looked her up and down. “Who are you?”

“Someone who can read it.”

“So can I.”

“Then we can agree what it says.”

His mouth tightened, but he handed her the paper. Sariel watched his hands. Large, clean nails, a pale scar across the right knuckle. The staff had a weight under its leather head.

Tavia read. She asked when the new demand had been issued, whether the eighteen replaced the twelve or supplemented it, and what quantity of grain had been used to reckon the sacks. Her voice remained mild. Each question made the man explain something in front of people who would remember.

“Garrick has men to feed,” he said at last.

“So has Millhaven,” Thorne answered.

“You want those men on the road or don’t you?”

“I want the amount we agreed to be the amount you take.”

Sariel glanced at Benjamin. His jaw was set. She touched his sleeve before he could move.

“Let them talk.”

“I am.”

“Keep doing it.”

He looked down at her hand, then nodded.

The discussion moved toward the mill because the leader wanted to see the grain. Tavia walked beside Thorne. Sariel followed far enough behind that nobody could claim she had joined the bargaining. Benjamin came with her. Thane emerged from the forge yard carrying a narrow plank he had been asked to shape. When he saw where they were going, he put it on his shoulder and followed.

At the mill, Halen stood by the sacks. He had not expected an audience. Sariel saw him straighten when Thorne arrived, and then stop as the man in the dark coat struck one sack with the end of his staff.

“These are light.”

“They were weighed,” Halen said.

“By you?”

“By the mill.”

“That’s what I asked.”

Tavia held out the notice. “It doesn’t specify a different measure.”

“It specifies eighteen.”

“The village has prepared twelve.”

“Then we’ll wait while it prepares six more.”

Thorne said the remaining grain was not surplus. There were household shares, seed, work already owed. The collector listened with the expression of a man hearing weather described at length.

One of the men behind him went into the mill.

Halen moved to block the door.

“Those aren’t marked for you.”

The man pushed him aside. Halen caught the doorpost and stayed upright, then reached to prevent him taking a sack from inside. Sariel saw the collector’s elbow draw back.

“Halen,” Thorne said.

The first blow struck the young man below the cheek. He staggered against the frame. The collector hit him again, using the short stick from his belt this time.

Sariel began moving before she had a plan. Benjamin caught her eye, and she pointed once toward Halen, once toward the open yard.

He nodded.

The doorway was too narrow for the beating and the rescue together.

Sariel went in low, beside the collector’s right hip. She caught his wrist as he raised the stick again and drove it against the frame. The impact hurt her palm. He swore and turned toward her. She put her heel behind his foot and pushed with her shoulder, not hard enough to throw him cleanly, only enough to make him choose between falling and letting go.

He let go.

Benjamin took Halen beneath the arms and drew him out. Halen’s feet dragged. Blood ran from his nose into his open mouth, making him cough.

The collector reached for a knife.

Sariel struck his hand with the dropped stick. He cried out. She hit him again, on the forearm, because he was still trying to reach it. He backed into the mill, and she followed one step too far before Benjamin called her name.

She stopped.

Outside, the man in the dark coat had swung down from his horse. Thane put the plank across the narrow space between the cart and the wall, bracing one end against a stone projection. A second collector tried to come through and found his shins against wood instead of an open approach. He grabbed the plank. Thane held on.

“Put it down,” the man said.

“You first.”

It sounded ridiculous. Sariel might have laughed if the man had not drawn his sword.

Benjamin pushed Halen toward Elena, who had come from the square with Kestra. Then his own sword was out. The binding Thane had put around its hilt looked pale against his hand.

“He’s coming away,” Benjamin said.

The collector raised his blade. Benjamin did not rush him. He stood where the plank and the cart narrowed the yard, leaving Sariel room to come through behind him.

Elena had one of Halen’s arms over her shoulder. Kestra tried the other and flinched when he put weight on her still-tender ribs. Sariel saw it, saw Elena shift, saw Roven arrive and take the young man’s weight without being asked. They moved toward the square.

The bowman at the rear pulled the covering from his weapon.

“Thorne,” Tavia said sharply.

The elder stepped between the bowman and the departing group. Not close enough to seize anything. Close enough that an arrow would have to pass him.

“You have your twelve sacks,” he said. “You can load them. You will not shoot into my square.”

The leader looked past him. More villagers had come out now. Tom stood beside the mill wall with a hammer in one hand. Linett was calling people back from the cart wheels. Nobody had formed a line, but there were bodies where the collectors had expected space.

Sariel backed out of the doorway. The man she had struck followed, holding his arm against his chest. He looked from her to the leader and began talking quickly.

“She—”

“I saw.”

“He stopped me taking—”

“I saw.”

The leader’s voice was louder the second time. His man fell silent.

Sariel still held the stick. She could feel the place where the wood had struck bone. She had expected the familiar clear feeling that came when someone was finally doing something. Instead she was acutely aware of the cart, the horses, the boy’s blood on Benjamin’s sleeve, and the bowman whose hand had not left the string.

“Move that board,” the leader told Thane.

Thane looked at Benjamin.

“When the sword goes away,” Benjamin said.

The man facing him hesitated. Then the leader made a small motion with two fingers. The blade lowered. Benjamin waited until it was sheathed before he put away Orren’s sword.

Thane lifted the plank.

No one cheered. The driver climbed down and began loading the twelve sacks. The uninjured men helped him. Thorne watched each sack go onto the cart. Tavia stood beside him with the notice folded in her hand.

The leader mounted last. He looked at Sariel for long enough that she could feel herself wanting to give him a face he would remember.

She kept still.

“Garrick will hear about this.”

“He can hear the whole of it,” Thorne said.

“He’ll hear what he needs.”

They left by the northern road. The cart wheels made two dark lines in the dust where grain had spilled and been crushed beneath them.

Halen was at Vennit’s cottage. Sariel went there with the stick still in her hand and did not realize she had brought it until Vennit told her to leave it outside.

The room was crowded. Elena stood beside the table holding a cloth to Halen’s face while he complained that she was pressing too hard. Vennit asked him which teeth hurt. His mother, a small woman with a loud voice, wanted to know why he had put himself in the doorway. Halen tried to answer both at once and spat blood into the bowl.

Sariel stayed near the door.

“He’ll keep the teeth,” Vennit said. “If he stops testing them with his tongue.”

Halen immediately stopped.

His mother looked at Sariel. “You got him out?”

“Benjamin did.”

“You helped.”

Sariel nodded.

The woman came across the room and took both her hands. Her grip was strong and flour-dry. She thanked Sariel without restraint, saying it twice, then once more when she seemed to remember how badly the beating might have ended. Sariel did not know where to look. She had received payment, praise, threats, promises. She had rarely been thanked by someone who was not trying to move the conversation toward the next thing.

“I’m glad he’s here,” she managed.

The woman squeezed her hands and let go.

Outside, a man waited beside the garden gate. Sariel recognized him from the weighing place. He had watched the collectors leave without speaking.

“You’ve made a fine beginning,” he said.

She stopped.

“He was beating Halen.”

“I saw.”

“Then what did you want me to do?”

“I want to know what you do when they come back.”

The satisfaction she had carried out of the cottage became hot and defensive.

“Let them beat him, then?”

The man looked away, toward the square. “My boys sleep above the grain. If they burn it, where do I put them? If they take it, what do I feed them?”

She had no answer ready. She could invent one. She could tell him how a narrow approach might be held, how men who came to collect were not necessarily men willing to fight, how a cart could be overturned. She could see the places where every answer might fail.

“I don’t know yet,” she said.

He nodded once, without pleasure, and went into the cottage.

Sariel picked up the stick from beside the door. Then she put it down again. She did not want it in the loft.

They ate supper in Mira’s kitchen while the bakery remained quiet around them.

Benjamin had washed his sleeve, leaving a damp place that clung to his arm. Thane had brought the plank back to the forge. Elena was still at Vennit’s. Nobody had much to say until Zara asked whether they intended to stay.

“Tonight?” Benjamin said.

“After tonight.”

Sariel looked at Tavia. Tavia was folding and unfolding the copy she had made of the demand.

“I don’t want another house around us while men decide how to get in,” Tavia said.

Benjamin’s face changed. “Neither do I.”

“Then we need to say something more useful than that.”

Mira set a bowl between them. “You need to eat something more useful than your own tongues first.”

They ate. The food was good, and Sariel resented being able to enjoy it. Then Thane described the expression on the collector’s face when the plank met his shins, and Benjamin laughed before he could stop himself. Tavia covered her mouth. Zara asked whether the plank would be charged to the village as a weapon or a repair. Mira said it belonged to a customer and would be charged to Thane if he had dented it.

The laughter lasted longer than Sariel expected. She let it. When Elena returned, they made room for her and listened while she described Halen arguing with his mother about whether he could return to work in the morning.

“Can he?” Benjamin asked.

“Vennit says no. I agree.”

“Does Halen?”

“Halen is practicing disagreement very energetically.”

Later Sariel went to the gate alone. It swung properly on the repaired hinge and rested without catching. She pushed it twice, then stood listening to the mill.

Benjamin came out and leaned against the wall beside her.

“You went back in,” he said.

“One step.”

“I know.”

“I heard you.”

“I know that too.”

He looked tired. She could see the mark the sword hilt had left on his palm.

“You got him clear,” she said.

Benjamin nodded, and for a moment his face showed the full pleasure of it. “We did.”

She stood beside him until the square had gone dark.

The rider came the next morning while Mira was taking out the first bread. He stayed in the saddle and asked for Thorne. Sariel watched from the doorway as the elder broke the coarse seal and read.

Garrick Ursillo would finish his harvest collections and return in three weeks. Millhaven would provide the missing six sacks, an additional payment for defiance, and an account of the strangers it had chosen to shelter. If the village intended to resist, it should be ready when the three weeks ended.

Thorne read the notice twice. Then he asked the rider to wait while he made a copy.

The rider said he had not been paid to wait.

“Then water your horse,” Thorne told him, and carried the paper inside.

Chapter 12 What they can promise

Kestra had expected the meeting to begin with the threat. It began with a disagreement about sheep.

A woman near the door wanted to know whether the southern grazing would still be open if the village put watchers on the road. The owner of the adjoining field said the sheep had no business being there in the first place. Thorne let them speak long enough for Kestra to begin wondering whether either had read Garrick’s notice. Then he asked who would take the sheep if their owners were driven from their homes.

The woman looked at the field owner. He looked at the floor.

“We’ll settle the gate afterward,” Thorne said. “Sit, both of you.”

They had gathered in the ground room of the old wool store near the square. The upper floor creaked whenever someone crossed the boards above. Wool had not been kept here for years, but a faint animal smell remained in the corners, mixed with dust and the apples drying on trays near the window.

Kestra sat beside Tavia, with her notebook balanced on one knee. She had brought it because she did not know what else to do with her hands. Around them villagers occupied benches, stools, sacks, the edge of a low chest. Others stood in the doorway. Halen’s mother had come without Halen. Vennit had told him to rest, and for once the village appeared willing to assist her in being obeyed.

Thorne read the notice. He read it without improving Garrick’s language or making it more frightening than it already was. Three weeks. The grain. The additional payment. The strangers.

Somebody asked whether handing over the strangers would end the matter.

Kestra’s pen stopped.

“It doesn’t say hand them over,” Tavia said.

“It says account.”

“Yes.”

“He can make that mean what he likes.”

“He can try. We should still know what he has actually asked before agreeing to something worse.”

Thorne nodded. “They are here to answer for themselves. So are we.”

Benjamin stood. He looked so much like a man about to promise to defend a doorway until he died that Kestra nearly reached for his sleeve.

“I can fight,” he said. “I’ve had training. I can help people learn to use what they have.”

“How many battles?” a man asked.

Benjamin looked at him. “No battle like the one you’re asking about.”

There was a movement in the room. Disappointment, perhaps, or relief that he had not made them believe something else.

“The watch taught me to hold a position,” he continued. “Orren taught me more after that. I can show people how to stand without getting in one another’s way. I can’t turn field workers into soldiers in three weeks.”

Tom, seated near the wall, rubbed his chin. “Can you turn them into people who stop hitting the man beside them?”

“I hope so.”

“That’d be an improvement.”

A few people laughed. Benjamin sat down with color in his face, but he was smiling.

Thane spoke next. He could repair fittings, strengthen doors, build barriers, and make things from available timber if someone told him what timber was available. He could not promise enough iron for the village by looking hard at Tom’s forge. Tom said he was grateful this had been understood.

Elena offered treatment and preparation, then looked across at Vennit.

“With her agreement. She knows the people and the supplies.”

Vennit nodded once. “There is work.”

Sariel did not stand. She said she could look at approaches, watch men without being easily noticed, and find ways through places other people thought closed. A woman asked if she meant stealing.

“Sometimes,” Sariel said.

That brought a different kind of silence.

“Will you be doing it here?”

“Not from you.”

“Good,” the woman said. “My sister has already borrowed everything worth taking.”

The room laughed more freely. Sariel looked down, surprised into a smile.

Kestra’s turn came before she had found a satisfactory way to describe herself. She could read several forms of writing that most people would reasonably never wish to meet. She knew a little magic, some history, and enough about old mechanisms to explain them badly to Thane. None of it sounded like something to set against men with swords.

“I can read old records,” she said. “And inscriptions. I can compare claims with what was written before. I have some training in arcane work, but I can’t promise a defense from it.”

“Can you stop an arrow?” someone asked.

“No.”

“Light a fire?”

“With preparation. Flint is quicker.”

The man looked disappointed. Kestra wished he had asked about a damaged text, or a library catalogue, or anything for which she could have produced an impressive and honest answer.

Tavia said she could write letters, examine terms, and speak to people who were accustomed to being obeyed. Zara could buy, sell, reckon quantities, and tell them when an offer required more than they could pay. Neither offered to become a fighter.

Thorne listened to all of them. Then he asked the village what it had to offer itself.

The answers took longer.

By noon Kestra knew who owned the spare carts, which households had relatives elsewhere, who could work a bow, and why the field owner would rather eat bark than take an oath to Baroness Valerius.

“His father disputed a boundary with her father’s steward,” Mira explained quietly when they stepped outside.

“Did he win?”

“He won the boundary. Lost the trees.”

Mira passed her a cup of water. The square looked unchanged. People were carrying baskets and arguing about prices. The mill wheel turned. Kestra had the unreasonable feeling that the village ought to have become visibly endangered now that she understood the details.

Several households wanted to pay Garrick. Others said they had already paid and watched the price grow. A few wanted to leave before the roads filled with the gang’s men. The Baroness had soldiers and stores. Why not ask her? Why not accept a strong neighbor’s help rather than trust seven strangers who had arrived with less food than they could eat in a day?

Kestra could not dismiss the question. She liked that less than she had expected.

Thorne came out carrying a ring of keys.

“You read old records?” he asked her.

“Yes.”

“Come and look at ours.”

He took her and Tavia into a small room behind the meeting place. A chest stood on blocks above the floor. Its lock was clean and well kept. Kestra noticed that with a fleeting, absurd desire to tell Sariel about a lock nobody needed to repair.

Inside lay bundles wrapped in cloth: accounts, boundary copies, marriages, a record of grain advanced after a bad winter. Thorne lifted a flatter parcel from beneath them and put it on the table.

“The market rights. My grandfather had a copy made. This one’s older.”

Kestra unwrapped it carefully. The parchment had darkened along the folds, and the seal hung in a little protective cup of stitched leather. She had expected something half legendary, a scrap whose supposed authority depended on nobody being able to read it. Instead she found a document with a date, witnesses, defined dues, and a description of the road and mill access precise enough to check on foot.

For the first time that morning, she knew exactly what to do.

She asked for a clean board, a little weight to hold the corners, and more light. Thorne opened the shutter. Tavia brought a lamp but left it unlit until Kestra asked. They worked through the first section together, Kestra reading the older forms, Tavia testing how a claim might be understood now.

“The village holds the market,” Kestra said. “Not by the neighboring estate’s leave. These dues go to the authority that recognizes the market, and these are separate payments for use of the road.”

Thorne leaned closer.

“That’s what I was told.”

“There is a restriction on changing the passage charge. It requires witnesses from the market households.”

“Does that stop the Baroness taking it?”

Kestra looked up. The answer had arrived in her mouth already complete: yes, of course, the provision was clear. Then she saw the ring of keys in his hand and remembered Garrick’s collector striking a sack with his staff.

“It gives you something to challenge a claim with,” she said.

“Who hears the challenge?”

She went back to the text. Tavia helped her trace the office named in the document. There would be a clerk at the market jurisdiction beyond the local estates, someone who received copies and recorded disputes. Reaching that person required a messenger, money, witnesses, and time. A reply would travel by the same uncertain roads that had kept them walking for weeks.

Thorne sat down.

“So it matters,” he said, “but it doesn’t stand in the lane.”

“No.”

He studied her face, then touched the edge of the parchment.

“Copy it anyway.”

They spent the next hour doing so. Kestra read the clauses aloud while Tavia wrote, then they changed places and checked each other’s work. The task absorbed her. The familiar shapes, the delicate problem of a letter worn into its neighbor, the satisfaction of recognizing a term she had once been told she would never need: she enjoyed all of it. When she found a confirming mark on the reverse, she called out before remembering to sound composed.

Tavia leaned over her shoulder.

“There,” Kestra said, pointing. “A later confirmation. They paid for this twice. Someone wanted it to survive an argument.”

“Then let’s make sure our copy does.”

Thorne brought them cheese and bread on a wooden plate. Kestra ate with one hand while following the line with the other. She did not notice she was hungry until the first mouthful.

The afternoon meeting had fewer people standing and more people prepared to speak.

Those who had gone home to eat had returned with questions from husbands, wives, parents, and neighbors who could not leave their work. Nobody had made one mind of the village during the interval. Kestra was relieved. She had begun to suspect that unanimity would mean somebody important had not been allowed into the room.

Tavia explained the market rights in plain words. Kestra answered questions about the copy. The field owner wanted to know whether the document made the Baroness’s offer illegal before she made it. Tavia said no. Mira wanted to know whether asking for help meant accepting any terms that came with it. Tavia said no to that too, but added that letters should say so clearly.

“And Garrick?” a woman asked.

“Garrick hasn’t claimed under these rights,” Kestra said. “He’s making a demand by force.”

“Then he won’t care what the parchment says.”

“I don’t expect him to.”

The woman nodded, as if that answer at least had not wasted her time.

They argued about payment. Zara had asked to see the grain accounts and could now tell them what six more sacks would take from particular households. She named no household without permission. Some people gave it readily. Others preferred to stand and say for themselves what they had left.

A man who favored paying began describing the cost of resistance. Kestra expected Benjamin to interrupt. He stayed quiet until the man asked whether the strangers would still be here when the grain ran out.

“I mean to be,” Benjamin said. “But we haven’t agreed to ask you for a fight.”

“You started one yesterday.”

“We stopped Halen being beaten.”

“And now?”

Benjamin looked at Thorne, then at the others. Kestra could see how much he wanted to give the room a promise large enough to settle it.

“Now we can help you make it harder to do again,” he said.

Tom shifted on his bench. “Doors can be strengthened whether we pay or don’t. A watch can be kept whether the Baroness sends men or doesn’t. None of that empties a field if we do it at sensible hours.”

“You said sensible hours last time you needed a roof beam,” Linett told him.

“I was younger.”

“It was spring.”

The laughter gave Thorne room to propose the arrangement. For one week, while messages went out and households considered their choices, the newcomers would help with inspection, repairs, watches, and preparation. Nobody would be enrolled to fight merely by lending a tool or accepting treatment. The village would decide whether to resist when it knew more about the grain, the neighboring offers, and what its own people could do.

Sariel asked who could authorize work on private doors. Their owners, Thorne said. Thane asked who could promise timber. Tom and the households who held it. Elena asked where supplies could be kept dry and who could help her carry a patient. Vennit began naming people, then stopped and said they would be asked before being put down.

Kestra wrote until her hand ached.

A man could give two evenings but not three. A cart could carry wood if it was back before the next market. A widow offered a room for someone injured, provided no armed guard was put in her doorway. She had had enough of men ordering her to stay inside her own house.

Thorne asked the seven whether they accepted.

Tavia spoke first. “For the week. We can make letters and copies, help with the work we’ve described, and account for what we use. If any of us takes other employment or chooses to go, that must be possible.”

Benjamin looked at her, then nodded.

“Yes,” he said.

The others answered, each in turn. Kestra heard her own voice sound firmer than it had in the morning.

The room came with sweeping.

Thorne showed them the upper floor of the wool store after the meeting. It was a broad loft divided by a wooden partition, with two windows, a small side room, and stairs that did not require them to climb over Mira’s customers. The village would give them its use through the first week. After that the arrangement would be reviewed alongside the work. Food would come through Mira against agreed labor, not simply appear whenever seven hungry people arrived. They would buy their own extras. They would keep the stairs clear and give the key back if they left.

“Are there beds?” Zara asked.

“Some frames. We can find straw.”

“Blankets?”

“Ask Mira what can be spared.”

“I’m beginning to see why she sent us here.”

Thorne smiled. “She sent you here because she needs her loft for flour.”

The side room could hold medicine and papers. Elena asked to see its shutter. Kestra asked whether the roof leaked. Thane answered before Thorne could, pointing to an old stain and the newer board above it.

“It did.”

“It may again,” Thorne said. “Tell me before you put anything valuable beneath it.”

At the forge, Tom offered a workbench and use of ordinary tools when they were not required for village work. Iron, fuel, and completed work would be entered with Linett. Thane could not promise the forge’s labor to someone else. He could use the fire while Tom was there until Tom was satisfied he knew its habits.

“I’ve had a forge,” Thane said.

“You haven’t had mine.”

Thane looked around the yard, at the bellows, the shallow quenching trough, the racks of unfinished work. Then he put his tool roll on the offered bench and unfastened it.

“Where do you keep the good files?”

Tom grinned. “We’ll discuss those when we’ve known each other longer.”

Kestra watched Thane lay out his tools. Their places on the bench were provisional. He kept moving one a little farther from the edge, then another, discovering how his hands wanted to work in a room that did not belong to him. She understood the pleasure in it so keenly that she wanted a table of her own at once.

Back at the loft, they found seven sleeping places and argued about them. Benjamin wanted the place nearest the stairs. Tavia wanted him there too because he snored and she wanted the partition between them. He denied snoring. All six of the others answered at once.

Sariel chose a place where she could see a window. Elena took the smaller space near the medicine room. Zara put her pack down and went to ask Mira about bedding before the best of it was promised elsewhere.

Kestra discovered a shelf beneath one window wide enough for her notebook. She wiped it with a damp cloth, waited for it to dry, and arranged the scroll case, ink, and copied market rights along it. The afternoon light fell across the page without her having to fight anyone for a lamp.

Sariel came beside her carrying two cups.

“Mira says the first cup is free because we’ll otherwise come back and ask where the well is.”

Kestra took one. The water tasted faintly of the wooden bucket.

“There’s a place for your book,” Sariel said.

“I noticed.”

“You looked pleased.”

“I am.”

Sariel rested her elbows on the sill. Below, Tom crossed the square with Benjamin, explaining something with both hands. Elena emerged from the bakery carrying a folded blanket against her chest. Zara followed with three more and an expression suggesting she had prevailed in a matter of state.

Kestra drank. When she put the cup down, it left a wet ring beside the notebook. She moved the book, fetched a scrap of cloth, and wiped the ring away.

“We’ll need a table,” she said.

“We have a floor.”

“I’m progressing.”

Sariel laughed. Downstairs someone began bringing the bed frames in, striking a stair with the first one and asking loudly whose idea this had been. Kestra went to hold the door. By the time the last frame was upstairs, all seven were talking at once, and she had forgotten to be careful of how happy she sounded.

Part Three Learning the work

Chapter 13 A bad rehearsal

By the time Benjamin had arranged the first line, the woman with the milk cart had decided to go through it.

She came between two men carrying ash poles, pulling her little cart by its shafts. Neither man knew whether to move. One stepped backward; the other raised his pole and caught its end in the handle of a pail. Milk spilled over his shoe.

“Hold your places,” Benjamin said.

The woman stopped.

Sariel, sitting on the low wall beside the mill approach, shut her eyes briefly. Benjamin had been up before anyone except Mira. He had eaten standing, worked the stiffness from his left shoulder, and gone over his instructions in a voice low enough not to wake the room. She had heard him anyway. He had wanted this to go well so much that she had pretended to remain asleep rather than make him aware of being heard.

“My place is at the dairy,” the woman said. “His place is holding that pail upright.”

The man disentangled his pole. Benjamin came to help and was given the wet handle without ceremony.

“We only need the road for a little while.”

“You’ve had it since the bell.”

“Half the people hadn’t arrived.”

“I know. They were working.”

A cart waited on the north side of the crossing. Its driver had folded his arms over his belly. Behind it, two boys carried a sack between them and argued about which was doing most of the lifting. The mill wheel turned steadily. Every few moments the grain machinery inside made a heavy wooden knock. Nobody had agreed to stop any of it for Benjamin.

Sariel slid off the wall.

“Let them through.”

He looked at the space she had left, then at the line. “We haven’t even tried it.”

“We can try it after they pass.”

That required everyone to move. The man with the wet shoe set down his pole to wipe his foot, then went to speak to the cart driver. One of the others followed. A third announced that if there was to be a delay he would fetch the sack his wife needed. Benjamin stood holding the milk pail while his defense of Millhaven separated into people who had remembered things.

Sariel took the pail from him and returned it to the woman.

“How much did he spill?”

“Enough that you can come and wash the cart when you’re done.”

“I didn’t spill it.”

“You’re the one who asked.”

The woman departed with a little of the milk running from one wheel. Sariel watched her until Benjamin said her name.

“Don’t.”

“I haven’t said anything.”

“You’re waiting until it will be funnier.”

She looked at his face. “Probably.”

He laughed unwillingly, then bent to pick up the pole. Its owner returned to claim it, carrying a sack as well. Benjamin waited while he put the sack down. He asked the driver to let them have the crossing for the time it took to unload the cart.

“Then unload it,” the driver said.

Sariel helped. It was better than sitting on the wall watching Benjamin discover how many people could refuse him without even raising their voices.

The first attempt lasted less than a minute.

Benjamin put six villagers across the approach on the north side of the narrow overflow channel. Sariel was to come toward them with two others and test the line. Nobody was to strike hard. The practice poles had strips of old sack tied around their ends; Tom had refused to provide sharp iron for people who could scarcely carry it without hitting a neighbor.

“Keep the points together,” Benjamin said. “They can’t get between you if you keep—”

Sariel went around the line.

She stepped off the road into the rough strip beside the mill wall, ducked beneath a low branch, and came up behind the man nearest the water. He turned to look. His neighbor turned too. Their poles crossed. Sariel tapped the first man on the back with her padded stick and stepped out of reach before he could decide what response was permitted.

Benjamin lowered his sword. He was using it only to indicate positions, and the temporary binding at the hilt looked darker where his palm had warmed it.

“You were meant to come from the road.”

“Will they?”

“The first time. So people understand.”

The man she had tapped began laughing. “I understand.”

“So do I,” said his neighbor, disentangling their poles.

Sariel saw Benjamin’s mouth tighten. She had meant to show him the opening. She had also enjoyed doing it in front of everyone. Both things sat uncomfortably together while he walked over to inspect the strip.

“Again,” he said.

He moved the nearest defender to cover the wall. Sariel went toward the opposite end and found that the miller’s empty handbarrow blocked the way she wanted. She could climb over it, but she would have to put down her pole. The man watching that end grinned at her.

“Mine,” he said. “Don’t break it.”

She came straight at him instead. He thrust too early, leaning well beyond his feet. She caught the padded end under her arm. His neighbor tried to help and hit him on the shoulder. The third man backed toward the crossing, tripped over the edge of its deck, and sat down hard.

Elena came off the wall before he had finished swearing.

“Stay there.”

“I’m not planning a journey.”

She knelt, checked his ankle, and asked him to move his toes. Benjamin stood over them looking appalled.

“It’s only a bruise,” the man said.

“Let her decide,” Sariel told him.

He glared at her. “It’s my backside.”

Behind them Thane began laughing. He put both hands over his face, which did not help. The seated man looked around, saw who was laughing, and began to laugh too. Elena waited for him to stop shaking before asking whether he could stand.

They got him to his feet. Nothing worse had happened than embarrassment and a sore hip, but the group had used the best part of the morning. The cart driver called for the road. Two people went away without asking. Benjamin sheathed Orren’s sword, then drew it a little way again to check that he had not damaged the edge against the scabbard mouth.

Sariel went beside him.

“I should have told you about the wall first.”

“Yes.”

“I wanted them to see it.”

“They did.”

She waited. He rubbed a mark on the blade with his thumb, very carefully.

“Will you show me the other ways?” he asked.

“All of them?”

“The ones you’ve already found would be a start.”

She looked past him at the mill and the raised lane behind it. There were three ways she would use if she were alone. She had told him about one.

“After we wash the milk cart.”

He stared at her. Then he put the sword away.

“How much milk?”

“Enough.”

Thane made the practice rail during the noon rest, using two trestles and a piece of timber Tom had already rejected for a gate.

“It won’t stand a charge,” Benjamin said.

“It won’t have to. We can put it in the yard, where nobody needs to deliver twenty sacks through your chest.”

He set a wooden wedge beneath the short leg. Sariel had seen him make more elegant things from worse timber, but this could be carried by two people and laid flat against a wall. He made Benjamin lift one end to see how it felt.

“Before supper,” Thane said. “A little at a time. Tom can spare the boys then, and the people at the mill change work before the light goes. If we finish when we said we would, they might come again.”

Benjamin tried the weight, nodded, and helped carry it into the open patch behind the mill. Here the raised lane bent west, leaving the mill’s rear wall for the cart shed and the higher ground toward the chapel. The overflow crossing lay below them, close enough to see. Sariel could imagine being driven backward over it and disliking every step.

Elena had brought a blanket and two poles.

“No,” Benjamin said immediately.

“Yes.”

“Tonight we need people to learn where to stand.”

“And where to put someone who can’t.”

She spread the blanket on the ground. Kestra, sitting beside the shed with a writing board, lifted her feet out of its way.

“I’m supposed to be watching from here,” she said.

“What are you watching?” Elena asked.

“The crossing. The line. Anything coming behind them.”

“Can you see this corner?”

Kestra leaned forward. From her seat the mill wall hid the lower part of the approach.

“If I stand.”

“While you’re writing?”

Kestra set down the board with excessive care. “Where do you want me?”

“Come and carry the back end. Then tell me.”

Sariel volunteered to be the casualty before Benjamin could volunteer himself. She lay on the blanket with her arms folded. The earth smelled warm and full of old chaff. Thane rolled the blanket around the poles while Elena checked the distance between them.

“Comfortable?” Benjamin asked.

“Very. I might have been doing this all morning.”

“You might have stayed where you said you would.”

She looked up at him. He was smiling, but not entirely joking.

When they lifted, Kestra’s end rose first. Sariel slid toward Benjamin’s knees. He staggered backward, caught the weight, and made a noise that she was certain would be worth remembering.

“Together,” Elena said. “On the word. Not before the word because you know it’s coming.”

They lowered her. Kestra wiped her palms on her skirt. Her canal scrapes were healed enough to bear weight now, but she still disliked rubbing them against rough wood.

“Which word?” she asked.

“Lift.”

“Then don’t use it while you’re explaining.”

They laughed, set their feet, and tried again. This time Sariel stayed level. They carried her down to the crossing, turned, and attempted to bring her back up the raised lane. At the corner Benjamin walked wide while Kestra turned tightly. One pole struck the wall. Sariel’s shoulder bumped stone.

“Ow.”

“Sorry.”

“Are you actually hurt?” Elena asked.

“No. But I could become very convincing.”

They set her down. Kestra crouched to look along the corner. From this height the irregular stones at the lane’s edge were much more apparent. A person walking alone could step around them. Two people carrying Sariel could not both see them.

“I can’t stay by the shed,” she said. “I need to stand at the bend and tell the rear bearer when the front is clear.”

“Will you see the crossing?” Benjamin asked.

“Enough of it. Someone else can tell me what is happening beyond the mill.”

Sariel rolled her head to look at Thane. He was already measuring the width with his hands.

“Don’t rebuild the village,” she said.

“I was thinking of moving a stone.”

“Ambitious.”

He found a bar. She got up to help, and Elena made her lie down again until they had carried her all the way to the shed. By the time they reached it, Sariel was hot, thirsty, and unexpectedly frightened by how little she could do while other people held her above the ground.

She sat on the blanket while Kestra fetched water.

“Did you know it was going to hit?” Benjamin asked her.

“The wall? Yes.”

“Why didn’t you say?”

“I thought you knew.”

He rested his hands on his knees. Sweat ran into the collar of his shirt. “I couldn’t see your end.”

She accepted the water and passed it to him before drinking.

Before supper, eleven people came.

Only seven had been asked. The others wanted to see whether somebody would fall down again. Tom brought a stool and announced he was too old to be instructed standing. Linett told him he was not too old to carry it back afterward. Halen came with his injured face yellowing at the edges, but Elena pointed him firmly to a place beside her. He could watch and call an instruction. He could not volunteer to demonstrate that he had recovered.

Benjamin put away his sword. He stood beside the practice rail with a short stick and asked three people to hold the first position. Three more waited behind them. Everyone else was to watch the route.

“We’re going to leave it,” he said.

Tom looked pleased. “Excellent. Supper.”

“After we stop Sariel getting through.”

“That’ll make it a late supper.”

Sariel bowed to him. Benjamin gave her a look, and she stayed in front of the rail this time.

They tried a short advance. She pressed the padded end of her pole against the nearest defender’s and felt him meet it without reaching too far. His neighbor remained close enough to help. When she moved toward the end, a person from the second group came forward. There was no clever thing to do. She could strike hard, risk hurting someone, and prove nothing. She let herself be pushed back.

The man grinned, astonished.

“Don’t look at me,” Benjamin said. “Look at her.”

Sariel came in again while he was still smiling. This time he saw her.

They changed places. Benjamin made everyone take a turn at the back, including the two men most determined to remain in front. Then Halen called the withdrawal. It was only a word at first, spoken so softly that Tom asked whether anyone had heard the boy propose marriage. Halen tried again. The three at the rail stepped back together while the waiting group covered them.

One retreated too quickly and struck Benjamin’s foot. Another forgot his neighbor and drifted sideways. They stopped, moved the rail back into place, and did it once more. The second retreat brought them to the crossing without anyone turning his back on Sariel.

She followed, testing the distance. They were awkward, but she could no longer put her pole against one person’s chest without another person’s pole reaching her. She had to work to make the point, and she liked them for that.

Elena sent in her casualty.

Thane lay down with such enthusiasm that Tom accused him of practicing for his ordinary employment. Two villagers brought the blanket poles. Kestra stood at the bend and waited until they could hear her before telling them which end to move. Benjamin kept the rail between Sariel and the bearers. A woman who had missed the morning’s disaster saw the space closing and stepped into it without being asked.

Thane arrived at the shed with all his parts where they had begun.

“Remarkable,” Tom said. “Try again with something worth saving.”

Thane lifted one hand from the blanket and made a rude gesture. Linett laughed so hard that she had to sit on Tom’s stool.

They did it again. Then Benjamin stopped while everyone still wanted another attempt. He asked for the same short time tomorrow and helped put the rail against the wall. Sariel watched people go toward supper carrying their own poles. Halen stayed to ask whether the withdrawal could have a whistle as well as a word. His face still hurt when he raised his voice.

“Yes,” Benjamin said. “Show me what you can manage.”

Halen tried. The sound was thin but clear. They spent several minutes making it better.

Sariel found Benjamin at the bakery door after supper, holding two pieces of oat cake wrapped in a cloth.

“Stolen?” she asked.

“Mira gave them to me.”

“Then we should check whether she expects the roof repaired.”

He handed her a piece. It was still warm in the middle. They walked out of the square and took the lane toward the orchard, slowly enough to eat without dropping crumbs down their clothes. A little girl carrying a basin overtook them and told Benjamin he should have let the milk woman join the line. He thanked her for the advice with complete seriousness.

At the orchard wall he stopped to remove a pebble from his shoe. Sariel leaned beside him and looked through the branches at the pale sky. Someone had gathered the fallen fruit into baskets beneath the trees. The smell was sweet, with the sharpness of bruised skins.

“You were good tonight,” she said.

He looked up from his shoe. “So were they.”

“That doesn’t stop you being good.”

“No.” He smiled. “I liked it when she moved without waiting for me.”

“The woman by the rail?”

“Yes. I’d forgotten the space. She hadn’t.”

Sariel broke the last of her cake in two and gave him half. He took it without saying he had already eaten his own. They went on between the orchard and the chapel wall. There was nowhere urgent to reach. After a while he asked whether she had always known how to walk without making a sound.

“I make plenty of sound.”

“When you want something.”

“Usually supper.”

He laughed. She showed him how the edge of the path took a foot more quietly than its gritty middle. He tried, placing his feet with exaggerated care, and trod on a dry twig.

“That one was waiting for you,” she said.

He tried again. At the next tree she stopped and let him come up beside her. He was looking at the ground so intently that he almost touched her shoulder before noticing she had stopped.

“What?”

“Nothing. You did it.”

“Oh.” His pleasure was immediate. “I did.”

They stood smiling at each other in the gathering dusk. Sariel could hear a cow calling somewhere beyond the wall and the faint clatter of plates from a house below. Benjamin held out his hand, not quite touching hers. She put her hand in it.

On the way back they walked in the middle of the lane. He told her about a watchman who had spent a whole winter claiming to understand horses and had finally been bitten by a wooden one outside a saddler’s shop. She did not believe the ending. He insisted that Orren had known the man, which proved nothing except that Orren had enjoyed the story too.

They were still arguing about the horse when they reached the bakery. Mira opened the door before either could knock and told them they could finish being foolish inside, where she was trying to keep the warmth.

Chapter 14 Chalk in the rain

Kestra recognized Mareth by the way they waited for someone to finish speaking.

She had been looking at the wrong face. The man beside the bakery door wore a traveling coat and carried a staff, and for a moment she thought the coat belonged to the person who had pulled her from the canal. Then he turned, revealing an entirely unfamiliar beard. Behind him a smaller figure stood with a bundle beneath one arm, listening to Mira explain why no traveler needed to strike a cake with a knuckle before buying it.

Mareth waited until the explanation was complete.

“I was wondering whether it would survive my bag.”

“Your bag can learn some manners.”

Kestra put down the cup she was carrying. It missed the ledge and broke.

Mareth looked toward the sound. Their face changed.

She went to them without deciding what she would say. Their bundle slid between them when she embraced them, something hard knocking her ribs. She held on anyway. Mareth put an arm around her back and said her name, which made everything worse for several breaths.

“You’re dry,” they said at last.

She began laughing while she was still crying. “Mostly.”

Mira picked up the bundle before it fell into the street. She took the cake from the stranger’s hand, wrapped it, and sent him away with change he had stopped asking for. Then she brought Mareth and Kestra inside.

The others came in separately, summoned by people who had seen Kestra drop a cup and assumed something worth knowing must have happened. Benjamin embraced Mareth so hard that they complained about the condition of their spine. Elena made them sit. Sariel stood by the table with both hands against its edge until Mareth held one out to her.

They ate the cake. Mareth said Finn’s absence had frightened them before anyone explained it. Zara told the story of the fox settling into Alderfold as though it had employed the keeper. Tavia corrected two details, and for several minutes they could all speak about something that had ended well.

Then Kestra asked about the canal.

The room grew quiet enough that she heard a knife scrape the board in the kitchen.

“After we went,” she said. “Did anyone else come?”

Mareth put down their cup.

“I went back after I left you at the towpath. No one came while I could still reach that landing.”

“How long?”

“Until the approach was watched. Long enough to know I couldn’t stay and still be useful to anyone coming out.”

“Did you see the house fall?”

“No.”

“Did you hear—”

“Kestra.”

They spoke gently. She hated that for an instant. She had written a page of questions after they lost Mareth on the road, and more after reaching Millhaven. She had thought that another witness would give her something to put into the gaps.

“I know where I stood,” Mareth said. “I know which way you came. I know your bag caught, and you were reaching for the rope. I could get hold of your coat. Finn found the case afterward. I can’t tell you what happened inside the house.”

Benjamin looked at the table. Sariel touched the edge of Hest’s case through her pocket.

“Who sent you?” Kestra asked.

“Someone carrying the Coin’s request for a way through. I agreed to help with the passage. I did not know all the people who would need it.”

“Would you have stayed if you had?”

Mareth met her eyes. “I don’t know what you’re asking me to change.”

She looked away first. Her broken cup had been swept into a little pile by the door. Mira had set its handle on top of the larger pieces, as though some use might still be found for it.

After a while Kestra asked whether Mareth would stay in Millhaven.

“For a few days. I have work to do at the waymarks west of here.”

“What kind of work?”

“The kind that requires a brush. Will you come?”

She nodded before asking what she would be brushing.

The stone lay on its side beneath a tangle of bramble. Somebody had used its flat face to steady a fence post, and the post had fallen with it. The carved road on the upper surface ended in packed soil.

Kestra brought paper, charcoal, two books borrowed from Thorne, and the saved scroll. Mareth brought a spade.

They looked at her books. She looked at the spade.

“You said a waymark.”

“It is.”

“I thought we would study it.”

“We will see more when we can see it.”

They gave her the brush.

By midday she had mud beneath every nail and had learned that a bramble could catch the back of a collar without appearing to move. Mareth freed her twice without comment. The third time they began laughing, and she joined them because the alternative was to kick the stone.

They worked it upright using a wooden lever borrowed from the orchard keeper. Beneath it lay a little hollow filled with pale roots. Kestra stared at the roots while Mareth packed earth around the base.

“Was it doing anything down there?”

“Keeping a fence post from sinking, for a while.”

“You know what I mean.”

“I don’t know whether anyone asked anything else of it.”

When the stone stood, they ate bread in its narrow shade. Kestra had brought a pear and gave Mareth half. They examined her copy of the inscription, turned the paper, and asked her where the road went.

“West.”

“To what?”

“The chapel road joins the old track beyond the orchard.”

“Have you walked it?”

“No.”

“Then show me what you do know.”

She pointed back toward the village, impatient now. “The way we came.”

“Describe it.”

She named the gate, the low wall, the turn where the pear trees ended. Mareth listened. When she finished, they took a small bronze disc from inside their coat. A branching road had been cut into its face. Its blue wool cord was frayed near the knot, and the bronze was warm when they laid it in her palm.

“This is consecrated to the Traveler. You may borrow it for the lessons.”

She held it by the edges, careful not to smudge what could not be smudged.

“What does it do?”

“What you learn to ask through it. Today, one small thing.”

Mareth drew a mark with chalk on the sheltered side of the stone. They showed her the order of its strokes, then had her copy it on a flat shard. Her first version was neater than theirs.

“No,” they said.

She examined it. “The branches are equal.”

“They aren’t meant to be equal.”

“The inscription—”

“Where are you sending someone?”

She looked toward the gate. The larger branch of her mark pointed toward a broken length of wall. She had copied the shape as it lay on Mareth’s hand, not as it sat beside the actual path.

“Oh.”

“Yes.”

She rubbed it out. The second version was less beautiful and faced the right way.

They walked the path to the gate, marking the starting stone, the turn, and the endpoint. Mareth made her count her ordinary paces. She tried to lengthen the last few and received a look that reminded her painfully of several teachers she had disliked.

“One hundred and twenty is as far as I want you attempting this,” they said. “Not one hundred and twenty strides arranged to impress me.”

“I wasn’t—”

“You were.”

She laughed. “A little.”

“Tell me if you want to attempt a longer road. We will begin with why.”

At the starting stone they showed her the invocation. It was not a song. The words named departure, the first turn, and arrival, with a pause in which she had to retain the actual stretch between them. Her old studies helped her hold the relation of one mark to another. They did not let her substitute the picture in her mind for the muddy path they had walked.

She spoke too quickly. Nothing happened.

She tried again with the same speed and a louder voice. A woman working among the pear trees looked over the wall.

Mareth waited.

“I know,” Kestra said.

“Then take your time.”

The third attempt lasted a full minute. At its end the disc warmed in her palm. She looked toward the turn and saw a pale edge lying along the path, thin as light beneath a door.

She took one delighted step.

The edge stopped short of the turn.

She stopped too.

“Which end did you name?” Mareth asked.

“The gate.”

“Which gate?”

Her mind supplied the gate to the orchard, standing to the left of the path. She had meant the gate beside the lane, farther on. Both had figured in her description. She had not noticed when one replaced the other.

The light reached no farther. Beyond it, the chalked route continued to the other gate.

Rain came that night and continued through the morning.

Kestra went to the waymark early, carrying her books beneath her coat. Two of her chalk marks had washed away. The one under the stone’s lip survived, pointing cheerfully toward an instruction that no longer existed beyond it.

Mareth found her crouching in the mud.

“You came without me.”

“I wanted to see whether it would still work.”

“And?”

“It shouldn’t.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

She showed them her notes. She had written the exact places, corrected the confusion over the two gates, and copied the sequence of words without embellishment. She had also drawn a small roof over each mark.

Mareth considered the roofs. “What would you make them from?”

“Broken pots. The orchard keeper has a pile.”

“Ask before taking them.”

“I did.”

They smiled. It warmed her more than the rain had allowed anything else to do all morning.

Thane came to help fit the fragments. He was supposed to be repairing a barrow and brought its wheel with him, rolling it down the lane with a stick. He claimed that moving it this way counted as an inspection. Kestra doubted this, but the wheel traveled very straight.

He bored little holes through two pot fragments and fixed them above the exposed marks with wooden pegs. At the gate they found a natural hollow in the post’s side. Kestra made the mark within it, where the rain could not reach directly.

“Will it stop working if somebody moves the post?” Thane asked.

“If the route changes, I must walk it again.”

“How much?”

“I don’t know.”

He nodded, pleased rather than disappointed. “We can find out before anyone needs it.”

They did not move the post. They moved a barrow across the path and discovered that a shining direction could lead a person directly into a wheel. Thane touched it with his boot, looked at Kestra, and spread both hands.

“The barrow is still a barrow,” she said.

“I’m reassured.”

She wrote that down, though in different words. Mareth watched without rescuing her from any of Thane’s questions.

The invocation took longer when she was cold. Halfway through she lost the endpoint and found herself thinking about the water entering her shoe. She stopped. Mareth told her she had been right to stop, and sent her to warm her feet before trying again.

She objected until she stood and discovered that she could scarcely feel her toes.

They ate in the bakery kitchen. Thane put the wheel beside the door and was told that if he intended to bring livestock next he should send advance notice. Mira gave Kestra dry stockings and required her to return them without magic. Kestra was too hungry to find an answer.

She practiced the order of the path by naming the things between her chair and the bread shelf. When she came to the turn around the table, Mira put a stool in her way.

“Still a stool,” Thane observed.

Kestra threw a crust at him. He caught it, ate it, and looked smug for the rest of the meal.

For four days she went back.

The work became familiar before it became easy. She could clean a mark without erasing its useful edge. She learned which chalk stayed on damp stone and which became paste. She stopped making all her paces the same size when the path itself required a shorter step. Mareth made her walk both ways before preparing the direction she wanted, so that she knew what a traveler would see and what she had mistaken for obvious.

They borrowed Vennit’s small sandglass. Kestra timed the preparation, including the walk, at roughly ten minutes when nothing needed repair. The invocation took another quiet minute. The working lasted about one turn of the glass, ten minutes during which the marks gave a willing traveler the pale edge she had first seen. Afterward the chalk became chalk again.

She could renew it with another minute at the start if the marks remained sound and she still knew the path from that day’s walk. She could not renew it from her bed because she remembered yesterday’s weather. Mareth let her argue that point until she heard how much of the argument depended on wishing she need not go outside.

On the fifth morning mist lay along the orchard wall. Kestra could see the starting stone and the first tree, then nothing beyond it but a wet brightness.

Thane arrived with the barrow. Its repaired wheel turned without wobbling. He had loaded it with stones because, he said, stones rarely complained about being used in experiments.

“Where’s Mareth?”

“At the other end.”

“Then if I vanish, at least there will be a witness.”

“You won’t vanish.”

“That’s the spirit. Reckless promises before breakfast.”

She caught herself smiling too broadly to answer. He saw and smiled back.

Kestra walked the route once more. The marks were intact. No branch had fallen, no animal had put a hoof through the soft edge beside the gate. She returned, set the sandglass on the wall, and held the bronze disc against the starting mark.

This time she did not think of the letters. She thought of the stone beneath her shoe, the slight slope, the place where the wall leaned toward the path. She named the turn without seeing any other turn. When she came to arrival, the gate stood in her mind with its lower hinge crooked and a bit of red cloth caught on its thorn.

The disc warmed.

A pale seam opened along the path and passed into the mist.

Thane bent over it. “I can see that.”

“Good.”

“Can you?”

“Yes.”

“Then stop looking as though it’s done something without permission.”

She laughed. He took the barrow handles and went forward. At the turn he slowed, then vanished into the whiteness. She could hear the wheel. It rattled over a little stone, fell quiet, and rattled again farther away.

Mareth called from the gate.

“He’s here.”

Kestra put both hands over her mouth. She had expected it to work; she had prepared it to work; none of that prevented the immense, foolish happiness which made her want to run after him and ask whether he had seen exactly what she had seen.

She picked up the sandglass and ran, carefully around the barrow when she reached it. Thane set down the handles so she could embrace him without knocking them both into the hedge. Mareth laughed and caught the sandglass she had nearly dropped.

“I did it,” she said.

“You did,” Thane said.

She held his coat for another moment, smiling against it. Then she turned to Mareth, who drew her into an embrace of their own.

They stood together until the mist began to lift.

Thane wanted to try from the other direction.

Kestra looked at the sandglass. “There’s time left.”

He turned from the gate toward the starting stone and frowned. To him, approaching from the endpoint, the path was merely a path. When he circled back to the starting stone and entered as intended, the seam appeared again.

“One direction,” she said, opening her notebook.

“Can you make another?”

“Yes,” Mareth said. “And the first will cease when the second takes. She has one working to hold through that symbol.”

Kestra looked at the disc. It lay quietly against her palm, doing nothing to display the thing she had accomplished.

“One is enough for today,” she said.

Thane took the barrow toward the village. He had promised to bring the stones to Tom and meant to arrive before anyone thought to charge him for borrowing them. Mareth stayed while Kestra checked each mark and put away the glass.

“You can keep practicing with it,” they said.

“For how long?”

“While I’m here, certainly. Before I leave we will make a proper arrangement. It belongs to the Traveler’s house at Three Oaks waystation. I am answerable for where it goes.”

She touched the frayed cord. “May I mend this?”

“Please.”

At the bakery she found a length of blue wool in Mira’s basket and exchanged it for the work of sorting a jar of mixed buttons. She sat by the window, making the new knot slowly. Outside, Thane stopped Benjamin in the square and pointed back toward the orchard. Benjamin looked up, saw her, and raised both hands in celebration.

Kestra waved the disc at him. The woman buying bread behind him asked what it was, and he began explaining with such large gestures that Kestra laughed until she could not finish the knot.

Chapter 15 An invitation without a patient

The clean cloth was in the wrong basket.

Elena knew this because the basket she reached for contained onions. She had put it beneath Vennit’s table herself, after moving the cloth to make room for a boy who refused to sit anywhere he could see the knife. Now Halen was bleeding into a folded apron, his mother was asking whether she ought to hold him, and Elena had an onion in her hand.

Vennit looked at it.

“Later,” she said.

Elena put it down. The boy on the stool began laughing and immediately stopped, frightened that he had done something wrong. Halen laughed too, then winced because the bruising along his jaw still hurt.

“Good to know I’m not your worst patient,” he said.

“You’re not even the patient I told to come today.”

“I caught the edge on a sack.”

“Doing what?”

He looked at his mother. She looked at Elena with the expression of someone who had asked precisely the same question before leaving home.

Vennit took the clean cloth from the shelf behind Elena. She handed it over without speaking. Halen’s cut was small, reopened where the collector’s blow had split the skin near his temple. Elena cleaned the place while he watched the boy on the stool try to conceal a splintered finger inside his shirt.

“Does it hurt?” the boy asked.

“Yes,” Halen said.

The finger disappeared farther.

“Not enough to make the onions worthwhile.”

Elena laughed. Her hand steadied as she did. When she had finished, she asked Halen to tell her what work he had been doing and listened through the explanation of how lifting an empty sack became lifting a nearly empty sack, which became helping with one that somebody else had already begun.

“You could call the drill again,” she said. “You did that well.”

“They don’t drill all day.”

“No. You could sit with your mother and repair sacks.”

“I can do that at home,” his mother said.

He gave both women a betrayed look. Elena almost promised him another task, then remembered the people she had already promised to visit. She would not have time to invent a useful occupation for everyone who disliked resting.

“Ask Thane whether he needs someone to sort fittings. Tell him I said no lifting.”

“He’ll give me the rusty ones.”

“Then you’ll be doing him a kindness.”

After Halen left, Vennit treated the boy’s finger. She showed him the splinter in the point of her tweezers and let him put it into an empty pot himself. He wanted to keep it. His father said that was a ridiculous thing to want, and Vennit gave him the pot.

When the door closed, Elena began sorting the baskets.

Vennit washed her hands. “What will you reach for first?”

“Cloth.”

“Which kind?”

“Clean. Dry.”

“And after you’ve used that?”

Elena looked at the table. They had worked around each other all morning, moving bowls, asking for things, putting them where the other would think to look. Sometimes that had succeeded by familiarity, sometimes because Vennit remembered where Elena had put a thing Elena herself had forgotten.

She cleared a space at the near end.

“Used cloth here. Clean on the shelf. The small basket for dressings already cut.”

“Where can a person reach them while holding pressure with one hand?”

Elena moved the basket closer.

Vennit nodded and began peeling an onion for their dinner.

At the week’s review, Thorne renewed the room and meal arrangement through the days remaining before Garrick’s return. The woman with the milk cart asked whether the drills would continue at supper time. Benjamin said they would. She nodded and withdrew the complaint she had brought folded in her apron. Linett kept the forge accounts separate, and Mira said extra mouths still meant extra work. Elena left with another turn at washing the bakery pans and the knowledge that their key would remain theirs to use.

They spent the next afternoon preparing what could travel.

Elena had thought replenishment meant replacing the things she had used since the fire. Vennit brought out three wooden splints of different lengths, a roll of soft padding, and a pair of shears that could cut a folded sleeve without chewing its edges. She set them beside Elena’s little jars.

“Will you lend these?”

“For the work you’re proposing.”

Elena picked up the shears. The handles fitted her fingers properly. She opened and closed them once, then tried them on a scrap. The cut was so clean that she did it again merely for the pleasure of seeing the two halves fall apart.

“They belonged to my teacher,” Vennit said.

Elena stopped.

“You may use them. She bought them to cut cloth.”

“I’ll keep them dry.”

“Good. What would you use instead if they broke?”

Elena showed her the small knife from the Coin’s kit. Vennit inspected its edge and sent her to Tom with it.

At the forge, Linett took the knife and asked what else needed doing before Elena could escape. She had a slate on the bench, with jobs written in a hand large enough to be read by a man determined not to look closely.

“Nothing urgent.”

“Then tell me the unurgent things before they become urgent.”

Elena admitted that one flask leaked at its stopper and the medicine bag’s fastening sometimes came free. Linett wrote both down. From behind the forge came a heated disagreement about the proper shape of a chicken’s leg.

Elena looked toward the noise.

“They’re making a toy,” Linett said. “Apparently no bird has ever managed to walk before.”

Thane appeared carrying a little wooden hen with two wheels beneath it. Its head moved when he pushed it. One wing had been cut from thin tin and the other was still a piece of penciled wood.

“Is this how a chicken bends?” he asked.

Elena considered the mechanism. “I spend very little time treating chickens.”

“It’s a joint.”

“It’s a piece of wire.”

Tom came after him, wiping his hands. “That’s what I said.”

“You said nobody would notice.”

“Nobody who’d paid for a toy hen.”

Linett took the bird and rolled it along the bench. Its head dipped twice, then stuck with its beak against the wood.

“Hungry,” she said. “You’ve got that part right.”

They all laughed except Thane, who studied the stuck head until Tom put a hand on his shoulder and laughed directly at him. Thane gave up and joined them.

Elena stayed while Tom sharpened the knife. He let her try the edge and showed her the stone he had used, a narrow piece she could carry without regretting its weight. Linett found a spare leather fastening in a drawer. Thane insisted on fixing it to the medicine bag himself, saying he needed a task with fewer opinions attached.

When Elena returned to the cottage, Vennit had cut a stack of square pads. She had put a little blue thread into the corner of the cloth used for washing, so nobody would confuse it with the dry dressings under a poor lamp. Elena sat beside her and began rolling bandages.

They made six rolls, long enough to be useful and narrow enough to handle with wet fingers. Twelve folded pads went into a waxed wrapper. The splints were tied together with their padding. Two flasks would hold water, one for drinking and one for cleaning; the necks felt different in the hand, and Elena tied a cord around the cleaning flask so a stranger could distinguish them in the dark.

Vennit brought a brown bottle from the high shelf.

“For pain. You know the preparation?”

“Yes.”

“Tell me.”

Elena did. Vennit corrected the strength she was assuming, then made her write the directions on a piece of wood attached to its neck. The bottle stayed in its own padded pocket. Nothing else was to go into that pocket, however convenient the space appeared.

By the time they finished, Elena could put a hand on what she needed without looking. She closed her eyes and tried it while Vennit named things. Cloth. Shears. Padding. The brown bottle. A clean needle in its case.

“Food,” Vennit said.

Elena opened her eyes.

“For you.”

“There isn’t room.”

Vennit waited.

Elena took out one of the jars she had carried from Silkstrand because it had once been expensive. It contained a little powder she had not used on the road and could not imagine needing at the mill. She set it safely on the shelf and put bread in the space.

Mira came to the cottage while Elena was helping Vennit fold the last cloth.

“Supper,” she said.

Elena put down the cloth. “Who?”

Mira blinked. “Us, I hope.”

“Who’s ill?”

“Nobody I invited.”

Vennit turned toward the shelf. Elena suspected she was smiling.

Mira took a little leaf out of Elena’s hair. It had come from the padding they had shaken in the yard.

“Come when the lamps are lit. Bring yourself. If you bring the bag, put it in the room first.”

“I should check Halen.”

“He was sorting rusty things at the forge. He complained quite healthily.”

“Vennit might need—”

“I will send if I need you,” Vennit said.

Elena looked between them, embarrassed by how pleased she was. “What should I bring?”

“A story,” Mira said. “Or an appetite. Something I haven’t already baked.”

After she left, Elena tried to finish the cloths too quickly. Vennit took a badly folded pad from her and opened it again.

“Go and wash.”

“There are only four left.”

“I can count.”

Elena went to the lodging and found Tavia sitting with her right sleeve rolled above the healed burn. She was trying to arrange her hair using a mirror small enough that she could see only one eye at a time.

“Will you?” she asked, holding out the comb.

Elena stood behind her and drew it through carefully. The burn had left a tender patch which disliked the seam of Tavia’s best sleeve. Elena had become so accustomed to looking at it that she almost asked to examine it again. Tavia watched her in the little mirror.

“It’s behaving.”

“I wasn’t going to ask.”

“You were going to think loudly.”

Elena smiled. She divided the hair and began a braid. Tavia closed her eyes, content to have someone else do the work.

“Do you miss having a room?” Elena asked.

“We have a room.”

“One you can close.”

“Yes.”

“So do I.”

Tavia opened her eyes. “I miss putting a thing down and knowing who moved it.”

“I’d like a cupboard no one else uses.”

“For your medicines?”

“For my stockings.”

Tavia laughed, an open delighted sound. “You should demand that in the next agreement.”

Elena tied the braid and let it fall over Tavia’s shoulder. “There.”

“Would you ever take a practice? Somewhere with a proper room?”

Elena hesitated. She had imagined one often enough that she knew where the window ought to be. She did not like saying it aloud in a borrowed room while people who could not afford treatment waited at Vennit’s door.

“I’d like to.”

“Then you should.”

“With money, perhaps.”

“Yes, that tiresome part.” Tavia stood. “You’d need someone to keep the appointments from becoming promises you couldn’t possibly keep.”

“I know someone very good with papers.”

Tavia’s smile changed a little. “You do.”

They went downstairs together, leaving the medicine bag on its hook.

The table would not hold everyone until Benjamin moved the bench.

He had repaired its loose leg that afternoon. Mira had warned him that it leaned, and he had taken this as an invitation to improve it. Now he lifted one end and the leg remained on the floor.

For a moment he stood holding the bench as though waiting for it to admit responsibility.

Thane considered the leg on the floor.

“The other end seems sound,” he offered.

“Thank you,” Benjamin said. “I did nothing to that end.”
