Tom picked up the leg. A fresh split ran from the new wedge to the edge of the socket. He looked at it, looked at Benjamin, and handed it to Linett.

“Interesting work.”

Benjamin put the bench down. “I asked whether the wedge was big enough.”

“You didn’t ask whether it belonged there,” Linett said.

He began laughing then, bending over the failed joint with one hand against the table. Sariel caught the bread plate before his elbow reached it. Thane went to fetch his tools, and Benjamin went with him. When they returned, Thane made him do the work while explaining it. Nobody had to stand on ceremony around the broken bench; Mira set the bowls on the table and people ate while the repair continued beneath their knees.

Elena sat between Tavia and Linett. The stew was thick with beans and pieces of smoked meat. Mira had made small round loaves which could be broken without a knife, their crusts floury and hot. Elena ate the first piece too quickly and had to sit with her mouth open until it cooled. Linett did the same a moment later and they looked at each other helplessly.

“The polite way,” Linett whispered.

“Very refined.”

Across the table, Kestra was trying to explain her new working to Zara using three peas and a crumb of bread. Zara kept eating the endpoints. Mareth watched from the far end, making no attempt to assist either of them.

The repaired bench returned to service. Benjamin lowered himself onto it with great care. It held. Everyone applauded, and he bowed from the waist without standing, which produced another small creak.

“Enough,” Mira said. “If you all break it together, I haven’t that many chairs.”

The toy hen appeared after the bowls were cleared. Thane had finished the second wing. Tom had altered the little wooden cam beneath its neck. Rolled across the table, it pecked three times, lifted its head, and waggled its tail.

Elena laughed. It was a ridiculous little bird and unmistakably pleased with what it was doing. Linett sent it back toward Tom, who stopped it short of the gravy.

“Who is it for?” Elena asked.

“The neighbor’s little one,” Linett said. “She’s been waiting since spring.”

“It needed proper consideration,” Tom said.

“It needed somebody to tell you there was a birthday tomorrow.”

Thane rolled it again, slower this time, watching the movement with undisguised pleasure. Elena watched his hands. She had seen those hands mending straps, freeing wheels, working at things that had to be fixed before anyone could continue. Here they had made a chicken that pecked at an empty table. She wanted him to have a room full of such useless things.

When Mira asked what she was smiling at, Elena told her.

“Not useless,” Mira said. “Look at him.”

Thane had caught Tom’s sleeve and was showing him a tiny improvement he could make to the tail. Tom listened, nodded, and suggested something that made Thane laugh.

After supper Elena helped wash the cups because she wanted to remain in the kitchen.

Tavia dried. Through the doorway they could hear Benjamin and Sariel arguing about whether the toy’s walking noise should be included in its price. Mira sat beside the warm oven, her shoes off, rolling one foot over a little wooden ball. She had flour in a crease by her ear.

“I’d like a room with a window facing east,” Elena said.

Neither woman pretended not to know what she meant.

“For the light?” Mira asked.

“For mornings. And a table I could walk all the way around. A shelf higher than a child can reach, but not so high I need a stool every time.”

“What would it smell of?” Tavia asked.

“Clean linen. Whatever I was cooking in the other room.”

“An excellent other room,” Mira said.

Elena washed the next cup slowly. “I used to imagine a practice with nothing else in it. As if I would just stand there after the last patient left.”

“You’d need to sit down eventually,” Mira said.

“I suppose I could employ a chair.”

“Reliable things, if Benjamin hasn’t trained them.”

They laughed. Elena set the cup on the rack and let herself imagine someone coming home through the other door, asking what had happened that day without needing to be treated for it.

Tavia put down the drying cloth.

“I want letters that end,” she said. “A person asks for a copy, I make it, they pay me. Or I manage their accounts for a season and then choose whether to stay. No one deciding that because I know a thing I belong to the person who told it to me.”

Elena looked at her.

“Here?”

“Perhaps. Somewhere. Tema may know of work when his caravan comes through.”

Elena had heard Zara mention the merchant. She had thought of salt, grain, and the things Millhaven lacked. She had not thought of her friends leaving with a wagon.

“That would be good,” she said, and found that she meant it, though the thought hurt in a place she could not yet name.

Tavia took her hand out of the washing water and dried it with the cloth.

“You could write to me about the chair.”

“I’d need you to keep its accounts.”

“My rates would be terrible.”

“I know.”

Mira stretched her feet toward the oven. “If you two are finished arranging the rest of your lives, there’s another little loaf under that cloth.”

They found it, divided it into three, and ate standing beside the clean cups. Elena did not go back to the wool store for the bag until it was time to sleep.

Chapter 16 The Baroness offers help

The soldiers waited while a woman finished buying turnips.

Kestra noticed because Benjamin noticed. He stood beside the well with a bucket in each hand, watching the mounted men draw aside to let the woman pass. Their horses were well fed. The men’s coats were mended where they needed mending, their boots clean enough to show that someone had taken time over them. One dismounted to retrieve a turnip which had rolled beneath his horse’s feet.

The woman thanked him. He handed it back and asked where he might water the animal.

Nothing in the exchange looked like Garrick’s collectors.

The envoy came on foot behind them, having left a small covered carriage at the edge of the square. He wore a traveling coat with a velvet collar and carried his gloves in one hand. His other hand supported a basket. He asked for Elder Thorne, then saw Benjamin’s buckets and offered to wait until the water had been carried.

Benjamin set one down. “I can fetch him.”

“Thank you.”

Kestra lifted the abandoned bucket before anyone could make that a discussion too. It was fuller than she expected. The envoy put the basket on the well ledge and helped her clear the rim.

“For the bakery?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“I was told I should bring these there first.”

He lifted the cloth. Beneath it lay two rounds of cheese and a little parcel of salt. Good things, plainly meant to be eaten. Mira came out at the sound of her trade being discussed and accepted the basket after asking who had sent it.

“The Baroness’s household. Her steward remembered your cheese supplier was among the people delayed at the north road.”

Mira’s expression changed. “He was.”

“We hope to make that road useful again.”

He let the sentence rest. Kestra carried the water inside and found herself wishing he had been rude.

They laid the proposal on the wool store’s long table because it was the room with the most benches.

The villagers came with work in their hands. Linett brought her slate. Halen’s mother had a sack to mend. A man from the outlying fields stood near the door, his face and the back of his neck burned different colors by the sun. He had walked in early because he wanted to hear an offer of protection before anyone decided it on his behalf.

Thorne offered the envoy a chair and remained standing until everyone who wanted to sit had a place. The seven newcomers stood together at first, then separated as neighbors made room. Kestra sat beside Tavia. She had the old market papers beneath her board and Mareth’s disc safely inside her coat. She could feel the new cord at the back of her neck, an oddly comforting reminder that she had learned one thing recently which she could actually do.

The envoy opened with food.

Twenty sacks from the Baroness’s stores could be delivered within four days of acceptance. Eight paid soldiers would establish a watch on the north approach and remain through the threatened collection. Their provisions for the first six weeks would come from the household. A patrol would accompany ordinary traffic along the mill road twice each week. The Baroness would recognize Thorne as the village’s elder and preserve the use of its chapel and common ground.

The field worker took off his cap. Several people began speaking at once.

“Twenty sacks of what?” Mira asked.

“Mixed grain, principally barley. The schedule gives the proportions.”

“Sound?”

“Inspected before departure. You may refuse spoiled grain on arrival.”

“Who decides spoiled?”

“Your miller and our quartermaster together.”

“If they disagree?”

The envoy considered. “Set the disputed sack aside and send for a second inspection. I can add that.”

He drew the paper toward him and wrote in the margin. Kestra watched the point of his pen. It was easy to dislike an offer that concealed its conditions. This one let Mira improve it.

The field worker asked whether the soldiers would protect houses outside the square.

“They would patrol the road and answer alarms.”

“How far?”

The envoy requested a description of the house. He listened to the turns, asked about the orchard, and said the patrol could include it if the path remained passable. He did not promise to put a soldier at every door. Kestra saw the man weigh that answer and find it better than what he had now.

Then Tavia asked what acceptance required.

The envoy turned the first sheet over. “An oath of fealty, recognition of the Baroness’s jurisdiction over the mill road, and the accustomed contribution toward its maintenance.”

“Whose accustomed contribution?” Tavia asked.

He smiled faintly. “The schedule is on the next sheet.”

They read it together. Kestra’s eyes went first to the grain figures and then to the small paragraph beneath them. One measure in twelve of grain milled for sale would be rendered to the household. Loaded carts using the mill road would pay a passage due. Disputes involving these contributions would be heard by the Baroness’s appointed officer.

“For six weeks?” Kestra asked.

The envoy looked at her. “The soldiers’ provisions are guaranteed for six weeks.”

“The contribution.”

“Continues under the new protection.”

Tavia placed her finger beside the line. “And if the soldiers leave?”

“The obligation remains, as does the Baroness’s obligation to defend her people.”

The room had become quieter. Halen’s mother stopped sewing without taking the needle out of the cloth.

Kestra read the maintenance paragraph a third time. Tavia touched her wrist beneath the edge of the papers and pointed to the old market instrument.

Kestra found the passage about the mill road. Its spelling belonged to a time when two letters had done the work of one, but the meaning was plain enough. Millhaven had been permitted to maintain the approach and keep the proceeds of its market use, subject to an existing payment which Thorne said they still made.

The envoy examined the copy.

“I don’t dispute that it says so.”

“Then why would we pay again?” the miller asked.

“The new arrangement includes armed protection.”

“Which the old payment was supposed to include.”

“From a district watch that no longer patrols here reliably.”

She had spent an afternoon finding the old right and had felt almost as pleased as she did when the Waymark worked. The envoy could acknowledge the page without producing the absent patrol.

Tavia asked whether the existing payment would be credited against the new contribution.

“That would require the steward’s agreement.”

“Will you seek it?”

“Yes.”

“Before the village answers?”

He glanced toward the door. “If you permit a messenger to use the road.”

Thorne nodded. “No one here has stopped your messenger.”

The man from the fields put his cap back on, then took it off again.

“How much is a measure in twelve against everything burned?”

Nobody answered him immediately. Kestra wanted someone to give him a better number. The losses she could imagine were much larger than the contribution, and the question was whether this offer would prevent them.

“My children sleep beyond your alarm,” he continued. “Those men came to my house before they came here. They took the grain I had in the loft. I can pay the Baroness if it means somebody comes when I call.”

“You’d pay on the grain coming through the mill too,” Linett said. “The price comes back to you.”

“I know how prices come back.”

She inclined her head, accepting the rebuke. “I meant it would be more than what you hand over at your own door.”

“And if there’s no door?”

Mira moved the cheese plate toward him. He had not taken any. He looked at it for several breaths, then cut a piece.

The envoy waited through the exchange. Kestra could not tell whether he was pleased by it. He had not invented the man’s children or the empty loft. The soldiers outside had not beaten Halen. Their horses stamped and turned their heads in the sunshine, and a boy was being allowed to touch one under the rider’s watchful eye.

Tavia asked who would command those soldiers if a dispute arose between a household and a road officer.

Their captain, the envoy said, under orders from the Baroness’s steward.

“Would Thorne be able to dismiss a soldier for misconduct?”

“He could make a complaint.”

“And until it was heard?”

The envoy put down his pen. “You are asking whether the protection would remain yours to direct.”

“Yes.”

“No. You would be joining the Baroness’s lands. That is the protection being offered.”

Beside her, Tavia sat back.

At the pause, Tavia sent Kestra outside with the supply schedule.

“Read it somewhere you can breathe.”

“I can breathe here.”

“You’re reading the same line.”

Kestra stepped outside and stood in the shade beside the wool store. Sariel was crouched near a loose stone, tying the lace of her boot. She looked up at the paper.

“Does it say we become respectable?”

“It says the mill road does.”

“How fortunate for it.”

Kestra smiled despite herself. She showed Sariel the schedule. Twenty sacks. Cartage through a named trading house. A seal at the bottom, pressed into soft wax and then protected with a twist of paper.

Sariel’s fingers stopped on the lace.

“Turn that.”

Kestra tilted the page. The impression showed an oval containing a three-spoked wheel. The lower spoke had a small break, so neat it looked deliberate.

“Lathen and Mere,” she read.

“I’ve seen it.”

“On what?”

“A delivery. Two mornings ago, by the northern turn. One of the men who came with the collectors was beside the wagon.”

“Were they taking it?”

“He was talking to the driver. There were flour sacks and salt behind him. That mark on the sack ends.”

“Talking isn’t taking.”

Sariel looked at her. “I know.”

Kestra almost apologized. She was already imagining the moment when they carried the seal back inside and the room understood that the Baroness had sent both the threat and its remedy. It would be a satisfying moment. It might also be wrong.

“Could it be the same company selling to both?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Without the Baroness knowing?”

“Yes.” Sariel tightened the lace. “Do you want me to say I didn’t see it?”

“No. I want something that tells us what it means.”

Sariel stood. They looked at the seal together, the tiny break in the spoke holding a shadow.

“Zara will know how to ask,” Sariel said.

They found her beside the carriage, discussing harness with one of the drivers. She finished the conversation before joining them. Kestra showed her the schedule and described what Sariel had seen. Zara did not smile or exclaim. She asked which road, which morning, how many sacks, and whether the collector had touched the driver’s papers.

“He had one in his hand,” Sariel said. “I couldn’t read it.”

“Good,” Zara said.

“How is that good?”

“Someone wrote something down.”

She folded a scrap from her own book, noted the seal and the date of Sariel’s observation, and asked whether she could keep the supply schedule until the meeting resumed. Kestra remained beside her while she copied the commercial name exactly.

“You don’t think it’s enough,” Kestra said.

“I think it’s enough to spend an afternoon on.”

The second part of the meeting was worse because nobody could pretend the first part had decided it.

Some wanted the grain and soldiers at once. Others wanted a limit on the dues, a local voice in the road watch, or a way to end the arrangement after the danger passed. The envoy could alter inspection procedures and delivery dates. He could seek instructions on the old payment. He could not offer temporary fealty or let the village appoint the captain.

Thorne made him say each refusal plainly. He also made the people supporting resistance explain what they expected to eat. Benjamin described the drills, the approaches he could hold briefly, and the number of people who had agreed to practice. When the field worker asked whether that would be enough against Garrick, Benjamin said he could not promise it.

Elena spoke about the households with people who could not walk far. She asked whether acceptance guaranteed carts if they still needed to move. The envoy said he could arrange two on reasonable notice.

“Will that be written?” she asked.

“Yes.”

He wrote it. Kestra watched Elena read the addition and nod.

At last Thorne asked for five days.

The envoy said the grain could not be reserved indefinitely. Zara asked whether it had been offered elsewhere, and he admitted that he did not know. After a short exchange, he agreed to hold the offer through the fifth morning. If the village accepted then, delivery would follow within four days, subject to the road. The distinction mattered enough that Tavia made him repeat it.

They signed only the account of what had been offered and when an answer was due. Thorne kept one copy. Tavia watched the envoy close the other into his case.

Outside, the field worker was speaking to one of the soldiers. Kestra heard the name of his farm and the man’s directions to it. The soldier listened without promising he would be sent there. The woman with the turnips came back through the square carrying an empty basket and stopped to thank him again.

Kestra took the old market papers upstairs. Tavia followed with the supply schedule.

“Was I too slow?” Kestra asked.

“At what?”

“The terms. You found the questions before I did.”

“I’ve been asked to make unfair terms sound reasonable for years.”

“Did you?”

“Sometimes.” Tavia laid the pages flat. “Sometimes I persuaded someone to change them. Sometimes I told myself it wasn’t my decision. I would like my next employer to pay for a copy because the copy is accurate.”

Kestra sat beside her. Below, the carriage wheels began moving over the stones. She waited until the sound had passed before speaking about the seal.

Tavia listened, then touched the copied impression with the end of her pen.

“Ask Zara to keep what she finds.”

“I thought so.”

“And don’t write a conclusion at the top of the page before there’s anything underneath it.”

Kestra looked at her own notes. In the margin she had written the Baroness’s name and drawn a line toward Garrick’s. She rubbed the line away until only a gray smear remained.

That evening she returned the sandglass to Vennit.

The midwife was sitting outside her cottage, stripping leaves from a bundle of herbs while a neighbor talked. Kestra waited, watching a bee work along the flowers by the door. The neighbor described the offer in terms of her brother’s roof, which was unfinished, and the two sacks he had already borrowed. She had heard about the soldiers. She wanted to know whether anyone had asked where they would sleep.

“Yes,” Kestra said when Vennit looked toward her. “The vacant store rooms first. Private households only by agreement.”

The woman nodded. “That’s something.”

“It is.”

After she left, Vennit took the glass and turned it over. The sand began its small steady fall.

“Did the lesson work?”

Kestra smiled. “Yes.”

“Then tell me about that.”

She sat on the step and told her. She described Thane disappearing into the mist with the barrow, the call from the far gate, the way the pale line had remained where she put it. Vennit asked whether she could mark the short way from the crossing to the cart shed if they needed to carry people through poor light.

“Once I’ve walked it and prepared it. It won’t take them the rest of the way to the chapel.”

“Someone can walk with them after the shed.”

“Yes.”

Vennit handed her a bundle to strip. Kestra worked beside her until the glass emptied. When she went back to the lodging, Zara was at the table with Sariel and a list of carts expected at the next market. There was room beside them. Kestra put her notes down and sat.

Chapter 17 What is in the store

Roven refused to leave until Sariel put something in the cart.

“It’s a cart,” he said. “People notice when you take one out to admire the weather.”

She looked at the empty bed. “What do you usually carry?”

“What somebody pays me to carry.”

“Very helpful.”

“Scrap wood will do. We can say I’m looking for a price on it.”

She helped load the broken fence rails he had stacked behind his house. He rejected two sound lengths she put in with them. Those belonged to a woman who needed a gate repaired and might quite reasonably object to her gate being offered to Garrick’s men.

Sariel liked Roven better each time he refused to let an interesting plan interfere with his work. She had not expected to like that quality in anyone.

They left before the square became busy. She walked for the first stretch, then climbed beside him when the road widened. The mare knew the route and had strong opinions about which stones were worth stepping around. Roven let her choose until she chose a patch of grass, at which point he reminded her that she was employed.

Sariel had Hest’s case inside her coat and a knife where she could reach it. Beneath the seat lay a piece of bread, an onion, and two apples. Roven had put them there without asking what she had brought.

“Are we going to sell the wood?” she asked.

“If anyone wants to buy it.”

“And if they do?”

“We’ll have money and an empty cart. You’ll still have your eyes.”

The north road climbed gradually away from the village. They passed the mill approach, crossed the narrow overflow on its old deck, and continued between fields where people stopped working long enough to see who was traveling. Roven greeted some by name. Others he acknowledged with a lifted hand. Sariel began to understand how little disguise a different coat would provide on a road where people knew the horse.

After an hour he gave her the onion to peel. They ate with the bread as the cart moved. She asked how long the lime yard had stood unused before Garrick took it.

“Long enough that people began calling the theft of its roof a repair elsewhere.”

“Who owns it?”

“Depends who wants paying. The old burners’ family left. A factor held the ground for a debt. Then the factor’s clerk stopped coming. None of those people asked my permission when they let it rot.”

“And now?”

“Now a man with a sword asks for rent on anything that comes near it.”

Sariel wiped onion from the knife. “Do you know his face?”

“Garrick? Yes.”

She waited.

“Pale scar under the mouth, more toward the chin. Looks at what your hands are doing. His men get out of the way before he asks. Don’t decide you’ve found him because somebody shouts.”

“Was he a soldier?”

“So he says when he wants you to think he’s better than the others. His sleeve came up once and I saw an old brand. I didn’t ask him to explain it.”

“What does he say about being a soldier?”

“Told me about an officer who ordered them to hold a ford, then rode away. Garrick says he stayed until dark.”

“Was that true?”

“I wasn’t at the ford. I was delivering timber. He wanted to know which of them ought to own the crossing afterward.”

“What did you say?”

“Asked where he wanted the timber.” Roven shifted the rein away from a sore place on his palm. “He told me, then asked again.”

The mare stopped to relieve herself. Roven waited with the patience of a man whose journey had always included this possibility. Sariel looked back along the road. From here, the village had disappeared behind the fold of the ground.

The lime yard lay behind a low wall, pale dust still caught in the sheltered cracks after years of rain.

Its main gate faced south toward the road. A watch hut occupied the southeastern corner, with a little porch shaded by a patchwork roof. The store building stood along the north side, long enough to hold far more grain than Millhaven had surrendered. Its loading doors opened into the yard. At the western end rose the broken mouth of an old kiln, black within despite the light.

Roven stopped well short of the gate. A man on the porch watched them. Another lay across a stack of timber, one boot moving in time to something he was humming.

Sariel got down and pretended to inspect a cracked wheel rim. Roven called toward the gate and asked whether anyone wanted dry wood.

The man on the porch asked how dry.

“Dry enough to burn.”

“Everything burns eventually.”

“Then you won’t need it.”

Roven began turning the mare. The watcher came out before he had completed the movement.

They argued over the wood. Sariel remained beside the wheel, moving her hands when the watcher looked toward her. Beyond him, a woman emerged from the store with an empty bowl. She crossed to a low cooking shelter against the eastern wall. A man came after her carrying two more. Nobody hurried.

Roven sold three rails. Sariel helped carry them inside the main gate, keeping her eyes on the rough ends as though avoiding splinters required all her attention. The watcher’s companion did not get off the timber. The store’s near loading door stood open; the farther one had been dragged only partway back, its lower edge scraping a groove in the threshold.

Inside, she saw sacks on raised racks. Red ochre crossed the seam of one, half covered by a black wheel. She slowed by a fraction.

“Put them there,” the watcher said.

She put down the wood. Roven counted his small payment twice. The delay gave her time to see a blue thread hanging from a paper bundle inside a partition at the store’s west end.

Then they went out.

She waited until the gate had passed behind them before speaking.

“The near stack has Millhaven grain.”

“Some of it.”

“You saw?”

“I delivered those sacks to the mill before they were full. I’ve looked at them rather more than you have.”

She laughed, softly. “Good.”

At the next bend he turned east onto a track which appeared to end among alder trees. Sariel looked back at the road.

“Where?”

“The side you’d want if you didn’t wish to ask the man on the porch.”

The track followed raised gravel along the edge of wet ground. Roven stopped where it widened into a little dry turnout sheltered by coppiced hazel. An old low wall lay beyond it, with enough roof left on a wood shelter to keep rain off a seated person.

“Quarter-mile below the yard,” he said. “Give or take a wheel. The carts can turn here.”

“Can they pass farther up?”

“At the side gate. Not in the alder notch.”

He showed her. The narrow place lay where two trees leaned toward each other, their roots holding the track above a damp hollow. A loaded cart could pass. Two trying to pass together would lose a wheel or a horse, perhaps both.

“After rain?”

“Still holds. Unless you leave the gravel.”

She stepped toward the darker ground beside it. Roven caught her sleeve before her foot went down.

“That isn’t shade.”

She crouched and put a stick into it. The stick went farther than she expected. Thin water closed over the hole.

Roven released her sleeve. “You see men very well. Let me look at the ground.”

They left the mare at the turnout and walked toward the yard. Roven knew where the leaves concealed an old cart rut and where a fallen branch would take a person’s weight. Sariel knew when to stop because a voice had changed direction beyond the wall. They moved more quietly together after the first few minutes, each waiting for the other to see what mattered.

The eastern side gate opened directly onto the raised track. It was wide enough for a cart, secured by a heavy wooden bar within. Sariel could see the end through a gap beside the post. Grease on the bracket would help it move without squealing. The weight would still need two hands.

They continued north outside the wall. Behind the store lay a narrow passage between its rear face and the boundary. A person could walk there, two could help someone between them if they turned their shoulders. No cart would fit. At the western end the path came out beside the ruined kiln.

Sariel crouched in the kiln’s shadow and inspected the little wicket in the west wall. Its lock was better than its boards. She could have opened the boards with a bar, but the sound would carry through the old masonry. The lock was an ordinary mechanism. She would need time, light enough to find the keyway, and a person to hold the leaf when it opened.

Beyond the wicket, the store’s west service door stood beneath an iron-strapped lintel. Old timber leaned against the inner wall; she could see its ends through a crack. Above it, a window opened into the tally partition. A pile of bricks reached partway to the sill.

Roven touched her shoulder. They became still.

A man came around the kiln carrying a cup. He walked past the wicket, checked its lock without much interest, and went toward the southern watch hut. Sariel counted the time until he appeared again. On his second circuit a voice called from the cooking shelter. He turned back before reaching them.

They waited while bowls clattered. The man on the porch remained where he was. The patrolling watcher did not return until the meal was over.

Sariel felt Roven shift beside her, straightening one leg. She had forgotten how long they had been still.

“Enough?” he whispered.

“Almost.”

The tally window was open. A clerk sat at the desk beneath it, sorting papers from an oak box. On the lid a broken-spoked wheel had been drawn in black. The clerk pushed one bundle aside, blue thread through the upper corners. He rested a bowl on the cleared space and began eating.

Sariel watched him wipe the spoon on his sleeve.

“Enough,” she said.

Zara came back from the market the following afternoon with Tema and a quarrel over a sack.

The quarrel was friendly in the sense that both seemed to expect it to continue. Tema’s caravan had stopped west of the square. He wore a good traveling coat whose pockets sagged under things he had no intention of setting down, and he carried the disputed sack over one shoulder as though its weight supported his argument.

“It is a sound sack,” he told Zara.

“It is a sound sack which has been charged as new.”

“It was new before it was filled.”

“So was I.”

He laughed and lowered it onto Mira’s bench. Sariel, who had been trying to draw the lime yard from memory, moved her paper before the dust reached it.

Tema inspected the drawing. He pointed to a shape beside the southern wall.

“Horse?”

“Watch hut.”

“Then I’ve insulted it.”

Zara put a folded receipt on the table. “This is what I wanted you to see.”

The seal was unmistakable: three spokes, a break in the lower one. Lathen and Mere had paid Tema for carrying salt and meal from the Baroness’s provisioning yard to a northern collection point. The receipt named the steward’s provision account. It also had a number which matched the copy Tema retained in his own book.

“Did you deliver it to Garrick?” Sariel asked.

“To a carter I was paid to meet. I didn’t ask for his childhood.”

“Did he wear a red binding at his wrist?”

“Lots of men wear something at their wrists.”

She described the collector’s face and the split in his front tooth. Tema considered, then nodded once.

“That sounds like him. I can say so. I can’t say what he did with the cargo after I signed it over.”

Zara put a finger beside the number. “This can be checked against the intermediary’s receipt at the store.”

“And then?”

“Then we will have more than a familiar mark.”

Tema sat on the bench. Sariel brought him water before asking whether he would let them copy the document.

“Yes. With the whole transaction, including what I did and did not undertake.”

Kestra arrived before the ink was dry. She read the receipt without touching the seal, then asked Tema to show her the corresponding page in his book. He did, pleased by the care even while objecting to the time it took.

Zara named the price of a small supply order she wanted carried on his next leg. He named a higher one. They went outside to inspect the sack again, and Sariel heard Zara laughing in the square. It sounded different from the laugh she used when someone else had made a joke. She was enjoying herself.

Kestra studied the sketch.

“Is that a horse?”

Sariel took the charcoal back.

This time she drew it large enough for everyone.

She used the wool store’s floor, weighting the corners of a sheet with stones. Thane knelt beside her. Benjamin stayed at the end where he would be able to see the south gate. Elena put her bag down in the place Sariel marked for the coppice turnout.

“Not inside?” Benjamin asked.

“Where you can bring someone without fighting beside them,” Elena said.

Roven described the raised track. Thane asked about wheel width, the side gate’s bar, and the groove under the loading door. Sariel told him what she had seen and where she had not been able to look. He drew a little block beside the door and added a board for the threshold.

Kestra copied the broken wheel and the shape of the paper bundle. Blue thread through the upper-left corners. Cream sheets. Numbers to compare with Tema’s receipt. The oak box at the west tally desk.

“I’m not taking every paper in the place,” she said.

“Take what connects,” Zara answered. “If you can’t tell, keep the bundle together. Don’t pull a signature off the thing it signs.”

The carts would enter through the east side gate after Sariel and Kestra opened it. Roven would drive the first, Desset the second. They could carry six grain sacks apiece over that track without asking more of their horses than they could manage. Two local loaders would work with them. Thane would keep the loading doors usable. Benjamin would cover the turn and the view toward the main gate. Sariel would watch the approaches once the entry was made.

“No carts in the north passage,” Roven said when Thane drew it wider.

“I haven’t widened the real thing.”

“I know what people do with drawings.”

Thane narrowed it again. They laughed, then walked the distances in the room as well as the room allowed. The first attempt put Roven’s imaginary horse through Kestra’s imaginary desk. The second left Benjamin unable to see the southern gate. Sariel moved the cart waiting place and saw how much better the turn became.

“Why didn’t we do this before?” Benjamin asked.

“You didn’t know you were standing in a horse.”

He looked down solemnly. “Explains the smell.”

They agreed on one short whistle to say the east gate was ready. No one would bring a horse through on the whistle alone; Roven would wait for spoken confirmation that the loading doors were clear. Two short notes meant leave now. Anyone could call them. Once called, the carts went to the turnout without waiting to see whether a more interesting instruction appeared.

Benjamin practiced the notes until Halen, listening at the door, said they were clear enough.

Sariel drew the north passage again on a separate scrap. She gave it to Benjamin and watched him fold it into his coat.

Thorne heard the plan with the owners of the grain present.

They identified the sacks by the old red-ochre crossed mill sails, and by the charcoal household marks tucked beneath the seams where a dealer’s paint would not cover them. The twelve surrendered sacks were not the only ones in Garrick’s store, but they were the twelve Millhaven could claim without guessing.

“What if there are more of ours?” a woman asked.

“Two carts,” Roven said. “Six each. More means another journey.”

“What if you find him?” Thorne asked Sariel.

She looked at Benjamin. He waited for her answer.

“We get the grain and the papers.”

“No pursuit,” Thorne said. “Not down the road, not into another building because you think the next thing will be easier. We are asking people to lend horses.”

Sariel could feel the map folded in her pocket. She thought of the store’s little window and the narrow space beside the kiln, then made herself look at the people around the table.

“No pursuit.”

Thorne asked the same of Benjamin. He agreed. Elena said she would take a person brought back early as readily as one brought back after the last sack. Kestra repeated what she was looking for and what she could not yet prove. Thane said he wanted to inspect the two carts under their intended weight before anybody called them ready.

They were permitted to go after the equipment and handlers were settled. Roven took his copy of the sketch and asked Sariel whether she wanted the remaining fence rails.

“Why?”

“Practice gate.”

She smiled. “Yes.”

“They’re a fair price.”

“You sold three already.”

“And look how useful the journey was.”

She followed him outside to argue about it, carrying the drawing where anyone could ask to see it.

Chapter 18 The things they borrow

Desset wanted to know who would pay for her horse if it came home lame.

Elena had expected questions about wounds. She had brought the medicine bag so the cart handlers could see what would be available, and had prepared an answer about how quickly they could reach the village. Desset stood beside her long cart, one hand on the mare’s neck, and asked about the horse.

Thorne looked at the animal, then at the cart’s sound wheels.

“We would have to make good the loss between us.”

“With what?”

“Coin where we have it. Work where we don’t.”

“I can’t feed a horse on someone else’s intention to mend a roof.”

Roven stood beneath the mare’s head while she inspected his sleeve for something to eat. He told Desset what he would pledge from his next carrying payments. Thorne added a sum from the village’s available money. Zara wrote both down. It was not enough to purchase another horse outright, but it would let Desset hire one while hers recovered or while they found a replacement.

She read the writing carefully.

“I’m not bringing her through the main gate.”

“No,” Sariel said. “The east side.”

“And no standing under that wall while you chase somebody.”

“We leave on the signal.”

Desset looked at Benjamin. “The signal when you give it, or when I give it?”

“Either,” he said.

She nodded. “Then put that down too.”

Zara wrote it. Elena had almost answered the first question with an account of her medical preparations. Now she was glad she had kept quiet.

The second man they approached refused his cart.

It was the lighter one Thane had hoped to borrow, with a bed low enough that a wounded person could be lifted onto it without much trouble. The owner listened to the plan and asked whether the bandits would know where it came from.

“Perhaps,” Sariel said.

“Then no.”

He kept trimming a stake while they stood beside his door. Elena wanted him to look up, to see how many people had agreed already, to understand that she had thought about the return journey. Instead she thanked him for hearing them and followed Thorne back to the square.

They would use the two carts already pledged. Thane examined the brake shoe on Roven’s cart and asked Tom for a replacement pin. Desset’s longer bed had a removable rear board. With six sacks laid forward, there would be room behind them for someone who needed to lie down, and space for Elena to sit beside the head. They would bring folded blankets and keep the rear lashings where they could be reached.

“Have somebody lie there now,” Desset said.

Benjamin climbed in. His boots projected over the lowered board.

“Someone shorter,” Roven suggested.

“No,” Elena said. “He might be the person we need to bring.”

Benjamin folded his hands over his chest and looked reproachfully at the sky.

They shifted the empty sacks they were using as measures, put a blanket beneath him, and found the arrangement that left his feet inside the cart. Desset tightened the side rope. Elena shook the cart gently to see whether he would roll.

“If you do that much harder,” he said, “I shall begin needing treatment out of spite.”

She laughed and helped him sit up. The space was there. She could feel her own breath loosen a little.

At the forge, Orren’s sword lay in pieces on a cloth.

Elena stopped at the sight. The blade was familiar at Benjamin’s side. Without its grip and the bindings that had kept it usable on the road, it looked long, narrow, and unexpectedly vulnerable.

Benjamin stood close enough that Tom had asked him twice to move his elbow. Thane worked at the new wooden grip, shaving the inside where it needed to fit. He had kept the sound parts of the old leather aside because Benjamin wanted them if they could be saved.

“Will it feel different?” Elena asked.

“It will stop trying to come apart,” Tom said. “Some people regard that as an improvement.”

Benjamin smiled but did not take his eyes off the work.

Elena put her splints on the bench. Thane had offered to smooth their edges after finding a place where the cloth might catch. He set down the grip and ran his hand along the shortest board.

“Here?”

“Yes.”

He found the roughness by touch, marked it lightly with his pencil, and worked it away. Elena watched the curls gather beside his fingers. He tried the board against the inside of his wrist, then passed it back.

“Better?”

She felt the edge. “Much.”

The medicine bag’s new fastening lay flat against her hip. She had used it a dozen times without its coming loose. She told him that, and he looked pleased enough that she said it again to Tom.

“He does occasionally finish something,” the smith said.

“The chicken?” Thane asked.

“That took two men.”

“The chicken has been delivered,” Linett said from the door. “Its owner slept with it. You can both retire.”

Benjamin laughed, his attention at last leaving the blade. Linett put a little bundle of bread on the bench and made them move the shaving pile before she opened it.

They ate standing while the glue on a small fitting set. Elena asked Tom whether he had always wanted a forge. He said he had wanted a horse when he was young, which was why nobody should be held forever to an early ambition. Linett said he still wanted a horse and had merely learned to describe it as useful transport.

Thane spoke about a workshop he had once had, the high window and the bench he could reach from either side. He did not tell the story of losing it. He described where the drawers had been, and the little rack for tools too frequently used to put away. Tom argued with the arrangement of the rack. Within moments they were drawing competing workshops in the dust on the bench.

Elena looked at Benjamin. He was smiling at them, holding his bread in both hands.

“What would you put in yours?” she asked.

“My workshop?”

“Your room.”

He thought about it seriously. “A bed that stays where I put it. A window I can open without making the whole house cold. Somewhere for people to sit.”

“How many?”

“Enough.”

Sariel appeared in the doorway as he said it. She had come for the practice lock and remained to steal the last piece of bread. Benjamin moved his elbow out of her way before she had to ask.

When Tom put the sword together, he made Benjamin watch. Thane explained what had loosened and what signs would tell him the repair needed attention.

Benjamin took the finished weapon outside. He moved it slowly at first, finding the new grip. Then he made a short cut at empty air, stopped it cleanly, and changed direction. Nothing shifted beneath his palm.

His face opened in a grin.

“Oh,” he said. “That’s better.”

Tom folded his arms. “You could have said so without trying to cut my washing line.”

Benjamin looked up. The line was well out of reach, but he stepped farther away anyway. Sariel leaned against the door beside Elena, watching him with a warmth she did not bother to disguise.

He made another movement, more certain this time. The blade caught the afternoon light along its newly dressed edge. He put it away and went back to shake Tom’s hand, then Thane’s.

“Thank you.”

Neither made a joke of it.

Vennit was waiting at the cottage with three women who helped her when a birth required more hands.

They would cover the ordinary visits while she accompanied Elena to the turnout. A household with a sick grandfather had been told where she was going and who to send for if his breathing worsened. The women knew the man’s house and his daughter’s temper. They discussed which neighbor could be trusted to fetch water without stopping to talk on the way.

Elena stood aside until they finished. She had assumed the village would simply have less medicine available while Vennit was gone. The arrangement had required an hour of visiting and people who would rearrange their own days.

One woman asked whether Elena meant to carry all the medicines herself.

“Most of them.”

“Then who carries them if you fall?”

Elena glanced at the bag. “Someone else.”

“Will they know which is the water?”

She showed her the cord and the different flask necks. The woman tested them with her eyes shut, approved one and disliked the other. Vennit fetched a thicker piece of cord. They changed it before she left.

At the table, Elena laid everything out in the order she expected to need it. Vennit checked the brown bottle and its wooden label. They counted the rolls and pads, checked the wrapping, and left a second supply at the cottage so returning would not mean beginning again with what remained in a dirty bag.

“What will you do if two come?” Vennit asked.

“Look at the bleeding and their breathing first. Get someone to hold where I tell them. Ask you to take one.”

“And if I’m occupied?”

“Give a task to somebody else.”

“Which task?”

Elena named three she had practiced with the others. Hold this cloth. Keep this limb still. Bring the clean water, not the drinking cup which had already been passed around. Vennit nodded, then asked about the person who could walk and was making the most noise.

“Sit where I can see them.”

“What if they refuse?”

Elena looked at her. “Then I tell them where they can sit without being run over by a cart.”

The older woman laughed.

Elena folded the last cloth. She could see the place in her mind now: the turnout, the low wall, the roof that would cover one seated person. She could see the route from the yard to it. What she could not see was who would need the blankets.

Vennit put a hand over hers.

“We will be there before they begin.”

“I know.”

“Then pack for that.”

Elena closed the bag. She put the bread in last, where she could reach it without unpacking anything else.

Benjamin had memorized the route. Elena made him walk it anyway.

They used the village lane as a substitute for the track north of the turnout, marking the narrow alder passage with two barrels. The cart shed became the treatment point. A pile of sacks stood for the lime store. Thane and Sariel carried the blanket poles while Benjamin took the place of the person who had to keep the return clear.

“Two short notes,” Elena said.

He blew them. Roven began moving his cart. Desset followed, keeping enough space to stop if the first horse did. At the barrels Sariel shifted her end of the blanket too far outward, and Thane’s elbow struck the rim.

“Hold,” he said.

Benjamin stopped immediately. The carts kept moving.

“They should,” Roven called back. “Get them to your shelter. Don’t leave them behind us blocking the track.”

Elena nodded. She had not seen how easily the two movements could become confused until they happened at once. They reset the barrels and tried again. This time Benjamin sent the carts through while the bearers waited a moment, then followed them with the casualty.

He began to turn back toward the imaginary yard.

Elena caught his sleeve.

“Where?”

“To see who’s left.”

“Who is counting?”

“Sariel.”

“Then ask her.”

Sariel had put down her end of the poles. “I’m here. Kestra’s there. Thane has the other end. The loaders are with the carts.”

“And me,” Elena said.

Benjamin looked at all of them, then at the empty patch of lane he had been about to defend.

“Again,” he said.

They did it again, and once more after that. By the last attempt nobody needed to explain the barrels. The horses had become bored with the whole enterprise. Roven’s mare tried to eat a piece of straw from the pretend casualty’s blanket while they were setting him down.

The casualty was one of the loaders. He fed her the straw and announced that she had been the gentlest member of the company.

Kestra stayed behind to practice identifying the papers by poor light. Tavia had prepared several bundles, some relevant, some ordinary household accounts, all tied in similar ways. The first time Kestra chose by the blue thread and missed the difference in the seal. She swore, put it back, and made herself read the commercial name.

Elena sat beside her for the second attempt. She held the lamp low, as she might have to do if people were passing behind her. Kestra found the matching number, kept the attached receipt with it, and put the bundle inside oilcloth.

“What if the number isn’t there?” Elena asked.

“I take what I can identify. I don’t stay to read the whole room.”

Tavia nodded. She let Kestra keep the oilcloth.

Mareth came to watch the final check of the Waymark. Kestra had prepared a short stretch between the shed and the lane. If mist obscured the lime yard track, she could walk and mark the last turn toward Elena’s position before they went in. In clear weather Roven could show them the whole way without it. Elena walked the little stretch once while the seam was visible, then again after it faded. She could find the way both times in daylight. Kestra renewed the working at the starting mark. With a coat held across part of her view, the pale edge made the turn easier to place.

“It won’t stop you putting a foot in a hole,” Kestra told her.

“I’ll ask Roven about the holes.”

“Good.”

Kestra tucked the bronze disc away. Mareth touched her shoulder, and she smiled without needing to be told what had gone well.

On the morning of departure, Mira put the food into Elena’s hands because everybody else was carrying something that looked more urgent.

The parcels were small, wrapped tightly enough to survive a pocket. She had marked the one without onions for Vennit and refused to explain why the rest of them deserved onions more.

“Bring the cloths back,” she said.

“We will.”

“Washed, if you want me to sound pleased.”

Elena hugged her before she could make another instruction. Mira held her firmly, one hand between her shoulders. The square was full of ordinary movements around them: a boy dragging a broom, a woman shaking crumbs from a cloth, Linett checking the cart pin she had already checked once.

Tavia came down from the wool-store loft with the copies. Zara followed carrying the agreed purse and a list of what had been borrowed. Both would remain in Millhaven. Zara had business with Tema; Tavia would keep the village’s account of what had been authorized and receive whatever they brought back. Neither looked comfortable watching the carts being readied.

“Those papers don’t become more true if you get killed carrying them,” Tavia told Kestra.

Kestra nodded. “I know.”

“I’m saying it because I would find it difficult to remember.”

They embraced. Zara embraced Roven too, to his surprise, then told him exactly how much of the horse pledge she had placed in the separate purse. He thanked her as seriously as he had thanked Thorne for the agreement.

Thane came from the forge with his bar, wedges, and the little block that would hold the sagging door. He handed Elena the smoothed splints. She fitted them beside the bag and checked that the shears remained in their pocket. Benjamin stood near Desset’s horse, the repaired sword settled at his side. Sariel showed him the sketch one last time, then gave a copy to Roven.

Vennit climbed onto the front of Roven’s cart. Elena took the place beside the blankets on Desset’s. They would reach the turnout well before dusk, walk the last stretch together, and make the place ready while there was still light.

Thorne came to the center of the square. He checked the names against his paper, including the loaders, and asked whether anyone had changed their mind. The silence lasted long enough to be an answer people could actually give.

Desset took her mare’s rein.

“I’m going before she decides the bakery is her destination.”

Roven laughed and set his cart moving. Elena held the parcels of food against her lap. As they passed the forge, Tom lifted a hand, and Thane lifted his in return. The little toy hen stood in a child’s arms beside the door, its wooden beak pointing up at the sky.

Sariel walked at the front until the road widened. Benjamin went beside her, listening to something she was saying. Kestra caught up with Thane and asked to look at the block he had made. He took it from his bag and turned it so she could see the grain.

Elena watched the square fall behind them. At the mill approach, Desset slowed for the old crossing. The wheels bumped the first plank, then the second, and the blankets shifted softly beneath Elena’s hand. She put the food into the bag and steadied the water flask with her foot while the cart took the road north.

Part Four The cost of a mistake

Chapter 19 One more thing

Sariel had chosen the patch of darkness beside the wicket when the sun was still above the trees. Now somebody had put a pail in it.

She stopped Kestra with a hand against her coat. The pail stood beneath the latch, half full, with the handle leaning toward the wall. Moving it would make a sound. Leaving it where it was meant working with one foot in the water.

Kestra looked down, then at her.

Sariel stepped over it.

The lock was better than the wicket. Someone had bought a sound mechanism and fitted it to boards that could be pulled apart with a crowbar. They had discussed doing that. Thane had shown them how far the noise would carry through the wall by tapping the handle of his chisel against the practice barrier. Sariel had preferred a little time with the lock.

She put her left knee against the boards, keeping her weight clear of the bottom hinge. Her fingers found the case at her belt without searching. Hest had sewn a small ridge between the two narrowest pockets so that they could be distinguished by touch. Sariel took the shorter hook, found the first piece inside the lock, and let her breath out through her nose.

Beyond the wall a man coughed. Another told him to drink something.

The first piece moved. The second resisted until she eased the pressure on the turning iron. She could picture it now, an ordinary shape with an ordinary task, instead of a thing determined to refuse her. The keyway scraped her knuckle. She shifted her hand and tried again.

The bolt withdrew.

Kestra caught the door before its outer edge touched the pail. They looked through the opening together. The ruined kiln occupied most of the darkness to their left. Beyond it the west end of the store rose against the last pale strip of sky. Across the yard a light burned in the watch hut near the main gate. Someone had hung a coat over part of its window.

Sariel counted the visible men. One at the hut door. One sitting on a low stack of timber near the south entrance. No one between the kiln and the store.

She looked at Kestra, pointed across the yard toward the east wall, then touched the folded paper inside her own coat. The sketch lay in Benjamin’s hand as well.

They went along the store wall. Sariel placed her feet where Kestra could see them and waited at the loading doors. Neither leaf was shut completely. Grain dust made a pale wedge beneath the gap. Within, something scratched across the boards and stopped.

A rat, she hoped. It had made a very small sound for a man.

Kestra put her eye to the crack, withdrew, and nodded. She took the place beside the door while Sariel continued to the east gate.

The bar was heavier than she expected. She lifted one end, worked it out of its bracket, then lowered it against her shoulder. The wood was wet on the side facing the wall. It slipped. She caught it with her forearm before the end struck the stone and stood still with her teeth together.

From outside came a horse’s impatient breath.

She set the bar down. The first short whistle felt much louder in her mouth than it had at the rehearsal.

Roven pushed the gate inward. He had wrapped his lantern, leaving only a small opening at the bottom. Behind him the first horse wore a cloth nosebag with nothing in it. It tried to chew the fastening while he held its head.

“Ready?” he whispered.

“The doors are clear. The watch is at the south end.”

He passed that back in words. Desset walked beside the second horse, a short woman with her hair tied beneath a cap. She looked at the track inside the gate before bringing her cart through it. Her wheels crossed the shallow rut without a jolt. Sariel felt an absurd desire to congratulate her.

Benjamin followed the first cart. He touched Sariel’s shoulder as he passed, a quick pressure, and took his position where the loaded carts would turn. He could see the south gate and the store doors from there. The sword at his hip no longer rattled when he moved. Tom’s repair had given the old weapon a close, settled look, as if it had belonged to Benjamin for years.

Thane came last with the two loaders. He looked at Sariel’s forearm.

“What?”

“Nothing. The bar was wet.”

He wiped the bar with the edge of his coat and put it where the retreating carts would not run over it. Then he went to the loading doors.

The near leaf moved freely. The other had sagged until its bottom edge cut a groove in the threshold. One of the loaders leaned against it. Thane stopped him before he could shove harder.

“Let me get under it.”

He put a block beneath the heel of his bar and raised the door a finger’s breadth. The loader drew it toward him. Wood scraped once, sharply. Everyone stopped.

The man near the south gate lifted his head. Sariel could see his face now. He looked toward the carts, then toward the hut, where the light had gone out. He rubbed both hands over his scalp and called something she did not catch.

Roven answered before anyone else could.

“Unloading.”

The man made an impatient movement toward the building and lay back against the timber.

Thane eased the door onto its block. Kestra slipped through first.

Inside, the store smelled of old lime beneath the warmer smell of grain. Sacks stood on wooden racks with narrow spaces between them. Some had been marked twice, the later paint crossing out a name without quite concealing it. Sariel found Millhaven’s mark on the third stack and put her hand against the seam. A thin trickle of grain ran between her fingers. The sack had been dragged over something sharp.

“Take the sound ones first,” she whispered.

Roven stepped inside and checked the mark himself. He did not accept her nod as proof. She was glad of that. A mistake here would be carried half a day down the road and argued over by people who already had reasons to dislike what they were doing.

“This row,” he told the loaders. “Count them as they go out.”

Kestra had gone toward the small partition at the west end. It enclosed a desk and shelves behind a waist-high counter. An oak writing box stood on the desk, its lid inked with the broken wheel inside an oval. She opened it and held the shaded lantern close to the cream sheets tied with blue thread, comparing their marks with the copy they had brought. Sariel watched her for a moment. Kestra looked as she had at the guildhouse when someone left her the good lamp: intent, almost comfortable, with a little crease between her eyebrows.

Outside, the first sack landed in a cart.

“One,” Desset said softly.

The men found a rhythm. One lifted from the rack, the other took the weight at the door, and Roven settled each sack so that the cart would ride evenly. Thane laid a spare board over the threshold to stop their boots catching in the groove. Benjamin moved half a pace when the first cart turned, keeping the road in view.

Sariel went back to the door. The sky above the south wall still held a little light. They had time. They were using it well.

Desset looked at the load on Roven’s cart and held up six fingers. He answered with a nod, led his horse out through the east gate, and stopped beyond it while she brought the second cart forward. No one hurried. The cart wheels passed with a hand’s breadth between them.

Sariel found herself smiling.

She had been afraid of this moment for two days. Every small sound had arrived in her thoughts followed by a shout, a running man, the first person falling. Instead there was Desset telling the loader to set the next sack farther forward and Thane nudging the threshold board back into place with his boot. They were getting the village’s food out. The men at the south gate still thought they belonged here.

Kestra touched her elbow. She held a narrow bundle wrapped in oilcloth.

“I have what we came for.”

“All of it?”

“What I can identify. The rest would take longer.”

“Then take that.”

Kestra tucked it inside her coat. A corner made the cloth stand away from her ribs. Sariel pressed it flat, then let her go.

“Eleven,” Desset said.

A man came through the south gate.

He had been walking beside a horse. At the entrance he handed its rein to the man on the timber and continued toward the store, stripping off one glove with his teeth. Two others followed him. One carried a crossbow loosely at his side.

Sariel knew him from the description and from the way the others moved. The man who had been sitting got to his feet without being told. The crossbowman let Garrick pass ahead. Even the horse was led out of his way.

Garrick stopped beside the crossbowman, tucked a loose end of belt through its keeper, and gave it a sharp tug. The man flushed. Garrick went on without looking at him.

There was a pale line across Garrick’s chin. His coat was unfastened, showing the worn leather beneath it. He looked first at the loading doors and then at the east gate, where Roven’s cart waited beyond the wall.

Sariel stepped back into the store.

He did not come toward the carts. He spoke to the crossbowman, pointed toward the watch hut, and turned along the front of the building. The narrow door at its west end stood beyond the partition Kestra had searched. He would have to pass the kiln alone to reach it.

Sariel could get there first.

She knew the shape of that short passage: broken wall on one side, the store on the other, room for one man to turn badly. She had looked at it from outside the wicket. Garrick’s men were watching the carts. His sword hung beneath the coat he had not fastened. It was the first time she had seen him within reach of anything except a description.

“Twelfth,” Desset said.

Sariel moved between the racks toward the partition.

She heard Desset’s two short notes from the gate. Leave now. A clear sound, with no doubt about what it asked of her. She put a hand on the counter and vaulted it.

There was a window above the desk. Its shutter was hooked open against the wall. Through it she could see Garrick walking toward the west door. She could climb out, come down behind him, and do what they would otherwise have to attempt with frightened people and a whole village at their backs.

“Sariel.”

Benjamin stood at the end of the rack. His sword was out. He looked toward the loading doors, then back at her.

She pointed at the window.

“It’s him.”

“Come away.”

She went through.

Her boots found the piled bricks below the sill. One shifted, scraping another. Garrick stopped before he reached the door. Sariel stepped down and drew her knife. He looked at her hands first, then her face.

“You’ve mistaken the way out,” he said.

She drove at him before he finished turning. Her knife caught his coat and then his upper arm. He struck her across the side of the head with his gloved fist. The wall hit her shoulder. She kept the knife, but he had his sword halfway out before she found her footing.

Benjamin came around the corner of the store.

“Back,” he said.

Garrick drew fully and raised his voice. Men shouted in the yard. Sariel tried to go past him toward the wicket, found him already moving to deny it, and stepped instead through the west door. It gave beneath her weight. Benjamin came after her, backing through with his sword between Garrick and her face.

The door slammed against a stack of timber inside. They could not shut it. Garrick struck once through the opening and Benjamin caught the blow. The sound rang through the little partition.

“Loading doors,” Benjamin said.

Sariel went around the counter. At the other end of the store, the crossbowman stepped into the pale rectangle of the doorway. She saw his weapon come up and threw herself behind a rack. The bolt passed through a sack. Grain ran onto the floor in a long whisper.

The carts were gone from the yard.

Good. That much was good.

A second man entered beside the crossbowman. Sariel could hear him telling the others to come around the far side. Benjamin retreated from the west door, keeping the partition between them and Garrick. He was breathing hard. A cut below his ear had begun to run down his neck.

“The window,” Sariel said.

“He can see it from the door.”

She looked. The crossbowman had moved far enough inside to cover it between the racks. She could reach him before he reloaded, perhaps. Then Benjamin would have Garrick and the man at the door, and there would be no one to lift Thane’s threshold board for the returning—

Thane was not at the threshold.

Something struck the outside of the west door. Garrick glanced over his shoulder. A tall stack of old kiln boards fell against him, forcing him sideways into the passage. He swore and pushed at them.

Thane appeared in the gap. “This way.”

He had come around the north end of the store, through the narrow space between its rear wall and the boundary. His bar was under the lower hinge. He leaned on it; nails drew from rotten wood, and the bottom of the leaf twisted loose. Benjamin caught the free edge, lifted it over the timber inside, and forced it out into the passage. Garrick stepped away from it, still dragging a board off his sleeve.

“North wall,” Thane said. “There’s room.”

Sariel went first. The door’s upper hinge tore away from the rotten frame as Benjamin pulled it farther. Something above them cracked. Thane looked up, shoved Sariel clear, and put the bar beneath the sagging end of the lintel.

“Block,” he said. “The block in my bag.”

Sariel found it. He took it in his right hand and reached over the stone jamb to pack the gap he had made. The old iron strap across the lintel stretched, tore from its nail, and let the wood roll.

His hand disappeared.

Thane made a sound Sariel had never heard from him. His knees went down. The bar struck the ground beside her foot.

Benjamin hit Garrick with the door. He did not wait to see what it had done. He dropped beside Thane and seized the bar.

“Where?”

Thane pointed with his left hand. His mouth was open. No words came.

Sariel put the bar back beneath the beam. Benjamin leaned his weight on it. The beam lifted a little, then caught against the torn strap. Thane’s arm shook. Sariel could see his fingers beyond the wood, but they did not look joined in the way they should.

Garrick’s sword came around the edge of the door. She caught the door with her shoulder and pushed it against him. A point went through the thin board beside her cheek.

“Again,” she said.

Benjamin set his feet wider and pressed down. Thane pulled his hand free.

Sariel dragged him toward the corner. Benjamin let the beam fall and followed, kicking the bar away from Garrick’s reach. Behind them the lintel struck the threshold. Dust blew into the passage. Someone on the other side began coughing.

They got into the space behind the store. It was barely wide enough for Sariel and Thane together. Benjamin came behind, turning every few steps. Thane’s right hand hung against his stomach. Sariel pulled the front of his coat around it before he could look down again.

“Can you walk?”

He nodded. The next step folded him against her.

“Tell me before your knees go,” she said, and hated the briskness of it.

At the east corner Kestra waited with one of the loaders. He took Thane’s left side while Sariel kept the injured arm from striking the wall. Kestra led them toward the side gate. She had left it open. Beyond it Desset’s horse stamped, held by its head; her cart stood just far enough down the track for them to reach it without crossing the gate’s opening.

“Roven’s gone ahead,” she said. “Get him up.”

Desset lowered the rear board. They could not lift Thane into the space behind the sacks without making him cry out. They did it anyway, with Sariel supporting his forearm and Benjamin taking most of his weight. Kestra put her folded coat beneath his elbow. Sariel climbed in beside him. When Desset set the horse moving, Benjamin kept pace at the rear board, with Kestra and the loader beside the wheels.

Sariel looked back once. A man ran out through the gate and stopped to aim. Benjamin pulled her down. The bolt struck the track behind the cart. At the first turn the wall went out of sight.

Thane was staring at the strip of sky between the trees. His good hand gripped the edge of a sack. Grain sifted through a torn seam onto his trousers.

“Did we get them?” he asked.

“Twelve,” Kestra said. “And the papers.”

He shut his eyes.

Sariel tried to say his name. The cart lurched and she had to hold his arm. She kept holding it through the alder notch and down into the hidden turnout, where Elena came toward them with her sleeves already rolled.

Chapter 20 The injured hand

Elena saw the coat wrapped around Thane’s arm before she saw his face.

“Here,” she said, pointing to the folded blankets. “Feet toward the track.”

The first cart had arrived with grain and no wounded. Roven had told her the others were coming. She had almost begun repacking the things laid out beside Vennit. Now she moved the water vessel clear with her foot and caught the blanket before Benjamin lowered Thane onto its edge.

Thane sat rather than lay down. His left hand closed over the coat when Elena reached for it.

“I need to see.”

He looked past her at Vennit.

“Both of us,” the midwife said. She knelt on his other side and put a hand against his shoulder. “We’ll go slowly.”

Sariel was still holding his forearm. Elena had to ask her to let go twice. When she did, her fingers stayed curled in the air before she put them beneath her knees.

The coat came away from the wrist. At the hand it caught. Elena moistened the cloth and waited. Her own breath sounded too fast. She looked at the clean cloths beside her and made herself choose one instead of reaching for whichever lay nearest.

“What fell on it?”

“A beam,” Benjamin said.

“How long was it caught?”

“I don’t know.”

“Could he move it afterward?”

“No.”

Thane turned his head. “You could ask me.”

Elena looked at him. His eyes were open very wide.

“Could you?”

“I tried.”

The last fold of cloth came free. His right hand had swollen across the knuckles. Two fingers lay wrong, their ordinary angles altered so completely that for a moment she could only remember them sorting little screws on the supper table. There was a wound along the outer side of the hand. Blood welled when she lifted the cloth, then slowed under the pad she put against it.

Vennit bent close. She asked him where he could feel her touch. Elena watched his answers as well as his mouth. Some came at once. At the last finger he hesitated and said he thought so.

“Don’t think about what I want,” Vennit said. “Tell me what happens.”

“I can’t tell.”

“All right.”

It was the same voice she had used with a child who would not admit swallowing a plum stone. Elena was grateful for it. She could not find that voice in herself.

A loader came from the edge of the turnout. “Nothing on the track.”

“Keep watching,” Vennit said. “We won’t stay long.”

Elena took the splint from her bag. The board looked crude beside the hand it had to hold. Thane had smoothed its edges for her two days earlier, drawing it across his palm to find the places where it might catch cloth. She turned it over so that she would not see the small pencil line he had left on the back.

Vennit saw the movement.

“Padding first.”

“I know.”

“Then do that.”

She laid the folded linen where it would support the wrist. Vennit kept the hand still. They did only what was needed to cover the wound and protect it on the journey. Every turn of the binding required Thane to let them touch him again. He looked at Elena throughout, as if she might change her mind and ask someone else to do it.

When they finished he was sweating, though the evening had cooled. Elena held a cup to his mouth. He tried to take it with his right hand and made a small, furious noise.

“I’ve got it,” she said.

He drank. Then he shut his eyes and leaned his forehead against her shoulder. She kept the cup away from his coat and put her free arm around him.

Vennit began clearing their things.

“Can we leave the grain?” Benjamin asked. “Put him flat in the cart?”

Roven looked at the sacks. “Her bed is longer. We’ll settle the sacks tight against the front and take the rear board out, like we tried with you. There’s room behind, and we allowed for a rider.”

“The wheels will still shake him,” Elena said.

“Less than carrying him all the way.”

They did it while she sat with Thane. Kestra stood at the edge of the blankets, both hands over the packet beneath her coat. Sariel went to help with the sacks. She lifted one end before the loader was ready and had to put it down again. No one said anything.

Vennit took Elena’s place while they arranged the bedding in Desset’s cart. A spare coat beneath the elbow, a roll beside the hip so that a jolt would not turn him onto his arm. Elena climbed up first and showed Benjamin where to put his hands. Thane managed the first step himself. He could not manage the second.

“Sorry,” Benjamin said.

“Stop saying it and lift.”

They lifted. Thane bit the collar of his shirt. When he was settled, he spat it out and asked Elena to move the coat beneath his elbow a little farther forward. She did. His face loosened for the first time since he arrived.

The cart began moving. Vennit walked beside it until they reached the main road, then took the place Roven had made for her on his cart. The two loaders went ahead. Benjamin and Sariel stayed behind them, far enough apart to watch both sides of the track.

Elena could see Sariel’s outline whenever they passed a gap in the trees. Her head was bare. She kept touching the side of her face.

“What happened?” Elena asked Thane.

He looked toward the dark beyond the cart.

“Ask her.”

At Vennit’s cottage the lamps were lit before they reached the door. Someone had sent a boy ahead from the first fields. Mira had brought hot water and the clean sheets Elena had asked her to keep ready. The room smelled of soap and the onions Vennit had left half cut on her table.

They moved the onions. They moved the bowl, the spare chair, the little heap of mending. The cottage became a place where Thane could lie with his arm supported and two people could work without climbing across him.

Tom arrived in his apron. He stopped when he saw the hand.

“Out for now,” Vennit said.

“I can—”

“I’ll ask if I need something made.”

He looked at Thane. “I’m here.”

Thane nodded without looking back. Tom went outside, leaving a dark mark from his fingers on the doorframe.

Elena had expected Vennit to take over. Instead the older woman put the lamp where she could see and asked what they had observed at the turnout. Elena answered in order: feeling, movement, the wound, where pressure had stopped the bleeding. Her voice steadied as the things became particular.

“Good. Tell me if that changes.”

They gave Thane the pain draught Vennit had prepared, waited until it began to help, and warned him before they touched the damaged fingers. Benjamin sat on the other side of the bed. Thane had asked for him. He gripped Benjamin’s wrist with his good hand and watched the ceiling while Elena washed away the dirt they could reach.

Once he told them to stop. They stopped.

He breathed through his mouth. Benjamin bent his head close and said something Elena could not hear. Thane answered with a shaking laugh, then closed his eyes again.

“Go on.”

They brought the hand into a position the splint could support. Elena had seen Vennit set a man’s finger after a cart wheel caught it against a gate. This was worse. The swelling hid things they needed to know; what moved beneath the skin did not always move together. Vennit would not keep trying merely because one finger still looked wrong.

“That is enough for tonight,” she said.

Elena wanted another attempt. She could imagine the part settling, the hand becoming a hand she recognized. She looked at Thane’s face and began preparing the clean dressing instead.

When the binding was done, his fingertips remained visible. Vennit checked them again and gave Elena a stool.

“Stay here while I speak to Tom.”

Benjamin rose stiffly. Thane had left red marks around his wrist. Elena noticed the dried blood beneath Benjamin’s ear.

“Let her look at that before you sleep.”

“I will.”

He touched Thane’s uninjured shoulder. “I’ll come back.”

“Bring my roll.”

Elena opened her mouth. Thane looked at her.

“I want to know where it is.”

Benjamin nodded and went.

Elena pulled the blanket up over Thane’s chest. Outside, Tom spoke in a low voice, asking the same question twice. She could not distinguish Vennit’s answer.

“How many?” Thane asked.

“What?”

“Fingers.”

“We don’t know yet.”

“Which ones are broken?”

“More than one. The swelling makes it difficult to tell what else is hurt.”

“And when that goes down?”

“We’ll be able to examine it better.”

He stared at her. “Will I hold a hammer?”

There was an answer she could have given him that would have let them both sleep for an hour. The words gathered easily. She pressed her palms against her knees.

“I don’t know how much movement you’ll have. It will take longer than the cuts.”

“How much longer?”

“Weeks before we can ask it to do much. Perhaps longer.”

“I can’t wait weeks to find out if it’s useless.”

“I know.”

“You don’t.”

She felt the rebuke in her chest. She wanted to tell him about the apprenticeship, the day her teacher had put her instruments outside the door. He was looking at his hand. She stayed quiet.

“Don’t let them give my things away,” he said at last.

“No one will.”

That promise she could make.

Thorne came after dawn. He had been counting the returned grain with Zara and the households whose marks were on it. He brought a cup for Vennit and stood outside until she told him where he could sit.

“Not in here,” Elena said when Sariel followed him through the door.
