Thane had finally fallen asleep. His face looked younger with the effort gone from it. Elena went outside with them and shut the door carefully.

Benjamin and Kestra waited by the low wall. Roven stood in the road, holding the two cart hire agreements. His sleeves were gray with lime dust. Nobody had washed properly.

Sariel had cleaned the blood from her face. A swelling above one cheekbone made her eye look smaller. She kept her hands at her sides.

“We were loaded,” she said. “Kestra had the papers. The signal had been given. I saw Garrick and went after him.”

Thorne looked at Benjamin.

“She told me who it was,” he said. “I left my place by the carts and followed.”

“Did anyone tell the carts?”

“No.”

“Did you call a different signal?”

“No.”

Sariel looked at the cottage door. “Thane came back because we couldn’t get through the store. He opened the west door. The frame broke while he was holding it.”

Elena could see Thane’s coat lying folded on a chair through the window. She had put it there because the blood on it had begun to dry into a stiff shape. Sariel’s words made her remember that she had been waiting with clean blankets while they did this.

“You heard the signal?” she asked.

Sariel turned toward her. “Yes.”

Elena wanted her to say she had not. She wanted the mistake to belong to darkness or noise, something they could solve by making a louder whistle. Sariel’s eye watered. She wiped it with the heel of her hand.

“I thought I could finish it.”

Roven folded one agreement over the other. “My daughter was waiting for that horse. Desset’s boys were waiting for her.”

“I know.”

“No. You knew we had carts.”

Sariel looked down. Benjamin started to speak and stopped.

Thorne asked Roven whether the full twelve sacks had arrived. He said yes. Kestra showed the wrapped papers, keeping them beneath the edge of her coat so that the morning damp would not reach them. Thorne did not take them.

“We’ll read those when everyone can sit upright. For now, nobody goes out alone on village work. If you see something worth changing a plan for, you bring it back.”

Sariel nodded.

“Say whether you accept that.”

“I accept it.”

Benjamin said that he did too.

Thorne looked at Elena. “What does he need?”

“Quiet. Clean cloth. Food when he can eat. Someone to sit with him while Vennit sleeps.”

“I’ll send someone.”

“Ask him first.”

Thorne inclined his head and went to the door. He knocked softly, waited, and accepted Vennit’s shake of the head when she opened it.

Tavia found Elena behind the cottage trying to wash Thane’s coat in a basin that was too small.

“You’ll need another change of water.”

“I have noticed.”

Tavia took the pail and filled it without answering. Her right forearm was bare. The burn had closed, leaving a patch of new skin that did not take the sun like the rest. Elena had looked at it every day on the road, then every other day, then only when Tavia asked. Now she looked at it because it was easier than looking at the coat.

“Thank you,” she said.

Tavia poured slowly. They worked together until the water no longer turned dark at once.

“Tema has found someone who needs a correspondent,” Tavia said. “At the western market. He’ll introduce me if I go with him.”

Elena rubbed the same piece of cloth between her fingers.

“When?”

“When the caravan leaves.”

“We haven’t settled that.”

“He has to settle it.”

Elena dropped the coat into the basin. Water went over her knees.

“Thane is lying in there.”

“I know where he is.”

“And you choose now?”

Tavia’s face changed, but she did not look away. “I chose before the raid. I hadn’t told everyone.”

“Who knew?”

“Zara. Tema. Nobody was keeping it from you.”

Elena stood. Her legs shook with the long night’s work. “I don’t have time for this.”

“All right.”

Tavia set down the empty pail. She did not go. After a moment Elena sat again, on the low step beside the basin. She could feel the wet cloth against her shins.

“I’m frightened,” Tavia said. “I was frightened before he came back hurt. I want a room where the work is finished when the letters are finished.”

Elena could see that room. Tavia would have a better pen than the one she kept sharpening to almost nothing. She would put the papers in order and know where to buy bread. There would be people who had never seen her stumble out of the canal with her sleeve stuck to her arm.

“I thought you might stay,” Elena said.

“I know.”

“I want you to.”

Tavia sat beside her. “I know that too.”

For a while they watched the coat settle in the water. Elena had spent weeks asking whether the burn hurt, whether Tavia needed the sling, whether she could carry her own bag. She had enjoyed being able to help. The thought of using any of it now made her ashamed before she had found words for it.

“Does your arm trouble you when you write?” she asked.

“After a long time.”

“Take the rest of the soft wrapping. It will keep the sleeve from rubbing.”

“Will you need it?”

“There is enough for that.”

Tavia leaned against her. Elena let her head rest against Tavia’s for a moment.

From inside the cottage, Thane called for water. Elena rose. Tavia took the coat out of the basin and began wringing it with both hands, carefully, while Elena went in.

Chapter 21 Copies that leave the village

Kestra had expected the paper to smell of the lime yard. It smelled of Thane’s coat.

She unwrapped it on Mira’s longest table, away from the kneading boards. The oilcloth had kept the damp out. A dark smear on one corner had dried without reaching the sheets beneath. She folded that corner inward and put a clean weight on it.

Zara drew up a chair. “Don’t cut the thread.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You had your knife out.”

“To sharpen the pen.”

“Oh.” Zara put her own knife on the table. “Mine’s sharper.”

Kestra took it. Tavia arrived with water, spare paper, and the expression she wore when she had already corrected three people before breakfast. She set the water well away from the documents.

“Have either of you slept?”

“Yes,” Zara said.

“How much?”

“That was a second question.”

Tavia sat down. Kestra felt herself smile, then stopped trying to determine whether it was appropriate.

They laid out the packet without separating it. Blue thread passed through the top-left corners. The sheets were different sizes, some folded inward to protect a signature, others carrying numbers along a torn edge. A few were no more than scraps. On the first, the broken-wheel mark of Lathen & Mere stood beside a sum that made Zara whistle softly.

“Enough for a whole season’s grain?” Kestra asked.

“Enough to begin moving it. This is an advance.”

“To Garrick?”

“To the firm. From the steward’s provision account.”

Zara put Tema’s public cart receipt beside it. The account named on the receipt appeared in a smaller hand on the recovered paper. The number beneath it matched a number on the next sheet, where a clerk had entered carriage, fodder, and wages against deliveries to the lime yard.

Kestra leaned closer. “That is the same hand.”

“Probably,” Tavia said.

“The lower part of the letter—”

“Probably. We can say what is on the page without claiming to have watched the clerk write it.”

Kestra sat back and waited for Zara to turn the next sheet.

Zara found the acknowledgment. It had been folded twice and pushed between two larger sheets. The signature at the bottom read G. Ursillo. Beneath it a short line confirmed receipt of the listed goods against the firm’s advance. It said nothing about Millhaven or the collection that had left Halen bleeding.

Tavia read the acknowledgment twice.

“What does it prove?” she asked.

“That he received what the steward financed,” Kestra said.

“Good. What else?”

“That the account continued after he began collecting from the villages,” Zara said. She put a finger beside a later entry. “Here. Salt and meal. The same delivery I asked Tema about.”

“And what will they say?”

“That he was guarding the road,” Kestra said, before she could decide whether she ought to know the answer.

Tavia nodded. “So we need the road users.”

Kestra looked toward the square. People were bringing sacks to be weighed beside the mill cart. Yesterday’s recovered grain had not become one simple heap belonging to a grateful village. Three households disagreed about a faded initial. Another had discovered that its sack contained less than when it was taken. Zara had spent the morning with them before coming here.

“Roven and Desset,” Kestra said. “Halen. The people who paid before us.”

“Ask what they will say in their own names.”

They worked until Mira needed the table for bread. Rather than send them away, she put a board across two stools and made them move the ink there. Kestra carried the packet herself. Tavia followed with the clean copies. Zara took the weights and nearly dropped one on her foot.

“Expensive occupation,” Mira said.

“Only when we do it standing up.”

“Then finish before the next batch.”

Halen would not sit with his back to the door.

Kestra moved her chair and let him choose. His face had healed enough that the bruise beneath his eye was mostly yellow. When he smiled, one side of his mouth still moved less freely than the other.

“You need me to say they hit me.”

“I need you to say what happened.”

“They hit me.”

She set down the pen. “Before that.”

He looked at the door. “I said we’d already given what they asked.”

“And they said?”

“That the amount had changed.”

“Did they name Garrick?”

“Yes.”

She wrote the answer, then read the passage back. Halen stopped her where she had described him refusing payment.

“I wasn’t refusing. I couldn’t give what wasn’t there.”

Kestra crossed out the words.

“Will that make a difference?” he asked.

“To what happened? No. To what we say happened, yes.”

He watched her rewrite it. When she had finished, he asked whether the people receiving the copies would send soldiers.

“I don’t know.”

“How long until they answer?”

“I don’t know that either.”

“Then why do it?”

He was rubbing the back of one hand with his thumb. She remembered Benjamin helping him across the square.

“Because the people selling Garrick food should have to decide whether they still want to.”

Halen considered that. “Put in the grain he tried to take. My mother’s grain. People keep telling it as if it began with me.”

She added the sack.

Roven’s account took longer. He knew dates by journeys and journeys by the animals he had used. Tavia brought an old market list and helped place them. Desset remembered which of Garrick’s men had demanded an extra payment because her cart had two horses instead of one. She made Kestra read that sentence back twice.

“A second horse doesn’t widen a road,” she said.

“No.”

“Make it clear that I told him that.”

By afternoon the complaint had acquired people whose names Kestra could attach to faces. Some agreed to have their statements copied. One man would speak only to Thorne and refused to sign anything that might reach the Baroness. Kestra wrote no account in his name. It irritated her to leave a useful page blank. Tavia gave the spare paper to Mira, who needed something to wrap a customer’s cakes.

Tema spread a strip of coarse cloth across the table before he set down his samples. Salt in a little twist, beans in a cup, linen folded to show its width. A small bottle of lamp oil stood beside a bundle of spare harness ties.

“I can leave these,” he said. “In the quantities Zara asked for here.”

Thorne read the list. “And the meal?”

“No.”

“We’ll pay after the first milling.”

“You may have no grain to mill.”

A man beside the window made an angry sound. Tema turned toward him.

“If I carried enough to feed you through winter, I wouldn’t have brought it on two carts. You have recovered grain. Use that before you borrow food you cannot see.”

Kestra had placed the market-rights copy beside Thorne’s elbow. It looked ancient and dignified, wholly indifferent to the cup of beans.

Zara drew Tema’s proposed terms closer. “You can leave the linen without the fine width.”

“It’s what I have.”

“You have the narrow roll beneath the blue wool.”

“That is promised.”

“Half is promised. You told me yesterday.”

He looked at her. Then he laughed and crossed out a line.

The total came down. So did the quantity of oil. They kept the beans because households leaving with the caravan could carry them without grinding. They kept enough linen for Vennit and Elena. Thorne asked whether the harness ties were necessary. Roven held up the split fastening from his cart and put it on the table.

“They are now.”

The new agreement named a quantity of milled grain that could be paid after the next sound harvest. It did not claim the mill if the harvest failed. Tema would accept a later delivery, with the cost of carrying it agreed separately; he would not accept an endless postponement. Thorne called two villagers over to read those lines with him. They argued about one phrase and changed it.

Kestra copied the final version. She liked the feeling of the sentences becoming short enough to understand without a second reading.

“And the papers?” Thorne asked.

Tema glanced at the wrapped packet. “One copy travels with me. I can show it to the merchants on my route and leave it with the office that records your market right. I cannot tell that office what to do.”

“Who carries the other?”

Roven had spoken with a rider going east. The man would go as far as the next market, where three receiving houses dealt with Lathen & Mere. He would carry a sealed packet and obtain acknowledgments for it. He wanted payment before leaving.

The man at the window objected again. “We have seven people here who brought this trouble. Send one of them.”

Kestra felt her mouth open. Thorne raised a hand without looking at her.

“The collectors came before they did.”

“And the raid didn’t?”

“I agreed to the raid. You stood in this room and agreed to recover your sacks.”

“I didn’t agree to a broken hand.”

“No,” Thorne said. “Neither did I.”

The room grew quiet enough for Kestra to hear Mira scraping a pan in the kitchen.

Tavia unfolded the copies. “If one of us carries this, Garrick can name the complaint as the work of the people who entered his yard. The rider can say where he received it, who sealed it, and where he delivered it. He can return while we are still needed here.”

They counted the rider’s fee against what remained from market money held for road repairs. The repairs would wait. Two households offered a small contribution rather than have the full fee taken from that fund.

Thorne paid the rider at dusk. Kestra watched him put the packet inside an oiled case and fasten it beneath his cloak. She had checked both copies against the originals, line by line, while Zara read the amounts aloud. Roven and Desset had witnessed the recovery; Thorne witnessed the copying. The originals would stay in the village chest, wrapped separately from the market rights.

The rider asked where the eastern road became uncertain.

Mareth answered him. They walked to the first turning together, their voices carrying back for a little while. Kestra watched until the horse’s pale hindquarters disappeared behind the hedge.

She had wanted to make a perfect record. A copy was already leaving with one blot scraped thin beneath a name.

The envoy returned the following afternoon with mud on the skirts of his coat and no grain cart behind him.

He accepted water. He did not accept a chair until Thorne took one himself. Kestra sat beside Tavia with the original packet closed before her. She had expected to be frightened. What she felt instead was a sharp impatience for him to ask the right question.

“I understand papers have been circulated,” he said.

“One packet has gone east,” Thorne replied. “Another goes west with Tema.”

“Papers taken from a store by armed intruders.”

“Along with grain taken from our households.”

The envoy looked at Kestra. “Do you know whose store it is?”

“Garrick uses it.”

“That was not the question.”

“No. It wasn’t.”

Tavia touched the edge of Kestra’s sleeve beneath the table. Kestra stopped.

The envoy spoke about provision accounts. Roads needed men; men needed food; the steward advanced money to established traders without inspecting every ultimate purchaser. He asked to see the documents. Tavia let him read the copies while Kestra kept the originals in view.

At the acknowledgment he paused.

“The signature has not been examined.”

“You may compare it with any you hold,” Thorne said.

“I hold no signature of this man.”

“Then ask the steward.”

The envoy put down the sheet. “Her ladyship will not proceed with a protection agreement while her good faith is being publicly questioned.”

A woman standing behind Thorne breathed out sharply. She had favored the agreement. Kestra remembered her asking where the soldiers would be housed, making the prospect real while everyone else argued about dues.

“And Garrick?” the woman asked.

“Her ladyship does not command outlaws.”

“Then who was going to protect us from him?”

“The soldiers offered.”

“Who aren’t coming.”

The envoy looked at the water in his cup. “An agreement requires confidence on both sides.”

He took away no originals. Tavia gave him a copy of the list of goods and told him where the other copies had gone. He accepted it with care, folding along the existing creases.

After he left, the woman sat in his chair.

“So now we have no soldiers.”

“We had an offer,” Thorne said.

“I know what we had. I helped count the beds.”

No one answered that quickly. Kestra looked at her hands. She had imagined that making the transaction clear would make the next choice clearer too. The woman had understood every word of it and still wanted someone to stand outside her door with a weapon.

“We need to finish the defense,” Kestra said.

The woman looked up. “Then tell me where my mother goes.”

Two mornings later a driver brought back the rider’s first acknowledgment. The receiving houses had accepted the packet. One had sent a question about the numbered counterfoil, written on the back of a price notice. Another had said it would make no further advance for deliveries to the lime yard until the steward clarified the account.

Zara read that sentence with her finger beneath it.

“He has grain still,” she said. “And men who can take more. But he’ll have to pay differently.”

“Will they leave him?” Benjamin asked.

“Some may. Some may expect him to get what he promised them.”

Roven had heard from a northbound carrier that Garrick was gathering the men who had been collecting elsewhere. Two loads expected at the lime yard had turned back. Nobody could say whether they had been refused credit or merely frightened by the raid. The store’s damaged door had been boarded. There was a guard at the old track now.

“How long?” Thorne asked.

“A few days,” Roven said. “Maybe fewer. He can’t eat a letter from the steward.”

Kestra wrote the carrier’s name beside the report. She left room for correction. Then she took her working book from beneath the complaint copies and opened it to the sketch of the mill approach.

Benjamin moved his chair closer. Sariel came to the other side of the table. A little later Elena arrived with Thane, whose arm lay in a fresh sling. Tom carried a stool for him and asked where they wanted the drawing board.

Kestra shifted the papers. There was room if she put the finished copies away.

Chapter 22 Five around a table

Tom put the drawing board down with its long edge toward Thane. Thane tried to turn it and struck his splint against the table.

He shut his eyes. Nobody spoke.

Sariel moved the board a little farther away. “There?”

“Turn it.”

She turned it.

“The other way.”

She turned it back, slowly this time, until he told her to stop. His left hand held the pencil awkwardly, the fingers crowded near its point. He drew a line that wavered where he wanted it straight. Then he put the pencil down.

“Someone else.”

Kestra reached for it. Sariel was grateful she had done so before Sariel could. She could not tell what help he would bear from her.

Tom left them the stool and went back to the forge. Thane had insisted on coming to the meeting, then grown angry when Elena asked whether he could manage the walk. She had brought him anyway, with Benjamin on his other side. Now there were five of them around Mira’s table, and the chair near the hearth where Tavia usually sat was empty.

Kestra drew the channel as Thane described it. Benjamin put a mug where the mill stood. Sariel moved the mug east to leave room for the lane behind it.

“Before that,” she said.

Nobody looked surprised.

“I went after Garrick because I saw him alone. I thought I could get to him before he knew I was there.”

“You nearly did,” Benjamin said.

Thane looked at him.

Benjamin lowered his eyes. “That doesn’t help.”

“No,” Thane said.

Sariel kept her hands on her knees. “There was nothing I knew about him that you didn’t. Nothing in the yard that made the carts less important. I heard Desset call us out.”

“I heard it too,” Benjamin said. “I followed without telling anyone I was leaving the carts.”

“I thought you had both gone out behind the first cart,” Thane said. “Then Kestra asked where you were.”

Kestra turned the pencil between her fingers. “I sent the first cart on. I kept Desset outside. I didn’t know whether to call again.”

“Call again,” Benjamin said.

“And if someone says the plan has changed?” Elena asked.

“Then they can explain it outside the yard.”

Sariel looked up. Benjamin was looking at her. His cut had been cleaned, leaving a narrow red line beneath his ear. He had not mentioned it since the cottage.

She nodded. “Outside.”

Thane shifted his arm in the sling. “I can’t hold anything for you now. If something needs lifting while you change your minds, somebody else has to be there.”

“We won’t put you where you have to,” Elena said.

“I know where I can stand. I want you to remember it when I’m not standing next to you.”

They stayed with that until Sariel could hear the sounds from the bakery again. Mira was telling a customer that bread did not grow larger because he had arrived late.

Benjamin pulled the paper closer. “Two short notes still mean leave. Anyone who sees the need can call. The others repeat it so the far end hears. No one waits for me to agree.”

“Where do they go?” Kestra asked.

“Across the channel. Onto the raised lane.”

“And if someone is missing?”

Sariel touched the strip of blank paper beside the mill. “I count people through here. I tell Thane who hasn’t come. I don’t go looking without telling someone the way I’m taking.”

“You might have to go before asking,” Elena said.

“Then I tell. I don’t disappear.”

She drew the narrow path she had found along the mill’s north wall. It was above the race, a working shelf used to reach a shutter and clear the wall of weeds. From the raised lane it ran east to the home end of the crossing. It offered cover from anyone watching the main lane, but it did not cross the water.

“If I have to get back to Benjamin, I use this. Then the deck. There isn’t another way over. Keep it down until you know I’ve come back.”

Thane studied the line. “Does everyone fit along that?”

“Single file. No stretcher.”

“Write that.”

Kestra wrote it.

Elena put a small bowl farther west along the lane. “The shed is here. Forty paces from the crossing, give or take the turn. We can treat there while the forward position holds. If they set fire to the mill, the shed becomes useless.”

“It isn’t joined to the mill,” Benjamin said.

“The wind doesn’t need a joining wall. The cart shed is timber, and there is straw in it now.”

“We’ll clear the straw.”

“We will. And prepare the orchard at the chapel.”

She placed a second bowl beyond the first. There was enough room between them for a small group to pass. Sariel had used that lane at dusk and remembered how the hedge narrowed beside the shed.

“I’ll ask to cut the projecting branches,” she said. “And take the loose stones out.”

“Ask whose hedge it is first,” Thane said.

“I will.”

He looked at her for a moment, then nodded toward the pencil.

The channel looked less impressive in daylight than it did on the drawing.

Sariel stood on the outer bank and looked across at the mill. The stream ran south beyond the building, on the village’s eastern edge. The overflow channel branched west across the north approach, carrying a narrow rush of water between steep, cut banks before bending back toward the race. The little wooden crossing was long enough to make falling unpleasant and short enough that she could speak to someone at its other end without raising her voice.

She could smell wet timber and hear the water sucking at the supports beneath the deck. At Mira’s table she had reached across it to move a mug.

Tom stamped on the deck.

“Sound underneath. The boards are another matter.”

Benjamin stood beside her on the north side, where anyone approaching from Garrick’s road would reach the village. “We put the first barrier there.” He pointed about a dozen paces farther north, at a slight widening of the approach. “Room to stand behind it. Room to carry someone past.”

“And no room for horses to go around without coming down the bank,” Roven said.

“They can get around it eventually.”

“Everything can be got around eventually. We still put gates on fields.”

Sariel walked the proposed line. A low stone wall met the approach on its west side. The stream’s wetter ground lay to the east. A pair of carts and a length of Thane’s practice rail could narrow the opening without making an enclosure. A person leaving could turn directly south for the crossing. There would be no ladder to climb while hurt and no gate someone might lock behind them.

She described the two places where an attacker could come through the western wall. Roven pointed out the third, hidden by nettles.

“Go round,” he said when she started toward it. “There’s a hole under that.”

She went round. The wall had fallen inward over an old drain. An adult could climb across, but would need both hands. She marked it on the sketch and put a stone above the place to make it easy to find again.

“Two watching the wall,” Benjamin said. “They don’t come forward because I shout for more people. They stay until I ask for them by name or the withdrawal sounds.”

“Who?” Roven asked.

“I’ll ask. I haven’t asked yet.”

Thane sat on the home bank with his splint on his lap. Halen held the board while Kestra drew. Tom crouched beside the deck and described the supports.

“Two frames,” Thane said. “Each its own boards and long pieces. Split it down the middle. We keep the width for a cart, but we don’t have to move the whole weight together.”

“Pull them toward us?” Halen asked.

“Yes. Over rollers on this bank. We leave the long pieces projecting back here, far enough for two people to hold the tails down while the others haul. Otherwise the far end drops when it leaves its seat. The whole frame comes back. Nothing broad stays over the channel.”

Tom rubbed his chin. “We’ll need better timber than those edges.”

“Use the sound pieces under the old deck.”

“Then what carries the traffic while we work?”

Thane looked toward the village. For a moment he said nothing. Sariel watched the muscles move beside his mouth.

“One side at a time,” he said. “No carts until both are ready. People can use the first half.”

He was talking faster now. Sariel wanted to listen to him for the rest of the afternoon.

Halen knelt to look beneath the crossing. “Mill can stop carts for an afternoon. Not for two days.”

“An afternoon and tomorrow morning.”

“I’ll ask.”

They agreed on the place the frames would be pulled back to. It had to remain empty. No casualty laid there, no spare cart, no heap of recovered tools. The foot route turned west before that space, following the raised lane behind the mill. Kestra marked the empty square on the drawing and hatched it so nobody could mistake it for a proposed shelter.

“How do they know when?” she asked.

Thane looked at Sariel. “She says who is across. Benjamin says whether he has anyone behind him. Then I call ‘Deck away.’ If I’m not here, Tom calls it. A whistle means leave the forward position. It doesn’t mean pull the ground out from under them.”

“And if we can’t see?” Halen asked.

“Then you wait until someone comes close enough to tell you.”

Six paces of boards. Sariel could cross them in a few strides now. She tried to imagine doing it with someone hanging from her shoulder, unable to see Thane at the other end, and had to swallow before she spoke.

“I’ll come to you,” she said.

Thane nodded once.

At the forge, he could not bear the first joint.

Tom laid the pieces together on the ground and asked Halen to bore the pilot hole. Halen set the tool a little crooked. Thane made a sound in his throat, rose, and reached with his right arm before the sling stopped him.

He sat down again. Sariel looked away too late.

“Show me,” Halen said.

“I can’t.”

“On the other piece. With your good hand. I’ll move it.”

Thane stared at him. Then he took the pencil in his left hand and drew a short line. Halen moved the brace until its shaft followed it.

“There?”

“A little toward you.”

Halen moved it.

“Yes.”

The hole went through cleanly. Tom handed him the next piece without praise or correction. Halen grinned at the back of Thane’s head and set it in place.

Sariel’s job was to strip old fittings from the removed boards. She sat on an upturned box, using a narrow bar and a pair of tongs Tom had lent her. The first nail bent. The second came out with a chunk of wood attached. By the sixth she had learned how to work the bar from both sides instead of dragging harder at the head.

Linett came from the shed with the food. She put a mug beside Thane and waited until he looked at it.

“Left side,” she said.

“I can see it.”

“Then pick it up. It’s hot now.”

He drank. She went on distributing bread as if that had been the only difficulty.

The retaining pins worried him more than the frames. A pin that needed two good hands and a careful angle would be useless when someone was shouting for the deck to move. Tom made a pair with long loops at the top. Halen tried one, then tied a wooden crosspiece through the loop so a whole hand could grip it.

Thane pulled it free with his left hand.

“Again,” he said.

Halen seated it. Thane pulled. On the fourth attempt it caught.

“There’s your fine smithing,” Thane told Tom.

Tom took it, found the burr, and filed it away. “There’s your fine fitting.”

Thane laughed suddenly. It surprised Sariel enough that she struck her thumb with the bar. The pain made her swear. Tom took the tool from her, showed her where to put the block, and returned it.

“Try that before inventing a new word.”

For a while the work became easy to be inside. Wood was brought, cut, and fitted. People argued about dimensions they could check. Thane forgot to guard his injured arm while explaining something and Elena, arriving with fresh cloth, made him stop. He complained, but he moved into the shade with her.

Sariel carried the stripped fittings to the basket. There were more of them than she had expected. They would not need to buy all the new nails Tom had put on his first list.

She went to tell Thane, then waited because Elena was examining his fingers. He watched her face, searching it for news. When Elena said the warmth was much the same as that morning, he nodded as if she had announced an ordinary repair estimate.

After she left, Sariel showed him the basket.

“Good,” he said. “Keep the long ones apart.”

She did. It was the first instruction he had given her since the raid that sounded like work they might do again tomorrow.

Thorne held the meeting outside because the bakery could not contain everyone who wanted to argue.

They put the drawing on a board where people could lean over it. Benjamin described the forward position and the reserve. Sariel named the ways through the western wall. Elena showed the two places for care and asked for people who could carry, fetch water, or sit with someone who could not be left alone. She did not call them all nurses.

Kestra laid Mareth’s bronze disc on her palm. Its blue cord trailed between her fingers.

“I can prepare the route from the forward approach, across the deck, and around the turn onto the raised lane. Only that piece. You have to start where I start it. If you come from the other end, it won’t guide you.”

“Can they see it?” a man asked.

“It guides people willing to follow the prepared way. It doesn’t know which side anyone is on. We can’t leave it as a secret road behind us.”

“And it carries people?”

“No. You still carry them. It shows the way through mist or smoke.”

She described the minute she would need undisturbed, the ten minutes afterward, and the ordinary chalk marks that rain could spoil. Sariel watched people deciding what that meant. One woman looked disappointed. Halen asked where he should stand so that a stretcher would not block Kestra while she worked.

They changed the place on the drawing.

The woman who had wanted the Baroness’s soldiers asked about her mother. Tema had space on a departing cart if they could supply bedding and someone to sit beside her. Another household wanted to send two children and remain. Desset offered places for them in the cart carrying her own boys as far as the caravan. She would return to the village; her sister beyond the western market would receive them.

“What happens to the houses?” someone asked.

“They remain yours,” Thorne said.

“And the grain we leave?”

“Put your marks on it. We’ll use only what you’ve agreed to contribute.”

A man wanted an empty house for defenders. The owner said no. They found another place. Sariel waited for somebody to call the refusal selfish. Nobody did. The argument moved instead to who would keep the key.

By the end, the drawing had grease on one corner and three corrections around the treatment shed. The village had agreed to defend the north approach, prepare the western lane, and send the people who wished to go with Tema. Thorne would answer Garrick’s next demand after hearing the village again. The five would not give that answer for them.

Sariel stayed to help put the benches away. Benjamin took the far end of the one she lifted.

At the bakery door he stopped. “I was angry with you.”

“You should be.”

“I still am.”

She set her end down carefully.

“I wanted to follow you,” he said. “I saw what you saw. I thought if you reached him, I could keep the other men away long enough.”

She had not known how badly she wanted him to say he had followed only because she needed rescue. She looked at his hands on the bench.

“I can’t do that again,” she said.

“Neither can I.”

Mira called that they were blocking the door. They picked up the bench and carried it inside. Afterward Benjamin asked whether Sariel had eaten. She had not. He pulled out a chair beside his, and she sat down.

Chapter 23 The westbound carts

Tavia’s bag would not close with the shoes in it.

Elena found her sitting on the floor with one knee against the bag and both hands on the fastening. The shoes lay beside her, wrapped in a piece of cloth too good to use for shoes. She had put on the coat she intended to travel in, though they were not leaving until after the midday bread.

“You’ll tear that,” Elena said.

“I’ve taken everything out twice.”

“Take the shoes out.”

“They are out.”

“Then don’t put them back.”

Tavia looked at the bundle. “I can’t arrive at a new employer carrying shoes in my hands.”

“You could arrive wearing them.”

“They pinch.”

Elena sat on the edge of the bed. “Then why are you taking them?”

“Because they look like shoes worn by someone who is paid to write letters.”

Elena tried not to laugh. Tavia saw her face and began laughing first, dropping backward against the chest behind her. For a moment she looked exhausted and happy at once.

“Help me,” she said.

They took everything out a third time. There were fewer things than Elena expected. A comb with two teeth missing. The paper Tavia had saved through the canal, its edges still curled. A penknife, stockings, the clean wrapping for her forearm. A folded scrap in Zara’s hand listed the name of the woman she was to meet and the house where she should ask for her.

Elena put the wrapping on top.

“Don’t carry the strap across that arm all day.”

“I won’t.”

“And if it rubs—”

“I’ll move it.”

“I’m allowed to say that.”

“I know.”

Tavia’s voice softened. She held out the arm. Elena examined the new skin, turning it gently toward the light. No open place, no heat beyond the ordinary warmth of the room. Tavia had begun using the arm without watching it. Elena had wanted that for weeks. Now it made the room feel less necessary to her friend.

“It’s good,” she said.

Tavia smiled. “You sound surprised.”

“I’m pleased.”

“Good.”

They packed the shoes along the sides, where the bag was deepest, and rolled the stockings into the spaces. The fastening met without force. Elena tied the last knot and pushed the bag toward her.

“Try carrying it.”

Tavia put it on, stood, and took three dignified steps around the room. On the turn she struck her hip against the bedpost.

“Very employable,” Elena said.

“Thank you.”

Downstairs, someone called for Zara. Tavia looked at the door. She had stopped laughing.

“Have they counted it yet?”

“Not without you.”

The common money lay on a clean cloth between seven places at the table.

Elena remembered it as a heavier purse. They had eaten most of the difference and slept under the roofs the rest had paid for. A few payments earned on the road had gone in; more had gone out for food, cart hire, the things her medicine bag lacked. The cloth did not make the sum look larger.

Zara sorted the coins by kind while Tavia read the short list of agreed expenses. Finn’s share had been settled at Alderfold. What remained belonged equally to the seven who had continued. Nobody had calculated a greater share for the person who had carried the heaviest bag or sat up with the fox.

Thane sat with his injured hand on a cushion. He watched Zara make the piles.

“We still owe for the cart,” he said.

“Taken out already.”

“The second hire?”

“Both.”

“And the timber?”

“Village work. Thorne agreed it.”

Thane looked at the five piles that would remain. “There isn’t much.”

“No,” Zara said.

Benjamin reached for a coin, then stopped before touching it. “We could keep it together until we know what the hand will need.”

Elena looked at him. He was watching Thane, trying to solve the thing everyone was afraid of.

Tavia sat very still. Zara finished the row she was counting.

“Until when?” Zara asked.

“I don’t know. A little longer.”

“After we leave?”

“Yes.”

“How do we take it then?”

“We’d send it.”

“With whom?”

Benjamin rubbed his face. “I’m not trying to keep your money.”

“I know. I’m asking how I buy food at the next stop.”

There was no sharpness in her voice, which made the question harder to put aside. Elena looked at Tavia’s bag, standing beneath the bench. The new employer was an introduction. Until someone agreed to pay her, the money on the cloth would have to be enough.

“We could ask for a smaller share now and the rest later,” Tavia said.

Zara turned toward her. “Do you want to?”

Tavia opened her mouth, looked at Thane, and closed it.

Elena moved her own pile nearer the center. “The five of us can keep ours together. If Vennit needs something we can’t pay for, I’ll ask the village and ask you. That isn’t a reason to change what we said about this.”

Thane looked up. “You can ask me too.”

“Yes,” Elena said. “You too.”

He nudged his pile with his left hand. One coin rolled onto its edge. He stopped it with his wrist and drew it back.

“I need this,” he said. “I don’t like that you need it as well.”

Zara sat down. “I know.”

“I wanted to buy better tools when we got somewhere.”

“You still might.”

He looked at the splint, and she did not try to finish the thought for him.

Benjamin put both hands flat on the table. “Take the shares. I shouldn’t have asked it that way.”

“You can ask,” Tavia said.

“And you can say no,” he answered.

She nodded. Her eyes had begun to shine. She was angry as well as moved; Elena could see it in the way she picked up her coins one at a time.

They settled the last small remainder by using it for food all seven would eat before the carts left. Zara wrote down what had been divided. Each person checked a pile, and then she passed the purse to Kestra with the five remaining shares inside.

“Don’t buy a whole library.”

“Not at these prices.”

Thane laughed. Benjamin leaned back and looked at the rafters. The counting was finished. Nobody could make it feel as ordinary as they wanted.

Tema’s caravan occupied the west side of the square in an untidy line. It had grown without acquiring another merchant’s cart: bundles lashed above goods, household handcarts behind, people who had expected to walk now asking whether an older relative could ride. Tema moved between them carrying a list with corrections down both edges.

Zara followed him with a shorter list and a piece of chalk.

“That chest comes off.”

The woman beside it put a hand on the lid. “It was my mother’s.”

“I believe you. It still comes off that cart. We can put the contents into sacks and keep the chest in your house.”

“The house may burn.”

Zara stopped. Elena saw her look from the chest to the old woman waiting on a stool beside the wheel. She did not say that the house would be safe.

“We need the space for her legs,” Zara said. “If we fasten the chest upright behind the axle, it will make every rut worse. I can take the contents or I can take the chest empty on the last handcart if someone will pull it.”

The woman’s son took the chest by one handle. He was scarcely taller than Elena, with arms made thin by a season of growing too quickly. His mother looked at him, then sat on the chest and began undoing the key from her neck.

Elena helped put the linens into sacks. The chest contained a wooden cup wrapped in a shirt, three little painted tiles, and a packet of hair tied with faded cord. She waited while the woman chose where each should go. There was time for that if someone stopped telling her to hurry.

Mareth came to the cart and helped the old woman stand. They asked whether she preferred the forward bench or the bedding laid behind it. She chose the bedding, then changed her mind when she saw how high she would have to climb. Nobody made the change into an argument.

Elena checked the bandage on the woman’s ankle before she boarded. It had rubbed during the walk from her house. She padded it again and showed the daughter where to look at the first stop.

“I’ll be with them,” Mareth said.

“Do you know how often—”

“I’ll ask her. And stop if it needs looking at.”

Elena nodded. The old woman had been following the conversation with increasing displeasure.

“I can speak,” she said.

“I’ve noticed,” Mareth told her, and held out their hand.

Desset brought her boys from the bakery carrying a loaf between them. They each held one end as if the bread might be taken away if either loosened his grip. She had written her sister’s name on a folded paper and put it inside the older boy’s coat. She made him show her where it was.

“When do you come?” the younger asked.

“When the work here is done.”

“When?”

Desset knelt. “I don’t know the day.”

He put his arms around her neck. She held him hard, one hand against the back of his head, and let the older boy lean against her shoulder. Elena turned toward the medicine basket so that she would have a reason to look elsewhere.

By the well, Kestra was trying to return Mareth’s symbol.

Elena went toward them with the little packet of spare dressings she had prepared for the road. Kestra held the bronze disc flat on her palm, the blue cord folded beneath it.

“You’ll need it,” Mareth said.

“So will you.”

“I have other work and other tools. This belongs to the house at Three Oaks. I can lend it longer; I can’t give it away.”

Kestra closed her fingers, then opened them again. “Until when?”

“Through the winter here. Tema says there will be grain to move when the roads open. If you take that work, keep it through the journey. Then return it by Three Oaks, or arrange a replacement through a Traveler shrine if your road has carried you elsewhere.”

“He hasn’t offered us the work.”

“He is a merchant. He has offered an account of work he hopes will exist. You can decide when there is something to decide.”

Kestra smiled, looking down at the disc. “And if we don’t travel?”

“Send word in spring. Someone will be passing.”

She put the cord over her head. The gesture was small and familiar now. Elena remembered the day Kestra had first come back from a lesson with mud to her knees and so much pleasure in her face that even Thane’s question about the other direction had not spoiled it.

“Thank you,” Kestra said.

Mareth touched her shoulder. “I’m glad it worked.”

“So am I.”

They smiled at each other. Elena waited until Kestra went to help with the handcarts before giving Mareth the dressings.

“There is a clean piece inside the outer cloth. Don’t use the outside against the skin.”

“I won’t.”

“And the bottle is Vennit’s. The label says what it is.”

“I’ll keep the label.”

Elena felt heat rise in her face. “I’m telling you things you know.”

“You’ve been awake too much.”

“Yes.”

“Will someone tell you to sleep when I’m gone?”

“Mira does it already.”

“Does she succeed?”

“Occasionally.”

Mareth put the packet in their bag. “Give her my encouragement.”

They ate the last meal together on the bakery steps because Mira would not let them turn departure into an excuse to block her kitchen.

There were pieces of bread still warm enough to soften the cheese. Zara brought the small jar of relish their leftover coin had bought. She divided it with unreasonable care, putting a little extra beside Thane when she thought he was looking away. He took the spoon and gave it back to her.

“Your share,” he said.

She ate it.

For a while they discussed the shoes. Tavia pointed out where she expected the right one to rub. Benjamin suggested cutting the heel down. Sariel said that would leave her with an expensive slipper. Thane began describing a way to stretch the leather and stopped only when Tavia said she would not dismantle her bag again.

“I’ll write when I know the address,” she said.

“Send it here,” Kestra answered.

“I meant mine.”

“Oh.”

“Are you expecting to have moved the bakery?”

“Only if Thane gets bored.”

He leaned back against the step. “The oven is in the wrong place.”

Mira’s voice came through the open door. “I heard that.”

They laughed, and Elena felt the laughter pass through her without catching on the things she was afraid of. Tavia’s shoulder pressed hers. The seven had eaten in worse places and would probably eat in worse places again. Just now the bread was good and nobody was telling them to hurry.

Benjamin asked Zara what her work would be.

“Purchases. Checking deliveries. Keeping Tema from paying twice for what somebody says is a better grade of wool.”

“Will he enjoy that?”

“He says he wants someone with judgment.”

“He may mean his.”

“I’ve asked about that.”

Elena could imagine her asking. Zara had a way of looking at a person after a question that made an easy answer seem insufficient. She would be good at the work. The thought pleased Elena and hurt at once.

Tavia would help with letters during the journey in exchange for food and a place on the cart when there was space. The introduction at the western market promised nothing beyond a meeting. She had asked for a sample of the work and the rate before agreeing to stay with anyone.

“What if you don’t like them?” Sariel asked.

“I’ll look for another place.”

“Will you have enough?”

“For a little while.”

Sariel considered that, then nodded. She did not ask for the employer’s whole history or offer to go ahead in disguise. Elena saw Tavia notice the restraint and lay a hand briefly over Sariel’s.

Tema called from the square. The first cart was ready to move.

Nobody stood immediately. Zara scraped the last of the relish onto her bread and ate it. Tavia folded the cloth beneath the remaining cheese. Benjamin began gathering the cups and had to be told that they belonged to Mira, who knew where to find them.

Thane rose with Elena’s help. He shook Zara’s hand with his left, then pulled her against him awkwardly. She went carefully around the sling. Tavia kissed his cheek and said something into his ear that made him close his eyes.

When she let go, he told her to buy better shoes with her first wages.

“Something extravagant,” he said. “Make us regret not coming.”

“I will.”

Sariel embraced both of them quickly, then held Tavia longer when Tavia refused to let go first. Kestra had written the address of the bakery even though everyone knew it. Zara accepted the scrap and put it in the trade book she carried beneath her coat.

Benjamin hugged Zara so tightly that she complained. He loosened his arms but did not let go until she had finished telling him how to find Tema if a message needed carrying. Then he embraced Tavia and cried without trying to hide it. She wiped his cheek with her sleeve and began crying too.

Elena waited beside the cart. She had rehearsed a sensible farewell while wrapping the dressings. None of it remained when Tavia stood in front of her.

“I’ll miss you,” she said.

“I know.” Tavia shook her head. “I mean, I’ll miss you too. That came out badly.”

Elena laughed and put her arms around her. Tavia smelled of the soap they had shared since arriving. She was warm and alive and leaving by a road she had chosen. Elena held her until the driver asked whether they were coming with the cart or staying in its way.

Zara took Elena’s hands. “Tell me what he needs. When you know.”

“I will.”

“And tell me something else as well.”

“What?”

“What you are doing when you aren’t looking after him.”

Elena looked toward Mira, who was returning to the oven with the cups. “I’ll try.”

Zara climbed up. Tavia settled beside the old woman, moving the bedding so that the woman’s feet would not strike a box. Mareth took a place on the road beside the first horse. Tema checked the last lashings, counted the carts with his eyes, and gave the signal to move.

The caravan went west through the square. A wheel caught on a stone and the driver swore. A child waved the end of a spoon. The old woman called for a shawl she was already wearing. They passed the forge and the last houses, moving slowly enough that Elena could have followed without effort.

She walked as far as the western hedge. The others came with her. At the turning Tavia looked back and raised her hand. Elena raised hers. The carts went behind the hedge, and for a while there was only the sound of the wheels.

They listened until even the wheels could no longer be distinguished from ordinary work in the village.

Thane shifted his arm. Elena looked at the fingers showing beyond the bandage, then stopped herself from taking his wrist in the middle of the road.

“Does it hurt more?”

“About the same.”

“Tell me if it changes.”

“I will.”

Benjamin put a hand on Kestra’s shoulder. She leaned against him. Sariel watched the empty turning a little longer, then picked up the basket they had brought for cups and abandoned on the grass.

“We should take this back.”

Mira had kept a bowl of stew warm for Elena. She set it down without asking whether the farewell had been difficult.

“Sit.”

“I need to check the shed.”

“It won’t move while you eat.”

Elena sat. The kitchen was hotter than the road. Mira had flour on the side of her face and a little burn near one wrist. Elena looked at it, then at the bowl.

“I did it yesterday,” Mira said. “Vennit looked.”

“I wasn’t going to say anything.”

“Good.”

She sat opposite Elena with her own food. Outside, Benjamin and Sariel were helping Thane toward the forge. Kestra carried the drawing board, keeping its marked face against her coat. The four stopped when Thane wanted to explain something at the door, and even from the kitchen Elena could recognize the shape of the argument.

“Do you want to go after them?” Mira asked.

Elena looked back at the turning beyond the window. “Part of me.”

“There’s still road.”

“I know.”

She took another spoonful. The stew had beans from Tema’s small advance and the last of a ham someone had contributed. It tasted of food cooked by a person who had expected to feed somebody, rather than whatever could be heated between journeys.

“I want a room for patients,” Elena said. “One where I don’t have to move the onions first.”

Mira smiled. “Vennit would like that too.”

“And a door I can shut when I’m finished.”

“Will you ever be finished?”

“For an evening.”

“Then begin with one evening.”

Elena looked toward the four outside. They had gone into the forge. Thane would need the dressing checked before sleep; the shed needed the last of its straw removed and benches carried in; she had promised Vennit an hour without interruption. There would be work tomorrow regardless of whether she chose to stay beside these people.

“I like them,” she said.

Mira reached across the table and squeezed her hand. “Finish eating. You can like them at the forge afterward.”

Elena ate the rest while Mira told her about a customer who had tried to pay for bread with three turnips and a promise about a fourth. When the bowl was empty, she washed it, dried it, and went to find the others.

Chapter 24 A route everyone knows

Kestra counted the paces before anyone arrived to tell her she was in the way.

From the chalked stone behind the forward barrier to the first edge of the deck: thirteen. Across the deck: five of her ordinary steps, provided she did not lengthen them because the water made her uneasy. From the home end, turning west around the mill’s corner and onto the raised lane, to the broad stone beside the cart shed: thirty-eight more.

Fifty-six. She walked it again without looking at the figures she had written. At the end she had fifty-seven because she had shortened one step to avoid a puddle.

She wrote that down too.

Beyond the shed, the lane continued toward the chapel orchard. There would be people at its turns, a rope along the narrowest edge, and strips of pale cloth tied where a bearer could see them. Her working would end at the shed stone. She had said so often enough that she was beginning to believe everyone else knew it as well.

The dawn mist lay mostly over the lower ground. Above it, the mill’s roof looked dark and solid. Kestra pressed her thumb against the bronze disc beneath her coat. She had wanted to attempt the working alone before the rehearsal, to make certain she would not fail in front of everyone.

Instead she walked back to the beginning and checked the chalk beneath its little shelter of stone.

Tom arrived carrying a pot of grease. Halen followed with two lengths of round timber. They placed the rollers on the home bank, in the space Kestra had hatched on the drawing. No one had filled it with spare equipment overnight. That seemed a small miracle until she noticed Linett moving a basket away from its edge with her foot.

“Is that yours?” Kestra asked.

“It belongs to someone who will want it. If they want it before the test, they can carry it.”

She carried it herself as far as the shed, then returned to help seat a retaining pin.

The two deck frames looked almost ordinary now that they were in place. Tom had used the soundest old boards on the walking surface. New cuts showed pale along the edges, and the paired pins at the home end had wooden grips large enough for a whole fist. A removable hand rope ran along each outer edge. The hooks that held it could be released from the home bank before the panels moved.

Kestra bent to look beneath the nearest frame. The long timbers were part of it, spanning from one stone seat to the other and projecting well back onto the home bank. Two helpers would hold those rear rails down while the frame came over its roller, keeping the far end from dropping into the channel. When they pulled it back, the supports would come too. They would not leave an inviting pair of narrow beams over the water.

“Head out,” Tom said.

She moved just before Halen set the roller down.

Thane came slowly along the lane with Benjamin. He had dressed himself badly and refused help with the last fastening. One side of his coat hung lower than the other. Elena had made the sling broader where it crossed his neck, and he kept worrying its edge with his left hand.

They had put his stool where he could see both ends of the crossing without standing in the hauling space. He looked at it, looked at the ground beyond it, and moved it a short distance uphill.

“Now I can see the feet,” he said.

Benjamin fetched the drawing. Sariel arrived with Roven and the two people who would watch the western wall. Kestra knew one by sight and the other only as the man who always stood at the back of meetings. Neither looked pleased to be awake so early. Both had brought their own gloves.

Elena came last with Vennit and the stretcher bearers. Vennit was carrying a basket of folded cloth, as if an ordinary delivery might disguise how much she cared about the exercise.

“Who’s pretending to be hurt?” Tom asked.

“You,” Elena said.

He laughed. “You want a heavy test.”

“I want you to tell us if the handles bend.”

He took off his coat and lay on the stretcher with exaggerated care. Linett put the coat over his feet.

“Don’t fall asleep.”

“I’ve been awake since before you.”

“Then you’ll have had time to improve your manners.”

They began with the loaded cart.

Roven brought it from the outer bank at a walk. The sacks inside contained sand. He had weighed them against the load he wanted the crossing to carry and made Benjamin watch the last sack go in. Kestra stood beside Thane with the drawing board, ready to record whatever changed. She hoped nothing would.

The first wheels came onto the frames. The new wood gave a small creak. Halen looked at Tom, then remembered Tom was lying on the stretcher and looked at Thane instead.

“Let it come,” Thane said.

Roven stopped with the wheels over the middle. The horse turned its head toward a patch of grass and tried to make the stop useful. Roven kept the rein short. Nobody moved beneath the deck. They had checked that before bringing the cart on.

Kestra watched the line where the two panels met. The gap widened a little under the weight, then held. One wheel sat farther to the side than she liked.

“Can it go over?” she asked.

“It can always go over if you drive it there,” Thane said. “That’s why we mark the edges.”

Halen brought a strip of white cloth and fastened it to the outer rope. Kestra wrote a note to put another at the far end.

Roven drove off. Then he backed the empty cart over after the sacks had been removed. It was slower and uglier, which made Thane more interested. A wheel caught the lip of one board. Roven stopped before trying to force it.

Tom sat up on the stretcher. “I said that edge was high.”

“And I said we’d test it,” Thane answered.

They planed the lip. Roven tried again. This time the wheel passed without catching. Halen grinned as if he had personally persuaded it.

Then the bearers picked up Tom.

At the forward barrier they discovered that Benjamin had left enough room for two people to pass but not enough for two people carrying a third. The rear bearer had to step over a cart shaft. He stumbled, recovered, and swore at Benjamin.

Benjamin took hold of the shaft. “Put him down.”

“I can get over.”

“Put him down. You won’t be able to look at your feet every time.”

They moved the cart farther west and turned the practice rail. It weakened the covered corner Benjamin had liked. Kestra could see him measuring the lost shelter with his eyes.

“Will that hold?” Sariel asked.

“For less time.”

“Can the other cart come forward?”

“Then it blocks my view of the wall.”

They walked both positions while Tom waited on the stretcher, complaining that a man might recover naturally before reaching the healer. Benjamin finally shifted his own place rather than moving the cart again. From beside the eastern end of the rail he could see the wall watchers and the open casualty gap. He would have less timber directly before him. Sariel helped him drag a short stack of sound boards to the new position.

“Cover to your left,” she said. “You can step back behind it.”

He tried the movement twice. The second time he kept his sword arm clear.

“Better.”

Kestra marked his new place on the drawing. They carried Tom through again. The bearers no longer had to look down at the shaft. At the crossing they could keep both pairs of feet on the same broad frame if necessary, leaving the other side free for people moving past.

Elena stopped them at the home end.

“Don’t turn him through the hauling space.”

The front bearer looked toward the rollers. “It’s wider there.”

“And it must stay empty.”

They practiced the turn west. Tom said his head nearly struck the mill wall. They moved the pale cloth marker farther out and tried a third time. When they reached the shed, Vennit opened the prepared door and showed them where to set the stretcher without blocking the next one.

There was pleasure in the repetition now. A mistake could be corrected before it contained a real wound. Kestra watched the bearers come back laughing at Tom’s account of his many sufferings and wanted to practice her own part at once.

First they had to remove the deck.

Everyone went to the home bank. Sariel counted them aloud, including Tom, who had got off the stretcher to help. Benjamin looked back toward the empty barrier and said nobody remained there.

“Deck away,” Thane called.

Halen unhooked the hand ropes. The other helpers pulled the retaining pins by their wooden grips. The first pin came easily; the second needed Halen to take a little strain off the frame with a lever. He did it without putting his fingers beneath the timber. Thane watched his hands.

Two people took each hauling rope. They lifted the home edge just enough to settle it onto the first roller. A separate pair of helpers leaned their weight onto the projecting rails as the others pulled. The far end scraped off its stone seat and hung above the water. Kestra watched one of the helpers shift his feet without lifting his chest from the timber. As more of the frame came onto the bank, Thane told the pair to ease off. The whole length cleared the channel, and they laid it down where it would not roll.

The second frame caught halfway.

“Stop,” Halen said.

One helper kept pulling. Linett caught his arm.

“Stop means stop.”

A roller had skewed beneath the frame. Tom set a second lever under the near end and raised it while Halen moved the roller with a stick. Kestra found she was holding her breath. Nobody had been hurt. She kept seeing Thane’s hand beneath every piece of wood they moved.

Thane told them to mark where the rollers belonged and keep the levers beside them. They tried that frame again. It came back in one continuous movement.

The open channel looked much wider with nothing over it. Water hurried below the steep, cut banks. Garrick’s men could get through if they had time and no one opposing them. They would not be able to run straight after the last retreating stretcher.

Kestra drew the roller marks while the crew put the panels back. Tom made her move one line because she had drawn it through the place a bearer needed to stand.

The mist rose before they were ready for it.

It came off the lower ground to the east and spread across the channel, thin at first, then thick enough to soften the far barrier. Kestra had been expecting to wait until evening for a useful test. She looked at her chalk, then toward the shed.

“Now?” Benjamin asked.

She walked the route once more. A boot had rubbed the mark beside the crossing. She renewed it beneath the stone’s overhanging edge, where feet and rain were less likely to reach it. The turn onto the raised lane remained clear. At the shed she checked the final mark, then went back to the beginning.

She could hear people shifting behind her, ready to move. She wanted to hurry for them. Instead she asked Halen to keep the starting stone clear until she had finished.

The bronze disc lay cool in her palm. She stood with the first chalk mark before her and the route held in the sequence of places her feet had touched: the gap, the deck, the turn, the broad stone. She spoke the invocation Mareth had taught her. Halfway through, a shout from the mill made her lose the next words.

She stopped.

“Again,” she said, before anyone could ask.

The second minute held. She finished and waited without trying to force an answer into the silence. A pale seam became visible at the edge of the approach. It followed the ground toward the deck, crossed the timber, and bent away at the home bank.

Kestra smiled before she could help it.

“Come from here,” she told the bearers. “Look where the line goes. Keep watching the ground as well.”

They lifted Tom. He had agreed to lie down again on condition that someone bring him food afterward. Halen went ahead. Benjamin walked at the side of the stretcher while Sariel kept the far end of the crossing clear.

Kestra followed them, the symbol against her chest. The line showed the turn she had prepared. It did not brighten the mill wall or show the water beneath the deck. She still had to look for the raised board at the edge, though she knew Tom had planed it. At the home bank the leading bearer saw the bend before Benjamin pointed it out.

“There,” he said. “I can see it.”

They took the turn without hesitation. At the shed the pale seam ended beside the broad stone. Vennit stood in the doorway with a cloth tied to her wrist, ready to direct anyone who did not know where to set a patient.

“Continue,” Elena said.

They carried Tom west along the lane toward the orchard. From here the way was marked by ordinary cloth and the people waiting at the turns. Kestra let them go without trying to extend the working. She stayed beside the shed and watched the seam hold at the stone.

Roven came up the lower path. His boots were wet to the ankle.

“Don’t use that today,” he told her.

“What happened?”

He pointed through the hedge. Across the low ground she could see a row of old stone openings beside a drainage wall. She had counted them from the lane before. Eight. Now an arch stood a little beyond the last, pale and complete, with a strip of dry ground running beneath it.

It looked shorter than the lane they had chosen.

Roven put his hand on her sleeve before she took a step toward it.

“How long has it been there?” she asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Was it there yesterday?”

“No.”

She looked at the opening. There was light beyond it, as if the mist ended a few paces past the stone. She could have walked to it, set a mark, compared its proportions with the other arches. She wanted to know what would happen if she counted from the far end.

The bearers called from the orchard. They had reached Elena’s alternate station. One wanted to know where the spare water should stand.

Kestra turned back to Roven. “Can we see the raised lane from both ends?”

“Yes.”

“And the stones are the same ones we walked this morning?”

He looked almost amused. “Come and look.”

They walked it together. She put her feet on the solid surface and checked each turning with the people assigned to stand there. At the lower-path entrance they stretched a rope and tied a cloth across it. Roven would tell the others why. Kestra made no additional waymark beneath the arch.

When she looked back from the orchard, the mist had thickened. She could no longer see enough of the drainage wall to count anything.

Thorne’s sister came for the spare bedding in the afternoon. Her house stood on the western rise beyond the orchard, and two households would sleep there until they could decide whether to go farther. She had brought a handcart and a jar of pickled beans for Mira.

Kestra asked whether she would keep something dry.

“Depends how large it is.”

“A scroll case.”

“Nothing living?”

“No.”

“Then probably.”

Kestra went back to the wool-store loft for it. Her account of the Coin had grown beyond the first neat pages. There were additions in the margins, names copied from other people’s memories, a description of the canal she had rewritten after Benjamin corrected where he had been standing. She could still hear the water when she read it.

She took out the pages describing the mill route and put them in her working book. The account of the fall remained unfinished. After what Sariel had told her of Kes’s command, she had left space for things she did not know. She had wanted to fill that space before allowing the case out of her hands.

Now she wrapped it and wrote her name on the cloth.

Thorne’s sister described where it would go: on the upper shelf in the little stone room behind her kitchen, beside household papers and out of reach of the damp floor. Kestra asked whether she might come for it later.

“That’s generally what keeping means.”

“I know.”

The woman smiled and put the case beneath the bedding. Kestra watched her tie it down. She kept the book with the fifty-six paces in her own coat.

At supper Thane asked who had eaten his cheese.

“You did,” Benjamin said.

“I ate the piece on this side.”

“Then there was only one piece.”

“There were two.”

Sariel pushed a slice across the table. “Have mine.”

He looked at it. “Did you take it?”

“No.”

“Then keep it. I’m conducting an inquiry.”

Kestra began laughing and could not stop when he turned toward her. She had cheese in her mouth, which made her a poor witness. Elena put a cup of water beside her and waited until she could breathe.

“It was under your bread,” Kestra managed. “You folded the bread over it.”

Thane lifted the remaining bread, examined it, and looked offended by his own competence.

“I wanted that later.”

“You’ve had it earlier,” Benjamin said. “Think of the time saved.”

Mira had given them the end of the table nearest the hearth. Outside, people were still carrying things along the lane. The preparations had not finished because the rehearsal had gone well. Kestra could hear Tom arguing with someone about returning a borrowed lever before morning. Linett came to the door, called for him, and then told him she would eat without him if he continued discussing the lever’s family history.

He went with her.

Benjamin watched the door close. “I’m afraid I’ll call it too late.”

Nobody asked what he meant.

“The withdrawal,” he said anyway. “I’ll think we can hold a little longer.”

“I can call it,” Sariel said.

“I know.”

“So can the people beside you,” Elena said. “We made them do it today.”

Benjamin nodded. He tore a piece of bread in half and passed the larger portion to Thane without asking whether he wanted it.

Kestra touched the book in her pocket. “I lost the words the first time.”

“You started again,” Thane said.

“There may not be time.”

“Then tell us when you need it. A minute is something people can understand.”

Sariel was looking at her own hands. “I’m afraid I’ll see him again and forget the rest.”

Kestra waited. She had no useful reassurance for that.

“Say my name,” Sariel told Benjamin. “If I stop looking where I should.”

“I did at the store.”

“Yes.” She looked up. “Say it again.”

He nodded.

Elena took a breath. “I’m afraid of having to choose who waits.”

Thane reached across with his left hand and rested it on her wrist. She turned her hand beneath his. For a while they sat like that, with the food between them and the light from the hearth moving across the cups.

Then Benjamin asked whether the working had felt different in the mist.

Kestra began to explain. Thane interrupted to ask about the mark beside the crossing, and she drew its shelter with a wet finger on the table. Sariel asked whether someone approaching sideways would see the line. They discussed how to bring people to the starting place without making them queue in front of the barrier.

Mira brought another plate of bread. This time Thane put his cheese where he could see it, and Kestra told them about the look on Roven’s face when she asked him to inspect a stone he had walked over for thirty years. They were still laughing when the first watch came to collect Benjamin.

Part Five The company at Millhaven

Chapter 25 The price of surrender

Sariel was eating the last of her breakfast when the north watcher came through the mill door.

He had run as far as the crossing and walked from there. Benjamin had asked the watchers to save enough breath to speak. The man plainly resented the wisdom of this. He stood with one hand against the frame while Sariel swallowed bread too dry to go down easily.

“On the road. Past the ash plantation.”

“How many?” Benjamin asked.

“Counted eighteen. More behind the bend. Two horses, one cart.”

“Have they seen you?”

“The front man waved.”

Sariel set the bread on the windowsill. Its uneaten edge bore the neat marks of her teeth. She looked at it for a moment, then put it in her pocket. She would be hungry later.

Outside, people began doing the things they had practiced. A woman took the well bucket into the bakery. Halen came from the mill with his sleeves already tied above the wrists. The hauling crew gathered by Thane’s stool on the southern bank of the overflow. Nobody pulled a pin. The panels still lay across the water, ordinary wet boards beneath the handropes.

Kestra was walking the route from the forward barrier. She touched the sheltered chalk mark beneath its starting stone, then came toward the crossing counting under her breath. Sariel stepped aside. Kestra’s lips stopped moving long enough for a grateful look, then went on with the count.

Benjamin fastened the lower strap of his forearm guard. It had been repaired twice, once by Thane and once by Benjamin after he discovered which buckle rubbed.

“I’ll look,” Sariel said. “East hedge as far as the split ash. Back the same way.”

“Don’t cross the road.”

“I heard him say eighteen.”

Benjamin looked at her. She could have made a joke of it, but neither of them was in the mood for the joke she would once have chosen.

“I’ll come back with the count,” she said.

He nodded and went to speak to Thorne.

Beyond the forward barrier, the north road rose between an overgrown wall to the west and damp meadow to the east. Sariel kept low beside the meadow hedge. Her boots found the firmer patches she had marked on her earlier walks. Ahead, a blackbird came out of a bush and flew across the road, scolding. She waited until its noise had carried away from her.

There were twenty-three men she could see. That did not make twenty-three in all. A cart stood behind the rear rank, and the driver kept looking at the load as though afraid it might escape. Spears leaned against its side. Three men carried bows. Others had swords, axes, a variety of shields, and the leather or metal protection they had been able to acquire. Two wore coats good enough to suggest that collecting grain had once been more profitable than this morning’s work promised to be.

Garrick walked beside one of the horses. His upper arm was bandaged beneath an open sleeve. Sariel could see the cloth when he lifted his hand to stop the column.

She remembered the knife entering. Not the feeling of it, which had been smaller and less certain than she wanted to remember, but the shock on his face afterward. He had been surprised that she had reached him. She had mistaken surprise for an opening she could keep.

A bowman went past him toward the western wall. Garrick caught the man’s coat and pulled him back. They argued. Sariel could not hear the words. Garrick pointed toward the barrier, then at the houses behind it. His man spat into the grass and returned to the others.

She counted again. Twenty-three visible; three bows; the horses led rather than mounted; no ladder on the cart. A man near the rear was wrapping cloth round the end of a long stick. There was a dark jar between his boots.

She left before he finished.

The messenger came to the barrier carrying an ash branch with a piece of pale cloth tied to it.

He stopped far enough away that a spear could not reach him. Sariel recognized the dark coat before she recognized the face. The collector who had disputed the sacks. His horse was somewhere behind him, and without it he seemed shorter, though no less certain that other people owed him room.

“Thorne,” he called.

The elder stood on the home side of the crossing. Benjamin waited beside the barrier, his sword still sheathed. Sariel had given her report. Now she watched the gaps in the western wall while Thorne came across.

“Say it here,” Thorne told the man.

The collector looked at the waiting villagers. “Ursillo wants the strangers. The five who have stayed. He wants the grain taken from his store and the rest of what is due. You put those outside the village, nobody else’s house need suffer.”

“How much is due?”

“You’ve had notices.”

“They’ve differed.”

The collector’s grip moved on the branch. Sariel saw him look at Benjamin’s repaired sword hilt and then at the cart barrier.

“Eighteen sacks. Besides what was stolen.”

“Thirty,” Thorne said. “And five people.”

“If that’s your reckoning.”

“What happens to them?”

“They’ll answer for what they’ve done.”

A man behind Sariel made a sound under his breath. The collector looked toward it, trying to identify a face. Sariel moved a little so that his view was obstructed by her shoulder.

“And after that?” Thorne asked.

“After that he goes.”

“For how long?”

The collector began to smile. “You’ll find him easier to deal with if you stop trying to make him sound dishonest.”

Thorne waited until the smile had gone.

“I’ll give the village your terms.”

“You have until the sun clears that roof.” The collector pointed at the mill. “Don’t spend it making speeches.”

He turned away. Sariel watched him reach the curve in the road before she looked at Thorne.

“There’s cloth and pitch on their cart.”

“I heard.”

“He doesn’t have to keep the promise.”

“No.” Thorne looked toward the square. “People will still wish he did.”

They gathered in the mill yard because it was close enough to the defense and broad enough that people could leave without pushing through a door. Watchers stayed at their posts. Those who came represented households, workshops, the remaining grain shares. Sariel stood near the back with Benjamin. Elena joined them with her bag across her shoulder. Kestra arrived carrying a piece of chalk, looked down at it in surprise, and put it away.

Thane could not leave the crossing quickly. Thorne went close enough to include him and his crew without making him cross the crowded yard.

The terms took less than a minute to repeat.

The man whose boys had slept above the grain spoke first. His sons were gone now, sent west with a household that had room for them. Sariel had watched him tie their rolled bedding.

“If they leave us alive, we can plant again.”

“Thirty sacks is seed as well as food,” Halen said.

“I know what it is.”

Mira stood with her arms folded beneath her apron. “We gave twelve. They wanted eighteen while the twelve were still on the ground.”

“This is different.”

“Yes. This time they want people too.”

The man looked at Sariel. She made herself let him. He had a house here and boys elsewhere, and she had brought Garrick to the west service door of a lime store because she had thought she could kill him.
