The truth of that would not make handing her over a sound bargain. It did make it difficult to stand still while he considered it.

“I can’t promise what he does if you refuse,” she said.

“Nobody asked you to.” His voice was weary. “I’m trying to get through the day.”

“So am I,” Mira said. “My oven takes two days to rebuild if they knock the mouth in. Longer if the bricks crack. Which man on that road is going to stop another helping himself when you open the grain room?”

Thorne asked whether anyone wished to accept the demand. Two people said yes. A woman asked if the strangers could go away without surrendering, giving Garrick nothing to find. Benjamin began to answer and stopped when Thorne raised a hand.

“He’s asking for thirty sacks,” Thorne said. “He has named a cause for taking them. He had a different cause three weeks ago.”

The woman wiped her palms down her skirt. “I know. I wanted to ask.”

“You should.”

Sariel looked at Elena. Elena’s attention was on a woman by the mill door who had started shaking and was trying to hide it by gripping a basket. She went to stand beside her, saying nothing Sariel could hear.

The decision came household by household. Some answered angrily. Some with a brief nod, as though they were agreeing to work they had hoped another person would do. Thorne asked those who had wanted to pay whether they would help carry water and clear the western lane. They agreed. Nobody required them to like the refusal.

“You five,” Thorne said at last. “Will you do the work you’ve offered?”

“Yes,” Benjamin said.

Sariel answered with the others. She could still feel the fear in her throat, but she knew the work she had promised to do.

The collector had to be called back from the bend. Thorne did not deliver a speech. The grain belonged to Millhaven’s households. The people had its shelter. Garrick would receive neither.

The man untied the cloth from his branch before he left.

Sariel’s place for the first attack was behind the western end of the forward barrier, where she could see the wall’s three breaches and send word to Benjamin. A millworker stood beside her with a short spear. He kept turning its shaft in his hands, seeking a place on the smooth wood that had not already grown damp.

“Your hands are the right way round,” she told him.

“I know.”

“Mine keep checking too.”

He gave her a quick look, then smiled so reluctantly that she liked him at once.

Across the lane Benjamin shifted his weight behind the rail. The moved cart left him a clear view of the western wall and room for a stretcher to pass behind. The little board shield fixed to the rail covered his left side when he stood, but he could step back from it. Sariel remembered him moving it during rehearsal, annoyed at losing the larger cover. Now she could see the value of the space.

Thane raised his left hand from the home bank. He had both levers where he could point to them. The removal ropes lay clear, their ends gathered beyond anyone’s feet. Halen and the others were waiting rather than touching things, which looked almost idle until Sariel remembered how hard they had practiced it.

The first arrow hit the cart.

It made a small wooden sound. Sariel had expected a great announcement. The shaft stood at an angle from a plank beside the millworker’s elbow, trembling while he stared at it.

“Down,” she said.

He was already lowering himself. Another arrow went over their heads. Someone shouted from the eastern end. Benjamin called for people to stay behind the timber.

The men coming up the road carried shields in front and spears behind. Six in the first group. Two more moved west, using the low wall. Sariel followed those two with her eyes. One stopped at the first breach, looked through, and went on. The other lifted a foot onto the stones beside the third, where the nettles hid the old drain.

“Third gap,” she called. “Two.”

The reserve men moved on the road side of the western wall. They had short poles with hooked ends, tools made for moving bundles. One hooked the climber’s shield as he lifted it over. The other thrust at his leg. The man came down outside the wall with a howl. His companion dragged him backward before Sariel could see whether the thrust had entered.

The six at the front struck the barrier together. The force moved the cart perhaps a finger’s width against its chocks. The millworker beside Sariel swore in astonishment.

“Hold your place,” she said, and put a hand against his shoulder because he had begun to rise.

Benjamin caught a spear shaft against the rail and drove it sideways. A village woman struck the exposed hands with the flat of a shovel. The spear fell. Another man tried to climb onto the cart wheel. Sariel stepped forward and cut the strap holding his shield. It did not fall as neatly as she had hoped. He caught it with his free hand, and while he was occupied the millworker thrust at his thigh.

The man fell against his companions. Sariel ducked a sword and went back behind the cart, breathless, with the cut strap in her fist.

“Good,” she told the millworker.

He looked amazed. “I hit him.”

“I saw.”

On the eastern side Benjamin had made the road too narrow for two attackers to use their weapons freely. He did not try to beat them both. He caught the first man’s blade, let the second foul his companion’s shoulder, and thrust through the space the movement opened. One attacker cried out. The other pulled him away, cursing Benjamin with a breath he might have used to fight.

The first assault broke apart. Men withdrew toward the bend, taking their hurt with them. The villagers began to shout. Benjamin raised his sword across the lane.

“Stay here. Let them go.”

A retreating man carried a shield under either arm. Garrick stopped him and pointed to the man limping behind him.

“Leave those. Get him.”

The man hesitated.

“You’ll want carrying next time, won’t you?”

He put down the shields and went back.

Sariel saw Garrick alone for a moment between the cart and a hedge, his bandaged arm close to his side. He was looking at the rest of the men returning. His face showed contempt so plainly that she could almost hear the words he was about to use.

There was a route down the outside of the western wall. She could reach the hedge while his attention was elsewhere.

The millworker was still beside her. His spear point shook. One reserve man had blood running over his ankle; the other was trying to see where it came from. Garrick turned away, and the opening narrowed, and Sariel did not go.

“West reserve needs replacing,” she called.

Benjamin heard. He looked across the lane, counted the men beside the wall, and sent one of the defenders back for help. Sariel took the injured man’s place while his companion brought him through the barrier. The attacker at the third gap had dropped a knife. She kicked it under a stone where nobody would step on it and watched the nettles.

A hand touched her shoulder. Benjamin had come close enough to speak without shouting.

“You saw him.”

“Yes.”

“Anything useful?”

“He favors the arm. Two of his men argued before they came up. They’re angry with him now.”

Benjamin nodded. “Tell Thorne. Come back along the wall.”

She did. At the crossing she passed Elena, who was taking the injured reserve man toward the cart shed. The man’s friend supported his other side. The injured man was telling them to mind a loose board.

Thane leaned toward Sariel as she crossed.

“Did the carts shift?”

“A little.”

“How much?”

She held her fingers apart.

He considered the gap with grave pleasure. “Then the chocks held.”

The water ran below the deck. Someone on the home bank laughed, one sharp disbelieving burst, and another person answered. Sariel found her piece of bread in her pocket. It had collected a little wool. She brushed it off and ate while she went to find Thorne.

Chapter 26 The lane behind the mill

The first man Elena treated apologized for bleeding on the bench.

He was sitting because he could still stand, and the bench put his cut ankle at a height she could work on. His friend held his foot. Every time Elena touched the wound the friend winced, which made the injured man tell him to stop being such a baby.

“It looks worse from here,” the friend said.

“Then look somewhere else.”

“Keep holding,” Elena told him. “You can look at the roof.”

The cut was long and shallow except where the blade had caught beside the ankle bone. Blood welled at that deeper place. She folded a pad smaller, put it over the opening, and gave the man his own hand to press with.

“Firmly. Keep it there while I get the bandage.”

“I can go back.”

“You can press that.”

He opened his mouth. She looked at him, and he closed it again.

Vennit had taken the other end of the cart shed. The doors stood open toward the raised lane, the floor swept, the spare cart rolled away to leave a passage. Their linen lay in a covered basket on the highest shelf. Clean water was beneath the bench, marked with a cord, and another bucket waited outside for dirty cloths. Elena knew where everything was because they had put it there together. Reaching for a bandage and finding it immediately gave her a brief, unreasonable happiness.

Then someone called from the door, and she looked up to see two people supporting a third, with a fourth trying to explain what had happened to all of them.

“Who cannot breathe?” Vennit asked.

Nobody answered.

“Who is bleeding heavily?”

The talker pointed at the person between the bearers. Blood covered his face and shirt. Elena’s stomach dropped until he raised his head and told the man pointing to get out of his way.

“Head,” Vennit said. “Here. The others sit by the door. If you can walk, leave room for somebody who can’t.”

Elena finished binding the ankle. She checked the toes, made the man tell her when he felt her finger, and asked his friend to help him to the stool outside. He could carry messages later if the bleeding stayed stopped. No running, no return to the wall.

A boy stood in the doorway with a bucket against his knees. He was old enough to carry it when full, young enough to have been kept away from the barrier. Elena had seen him at rehearsals, competing with another boy to set the covered water down without spilling any.

“Is that clean?”

He nodded.

“Pour into the corded bucket. Don’t touch the cup to the other one.”

“I know.”

“I know you know. I’m saying it so I don’t have to wonder.”

That pleased him. He poured carefully, and she could stop thinking about the water while she examined a swelling wrist.

Beyond the shed, a cheer rose and broke into separate voices. The first attack had ended. The man whose ankle she had bound began telling someone how the hooked pole had worked. His account acquired a larger enemy every time she heard a fragment of it.

Vennit glanced across at her. “Breathe when you straighten. You’re holding it.”

Elena did. Her back had begun to hurt already.

Tom came with another roll of cloth and a complaint about everyone putting things where he had just moved them from.

“The clear space at the crossing,” he said. “Apparently it looks like somewhere to leave a bucket.”

“Did you move it?” Elena asked.

“Yes.”

“Then complain to the person who put it there.”

“I did. I’m extending the complaint.”

He set the cloth on the dry shelf. It was old sheeting, washed and torn into strips by women at the bakery. Some pieces had weak places Elena would have to work round. She held one to the light, chose a sound length, and put the rest aside for padding.

“Thane?” she asked.

“Swearing at people without using his right hand. Does it beautifully.”

“Has he drunk anything?”

Tom considered. “I’ll ask.”

“Give him something. Asking only produces an argument.”

He laughed. Then a crash came from beyond the mill, and he turned toward it before Elena could say more.

This time the shouting lasted. There was no single sound she could identify as the fight. Feet pounded on the lane. Someone called for the western reserve. The injured man on the stool tried to stand, sat down sharply, and looked embarrassed when he saw Elena watching.

“You can take water to the people here,” she said. “Stay on the flat.”

He accepted the cup with relief.

A woman came through the door bent over, coughing into both hands. She had been carrying sand to the mill yard. Smoke followed her in gusts, thin at first, then dark enough to make the open doorway look narrower. Elena got her seated and loosened the tie at her neck.

“Where?” she asked Tom.

He had gone to look. Now he came back with his mouth pressed into a line.

“The drying house beside the mill. They put fire into the roof. Wind’s bringing it this way.”

The shed stood west of it, farther along the raised lane. Elena had watched the weather vane that morning; there seemed so much more to remember when she looked at the whole sky. The wind had been coming from the east.

“Vennit.”

“I can smell it.”

Elena looked round the shed. The man with the head wound lay on the bench; he was alert but dizzy. The wrist injury could walk. The coughing woman had begun to recover her breath. Two people remained outside, one with the bound ankle, another with a splinter driven into his palm. They had one stretcher here and the second forward at the barrier.

“Head wound first,” Elena said. “Then the baskets. Walking people go with someone. Nobody carries a patient alone.”

The water boy stared at her. She gave him the small basket of spare pads.

“Take this to the orchard. Tell them we’re moving now. Follow the white cloths.”

His eyes went toward the smoke.

“West,” she said, pointing. “You’ve walked it.”

He tightened his grip on the basket and went.

A man from the mill offered to bring his handcart into the shed. There was space for it, he said, if they moved the bench.

“No,” Elena told him. “You’ll block the door while we turn it. Take the bandage basket outside. We can load there.”

He looked offended, then saw the two bearers trying to bring in the stretcher behind him. He took the basket and moved.

Vennit wrapped the head-wounded man in a blanket so that his arms would not catch while they lifted him. Elena held his neck steady as the bearers slid him onto the canvas. He swore at the jolt and apologized at once.

“Save your breath for useful swearing,” Vennit said.

The smell changed while they worked. Wood smoke was joined by something hotter and nearer. A spark fell through a gap beside the shed’s door and died on the swept floor. Another landed on the old roof beam above it.

“Out,” Elena said.

She carried the medicine bag and the second flask. Vennit came behind her with the shears and the remaining clean cloth. The coughing woman put an arm round the man with the bad ankle, and he told her that if she sneezed him into a ditch he would be very cross. Both were frightened. The joke gave them a way to begin walking.

At the broad stone marking the end of Kestra’s prepared route, a village woman was waiting to guide people west. Elena told her where they were going, though she had helped choose the place. Hearing the agreement repeated seemed to steady them both.

“Past the split apple tree,” the woman said. “Under the canvas. I’ll send them.”

Behind Elena, Tom called to someone in the mill yard.

She turned. Through the smoke she could see him running toward the drying house. A woman stood near its door, pulling at a long board that had fallen across the opening. Someone inside was calling, the voice rising each time the woman failed to move it.

“Tom!” Elena shouted.

He heard her. He lifted a hand without turning back, and she saw the familiar shape of his shoulder as he set it against the board.

Vennit touched her elbow. The stretcher was waiting for her at the turn.

Elena went with it.

The orchard station had been a place for sorting windfallen apples. Canvas stretched between two sound posts and a tree, with one side left open to the west. Its floor was earth, covered now with boards and folded empty sacks. They had carried the supplies there because Elena had insisted on having a second place. She had resented the extra journey at the time, tired of preparing for things she hoped would not happen.

Now she put the bag down exactly where she had set it during rehearsal.

The man with the head wound wanted to sit up. Vennit let him try, watched his face, and helped him lie down again. Two women arrived with hot water from a house beyond the orchard. One began asking where to put it. Elena answered before she finished.

“Covered pot there. Cloth on the clean board. Stay if you can.”

“I can stay.”

“Then cut this sheet along the sound part.”

For several minutes the work became manageable. Elena washed grit from the swollen wrist and found no open wound beneath it. The splinter came from the other man’s palm whole. He wanted to keep it, so she put it in a scrap of cloth and told him not to trade it for anything foolish. Someone brought the rest of the water. Someone else brought a covered bowl of bread and cheese.

Elena ate a piece without tasting it. The second piece tasted very good. She was reaching for a third when she heard the bearers coming up the lane.

They were carrying Tom.

His boots moved with the stretcher, heels knocking once against a wooden handle. That was the first thing she noticed. Then his face, angry rather than frightened, and the black shaft projecting from the right side of his coat below the ribs.

“Careful,” he told the bearers. “It caught.”

“Put him here,” Elena said.

The words came easily. She was grateful for that, because the rest of her had gone strangely remote.

They set him on the spare boards beneath the canvas. The woman who had been cutting linen put down her shears and moved the bowl before anyone could kick it. Vennit was beside Elena without being called.

“The woman from the drying house?” Elena asked.

“Out,” one bearer said. “Her leg’s burned. They’re bringing her.”

Tom shut his eyes. “Good.”

A bowman on the outer bank had shot him as he helped the woman across the mill yard. That was what the bearer had seen. Tom had not fallen at once. He had tried to get her behind the mill door, then sat down very heavily and refused to let them lift him until they had taken her in first.

Elena cut his coat away from the shaft. Blood had soaked the shirt and spread beneath him. The weapon’s head was inside. She could not see where it lay, and probing would tell her too little at too great a cost.

“Leave it,” Vennit said quietly.

“I am.”

They packed cloth around it, steadying the shaft so that it could not move with every breath. Elena held pressure against the bleeding edge without pressing on the wood. Tom watched her face.

“Bad?”

“Yes.”

“How bad?”

She looked at Vennit. The older woman had her fingers against his wrist.

“We’re stopping what we can reach,” Elena said. “There’s bleeding we may not be able to reach.”

Tom took that in. His mouth twitched as if a complaint had occurred to him and proved too large to begin.

“Linett,” he said.

“I’ll send for her.”

“Not into the yard.”

“Here. I’ll ask her to come here.”

The water boy was waiting outside the canvas. Elena gave him the message and made him repeat the route. Linett was on the home bank with the hauling crew; he was to approach from the raised lane, not cross the deck, not go forward to look for anybody else.

Tom’s hand found Elena’s sleeve.

“Thane too. If they can spare him.”

She sent that message after the first. Then she sat beside Tom because standing made him look up and he was already spending too much effort on each breath.

Linett came without her apron. Elena had never noticed how constantly she wore it until it was gone.

She knelt on Tom’s other side and put her fingers against his cheek. He opened his eyes at once.

“I’ve made a mess of your coat,” he said.

“It’s your coat.”

“You chose it.”

“You complained for three days.”

“It was tight.”

“It was lined.”

Elena changed the wet pad beneath her hand. Vennit took the bloodied one away and gave her another without speaking. They had clean linen now. They had water and shelter and two pairs of trained hands. Tom’s skin continued to lose its color.

Thane arrived with Halen beside him. The younger millworker helped him over a root, then turned back toward the crossing after a quick word with Elena: the front was holding; the crews knew to wait; one of the reserve men was watching the rope ends; Thane would be called if anything changed. No panel would move in his absence.

Thane stood over Tom and seemed unable to decide where to put his feet.

“Come down here,” Tom said. “You’re blocking the light.”

He knelt awkwardly, holding the injured hand against his chest. Linett moved enough to give him room.

“The woman?” Tom asked Elena.

“She reached the other end of the canvas. Vennit’s seeing her burns.”

“Her husband will tell her about that roof now. Every day.”

Linett made a sound that might have been a laugh. She pressed her mouth against Tom’s hair.

“Don’t,” he said.

She drew back immediately.

“No. I mean don’t start making things easy for me.” His eyes went to Thane. “Either of you.”

Thane looked at the padding round the shaft. “I don’t know what to do.”

“I wanted the brown horse,” Tom said.

Linett’s face changed. “I know.”

“The one Roven said was too old.”

“I know which horse.”

“Good animal. Just slow.”

“You wanted it because it liked you.”

“Yes.”

He sounded surprised to have reached an answer without needing to improve it. He breathed for a while. Elena supported the padding when the movement threatened to shift the shaft. His fingers curled once against the ground, then loosened.

“The winter orders,” he said. “Linett knows. There are deposits.”

“I have the book,” Linett told him.

“Don’t give the iron back cheap. They bought work. They’ll have to wait or take what’s theirs at what it cost.”

“I can tell them.”

“Tell them hard.”

Thane bent his head. Tom looked at him until he raised it again.

“Don’t let them put you at the anvil because you’re there.”

“I can’t hold—”

“I know what you can’t hold. Tell them that. You can see when the wheel’s wrong. You can show somebody.”

“I’ll help Linett.”

“Ask her what help.”

Linett nodded. “He will.”

Tom took another breath, shallow enough that Elena felt herself trying to complete it for him. She stopped. She adjusted the folded blanket beneath his shoulder, and the small improvement in his position eased his face.

“There,” he said. “Thank you.”

He was looking at her when he said it. She had to swallow before she could answer.

“You’re welcome.”

“Can I drink?”

She wet a clean corner of cloth and let him take the moisture from it. His mouth was dry. He asked again, and she did it again, careful not to make him swallow more than he could manage.

He wanted to know whether the forge door had been shut. Linett said yes. He asked where she had put the little packet of good needles he had brought from the last market. In the drawer with the unused buttons, she told him. He said he had meant them for her and then frowned, irritated by how obvious that was.

For a little while they talked about the drawer. One runner stuck because the damp made the side swell. Thane knew which side to plane. Linett said she knew too, and Tom smiled.

The next time he closed his eyes they stayed closed longer. Elena felt his pulse against her fingertips, quick and faint. Vennit came back, looked at him, then sat beside the clean linen. The burned woman would keep her leg, she said. Tom did not seem to hear. Linett told him again, close to his ear.

His lips moved.

“What?” she asked.

Elena leaned nearer. She could not make out a word. Tom’s breath came with a small catch, stopped, returned once. Linett’s hand tightened on his shirt.

Elena waited. She watched his chest and counted because otherwise she would keep asking herself whether she had waited long enough. She felt for the pulse again. Vennit put a hand lightly against Tom’s neck, then looked at her.

Elena shook her head.

Linett did not cry out. She bent over him and stayed there. Thane’s left hand gripped his own knee so hard that the knuckles shone.

Beyond the canvas someone was calling for a dressing. Another voice asked where to carry a man who could not walk. Elena wanted the calls to stop for one minute. She wanted to go into the lane and tell them whose minute it was.

Vennit rose and answered. Elena stayed with Linett until she lifted her head.

“Let me wash his face,” Linett said.

Elena brought clean water. She set it within reach, laid a cloth beside it, and showed Thane how to support the bowl with his left hand against a folded sack. Then she went to the person waiting outside.

Chapter 27 The turning in the mist

Kestra found Thane trying to lower himself onto the stool without asking anyone to steady it.

She took its back. He sat, holding his right hand away from his body until the movement was finished. The splint looked large and dirty against his shirt. Its outer wrapping needed changing; Elena had said so that morning, before everyone acquired more urgent work.

“Tom?” Kestra asked.

Thane shook his head.

She had known from the way he came down the lane. She had nevertheless expected him to say something that would make her first thought wrong.

“Oh,” she said.

He put his left hand on the top of one thigh. It shook. Kestra kept hold of the stool, although it was perfectly steady now.

“Linett’s with him,” he said. “She’s staying.”

“Of course.”

“She was on the tail rail.”

Kestra looked toward the crossing. Each framed panel had two long rear rails projecting onto the home bank. A pair of helpers had been shown where to hold those tails down while the hauling crew drew the frame onto its rollers. There was another woman waiting beside the spare lever, looking toward the orchard.

“I’ll ask her,” Kestra said.

Thane nodded. “She knows which end. Don’t let them stand in the rope.”

The woman had practiced with Linett the previous afternoon. She went to the appointed place without asking why the replacement was needed. Kestra was grateful for that, and then angry with herself for wanting the absence to pass without a word. She could not find a satisfactory feeling to have, so she checked the space behind the frames instead. A loose bucket stood well beyond it. The ropes lay in their shallow grooves, and Halen was testing the nearer roller with the end of a stick.

Across the water the barrier still held. Kestra could hear Benjamin calling short instructions. Smoke traveled over the mill roof and drifted across the lane in torn strips. Beneath it a whiter mist was beginning to collect along the water. She had seen mist arrive at unsuitable hours before. The fields beyond the northern road were warm in the afternoon light; here, just above the race, her hands felt damp.

She touched the bronze disc beneath her collar. The blue cord had rubbed a small pink line against her neck. She drew it over the cloth of her shirt so it would lie more comfortably.

“What do you need?” Thane asked.

“To check the far mark.”

“You walked it this morning.”

“And nobody had dropped a spear across it then.”

He looked beyond her. A broken shaft lay on the approach, half covered by a grain sack somebody had used to drag it clear.

“Right,” he said. “Move the spear.”

She picked it up and laid it against the mill wall. The little stone shelter over the home-side turn still protected the chalk. Water had not reached it. Farther west, at the broad stone by the cart shed, the woman guiding people to the orchard had tied a second white cloth higher on her pole. The shed behind her smoked along one edge of its roof. Two men threw water onto the nearer posts, keeping the fire from jumping into the neighboring fence.

The boards were sound, the lane was clear, and the marks remained where she had put them.

She returned along it to the northern starting stone. She counted as she went, not because the distance might suddenly have changed by a useful amount, but because counting kept her from hurrying past a damaged place. Fifty-seven paces this time. Her shorter step at the turn accounted for the difference. The prepared route ended at the broad stone; the rest of the way belonged to guides, cloths, and people who knew their neighbors’ names.

Sariel was waiting behind the western cart of the barrier. She had a streak of soot under one eye and somebody else’s blood dried on a sleeve.

“Benjamin wants the way ready,” she said. “They’ve brought hooks for the carts. We may have to give them the front.”

“Are we withdrawing now?”

“Not yet. He wants the wounded moving first. Can you give us the full time?”

“From when I finish, about ten minutes. I need the minute here.”

Sariel glanced at the sheltered stone. “All right. I’ll keep people off you.”

Kestra crouched where she had practiced. Her knees recognized the awkward angle before her mind settled. She set the disc against the chalk at the start, checked that its blue cord was free, and laid her other hand flat against the earth to steady herself.

Someone behind her began to say her name. Sariel answered him. Kestra did not turn to find out what he wanted.

The words were familiar enough now that she knew where her breathing would fall.

She did not rush them. Once, in the academy, she had hurried a complicated preparation because everyone else in the room had finished. She could still remember the smell of the scorched table and the terrible eagerness with which she had offered an explanation. Here there was no one to impress except people trying to get across a ditch, and they would be better served by a complete minute than a clever account of why she had failed to give them one.

The first arrow struck the far side of the cart while she spoke. It made her shoulder tighten. She kept the next word where it belonged.

A second sound followed: wood cracking, then Benjamin telling somebody to let go of the hook. Footsteps scraped behind her. Sariel said, very distinctly, “Walk around.”

Kestra went on.

The rite did not require her to stop hearing. She heard everything: the little tug in someone’s breath, the shallow splash of water below the deck, Thane’s voice from the other bank demanding that a rope be laid down. The disc was cool against her palm. She waited for the warmth she remembered from practice, feeling the worn edge and the small nick beside the branching road.

At the last phrase the chalk mark seemed to pale beneath it. A line finer than a thread extended from the stone, running along the ground toward the crossing. Kestra lifted the disc. The line remained.

She stood too quickly and had to put one hand on the cart until the dizziness passed.

“Ready,” she told Sariel.

Sariel looked where she pointed. Her face changed.

“I can see it.”

“From here. Enter here.”

Sariel nodded, already calling the first bearers. The man on their stretcher had taken a blow to the knee and could not straighten it. He was insisting that they carry him with the bad leg on the other side, which would not have changed anything except the time required to put him down and turn him.

“Follow the pale line,” Kestra told the near bearer. “All the way to the broad stone. Then the woman there takes you west.”

The bearer looked down. “Like moonlight.”

“Yes.”

“Can I put my foot on it?”

“Yes. It’s showing the boards.”

He set his boot on the line, cautiously, as if he expected to feel something under the sole. Nothing happened beyond his standing where he meant to stand. He grinned at her, then looked forward and called the lift.

The four men carried their neighbor through the opening behind the carts. Kestra followed their progress until smoke hid their shoulders. She could still see where the working ran, passing between the handropes over the water and turning west on the far side. The bearers’ feet continued at the same careful speed.

A second group came: one man with a cut scalp, two unhurt defenders going with him, and a woman holding a spear she had broken halfway down the shaft. She was laughing in short bursts, unable to stop. Kestra told her she could leave the broken piece. The woman looked surprised, as though she had forgotten what was in her hand, and put it beside the cart.

Beyond the barrier someone shouted Garrick’s name. A hook came over the eastern cart’s side and caught a spoke. The rope attached to it tightened. Benjamin struck at the exposed length with his sword, but the angle was awkward and the first cut only frayed it. He stepped back as a spear came through, waited for its point to pass, and cut again. The rope parted. The cart settled against its chock with a jolt that Kestra felt through her boots.

“Start the withdrawal,” he called.

Two short whistle notes sounded from Sariel. Another defender repeated them, farther west. Thane’s crew did not touch the pins. Kestra could see them on the home bank, poised beside their ropes while the people they were waiting for came toward them.

Sariel went through with the second group to count at the home end. She stopped beside Kestra long enough to say, “I’m counting there. You tell me who you’ve actually seen.”

“I will.”

“Don’t come last.”

Kestra wanted to say that the symbol could travel with her. Sariel already knew. She swallowed the explanation and nodded.

The mist rose while the next people came. It did not roll in dramatically. It thickened in the hollow until the opposite bank lost its edges. Smoke moved through it in darker streams. A piece of the mill’s roof appeared to detach and float above the whiteness, then became a roof again when a gust opened the view.

Kestra looked at the lane instead. Her line went where she had put it. The chalk marks were modest things under stones, but the path joining them remained distinct.

She felt delight so suddenly that she almost laughed. She had learned this. Mareth had taught her, Thane had tested it, villagers had walked it and corrected her assumptions, and now people were using it without needing her to explain what it meant. A woman took an injured man’s hand and led him into the whiteness with her eyes on the ground. Their feet found the next boards. They went on.

“Kestra.”

Benjamin was beside the starting stone. There was a shallow cut along his jaw, new or reopened she could not tell, and his breathing was hard.

“Your turn. Those two go with you.”

He indicated the remaining western reserve men. One held a pole; the other kept rubbing a bruised shoulder. They had come in from the wall in good order, looking behind them together.

“The young man at the cart?” Kestra asked.

“I have him.”

She could see the millworker who had stood beside Sariel that morning. His spear had gone. He was trying to pull his boot from between a fallen shaft and the cart wheel. Benjamin was already moving toward him.

Kestra wanted to wait. One minute had seemed a great deal to ask of other people. Now she was tempted to spend more of their time because leaving made her feel disloyal.

“Two of you here,” she said. “You and him.”

Benjamin glanced back. “Yes. Tell Sariel.”

She took the reserve men to the starting mark, showed them the line, and crossed with them.

The boards were slippery beneath the mist. Kestra kept one hand close to the rope without gripping it, leaving the line of the handrope free for the man whose shoulder hurt. Water moved below them with the same indifferent rush it had made during practice. At the middle of the deck she could no longer see either bank clearly.

The injured man stopped.

“That’s the lower lane,” he said, pointing east into the white.

“No. We’re still crossing.”

“I can see the arch.”

Kestra looked. A curve of stone stood above a patch of dark water where she had expected only the mill race. It might have been one of the drainage arches revealed by the angle. She could not count the others through the mist, and the part of her that wanted to look longer was the same part that would have followed the extra arch during rehearsal.

“The boards are here,” she said. “Keep your hand on the rope.”

He hesitated.

She put her boot beside his, on the pale seam. “This one. Then the next.”

He moved. The reserve man behind them put a hand between his shoulder blades, light enough to guide rather than shove. They reached the home bank together.

Sariel met them at the end of the deck. She looked at both men before she looked at Kestra.

“Benjamin and the young millworker,” Kestra said. “The worker’s boot was caught at the cart. Benjamin went to free it. They’re the last I saw north of the channel.”

“No one at the wall?”

“These two came in. I watched them.”

The man with the pole confirmed it. “Both gaps clear. We called down the third. Nobody answered, and I looked before I left.”

Sariel repeated the information to Thane. He had moved his stool a little uphill, where the mist lay thinner. Halen stood beside him, watching the deck with the fixed attention of someone waiting for his name to be called.

A shout came from the north. It was not a word Kestra could identify. One of the hauling crew took hold of a rope.

“Leave it,” Thane said.

The man let go.

“Are they coming?” he asked.

“That’s what we’re finding out.”

Kestra wanted to look back across the deck, but a woman at the turn was trying to bring the stretcher toward the hauling space. One bearer had lost sight of the guide by the broad stone. He had followed the sound of Thane’s voice instead of the line on the ground.

“West here,” Kestra told him. “Before the rollers.”

He stopped. The wounded man on the stretcher asked whether they meant to take him swimming. The bearer apologized and backed one step while the others held their positions. Kestra put herself at the correct turn, pointing with her whole arm. They came past her, away from the deck tails and along the raised lane.

The ordinary guide appeared through the mist, waving her higher white cloth.

“Got them,” she called.

Kestra followed as far as the broad stone. The line ended at her feet. Beyond it the guide had a rope tied between posts on the lane’s dry side; people touched it with their hands and went west toward the orchard. Smoke had hidden the chapel roof. It had not moved the orchard or the posts.

“Keep them to this side,” Kestra said.

“We are.” The guide looked at the pale ground. “That’s a useful bit of work.”

Kestra could not help smiling. “Thank you.”

“Get yourself out of the road, then. There’s another coming.”

She stepped aside for a man carrying a water pot and went back to the home end of the crossing. She knew the raised lane and could follow its wall; the working did not guide her backward. To renew it she would need a minute at the starting stone again. Beyond the water she could hear the fight moving toward the channel. The smoke caught in her throat. Her eyes streamed, blurring the edge of the pale mill wall.

The young millworker emerged on the opposite bank while she was still wiping her face. His boot was missing. He crossed with one foot bare, clutching the rope in both hands.

Sariel caught him at the home end and brought him up to the raised-lane turn, leaving the deck clear.

“Benjamin?”

“Behind me. He sent me.”

“Anyone else?”

The man shook his head. “He got my foot loose. They came round the eastern cart. He told me to go.”

Sariel looked across. Kestra could see only the first few boards, then mist. A blade struck wood somewhere beyond it. The sound was followed by Benjamin’s voice, too distant for words.

“The lane’s clear,” Kestra told Sariel. “Guides have the wounded. Both reserve men are home.”

Sariel nodded. She had her knife out, held low beside her leg.

“Keep this man here long enough for Thane to count him. I’m taking the shelf to the deck head.”

She pointed along the narrow maintenance shelf against the mill’s north wall, a way round the people gathered at the turn. It reached the same home end of the crossing. Kestra had stood on it during rehearsal and disliked the space it left for her feet.

“I heard,” she said. “The shelf, then across.”

“Tell Benjamin where I went if we pass each other in this mess.”

Kestra nodded. Sariel stepped onto the shelf, one hand against the mill wall. Her body disappeared into the smoke almost at once. Kestra could still hear the measured scrape of her boots as she moved toward the deck.

She took the millworker to Thane. His bare foot had a long red mark where the shaft had pressed it, and he was shaking too hard to answer the first time Thane asked whether he had seen anybody left outside the barrier.

“Just Benjamin,” he managed at last. “Just him.”

Thane looked at the empty place where Sariel had stood.

“And Sariel now,” Kestra said.

He nodded. His left hand closed round the end of the stool. The crew waited beside the frames, and Kestra stood where she could see the first board, holding the bronze disc against the front of her shirt so that its cord would not catch if someone needed her hands.

Chapter 28 Leave the crossing

The shelf was narrower when Sariel could not see the water below it.

She kept her left palm against the mill wall and moved her feet sideways past the projecting stone. The knife in her right hand wanted putting away. She kept it out because the next place she would need it was only a few steps beyond the end of the shelf, and she did not trust herself to find the sheath cleanly with fingers this wet.

At the deck head she could hear Benjamin.

“Back,” he said.

No answer. The scrape of boots, then metal striking the barrier hard enough to ring through the smoke.

Sariel stepped off the shelf onto the home bank. She caught the handrope with her left hand and followed the known boards, one foot after another, looking up often enough to see anything coming toward her. A loose board moved beneath her heel; the frame beneath it remained firm. She had walked it carrying a sack. She had watched Tom cross on a stretcher while complaining about the view. The boards held her weight now as they had held his.

“Benjamin,” she called.

“Sariel?”

“Coming across.”

“Stay back.”

She reached the north bank and went west of the center of the road, toward the low wall. The barrier was thirteen paces ahead. One cart had slewed against its remaining chock; the other smoked along its canvas cover. The open space behind them looked impossibly small for all the people who had stood there earlier.

Benjamin was retreating from it. Garrick followed him, sword in his right hand, the bandage on his left upper arm dark with sweat. Another man came behind Garrick with a short spear. A third remained beside the burning cart, trying to unhook a strap from its wheel. Sariel hurried up the inside of the wall to meet Benjamin.

Benjamin glanced toward Sariel and lost ground at once. Garrick struck at his face. Benjamin caught the blade high, stepped back, and nearly caught his heel on a stone.

“Left,” Sariel said.

He shifted enough to give her space beside him. She had no shield. The knife was useful inside the reach of a sword and very little comfort outside it.

Garrick saw her. His mouth opened in recognition, and for a moment he looked almost pleased.

“There you are.”

Sariel did not answer. She watched the spear behind him instead. Its owner was moving west, seeking room to come round Benjamin. She picked up a fist-sized piece of broken stone and threw it at his face.

He jerked aside. The stone struck his shoulder. His spear point dropped long enough for her to catch its shaft under her left arm and drive forward. The wood grated against her ribs. He pulled back, stronger than she was. She let go rather than be dragged, slashing at his fingers as the shaft passed her.

He cried out and dropped one hand. The spear struck the wall. Sariel kicked its butt away and went back beside Benjamin before Garrick could turn on her alone.

“Crossing,” she said.

“I know.”

They stepped back together past the chalked starting stone. The pale line showed at Sariel’s feet. Garrick came with them. Benjamin’s breath was loud now, and the point of Orren’s sword trembled when he held it still.

“You came a long way for my grain,” Garrick said.

“Millhaven’s,” Sariel said.

“Only while they could hold it.”

Sariel saw the opening he wanted her to take. His wounded arm hung a little away from his body, his left side exposed. She could rush it and meet the sword coming from his right, while the spearman found room behind her.

She moved backward instead.

Garrick’s expression changed. He had expected anger to make her simple. She took a mean, bright pleasure in disappointing him.

“She’s leaving,” Benjamin said.

“So are you,” Sariel told him.

The spearman came again. This time Benjamin drove his sword against the shaft while Sariel stepped inside its reach. She hit the man with her shoulder and cut across the outside of his thigh. He fell against the wall, dragging the spear with him. She left him there. His breath came in harsh, furious bursts, but he did not stand.

Garrick attacked while her attention was turned.

Benjamin met the first stroke. The second came lower. Sariel heard cloth tear before she understood what had happened. Benjamin’s left side opened beneath the edge of his jerkin, and he made a short sound through his teeth.

His sword went down. Garrick drew back to strike again.

Benjamin caught the sword wrist with his left hand and threw his right arm against Garrick’s collar, letting his own blade fall so he could seize the cloth. They struck the low wall together. Garrick’s weapon arm was stretched outward, held for an instant by Benjamin’s weight rather than his failing strength.

Sariel was already close.

She drove the knife up beneath Garrick’s right arm. It caught against something hard. She changed the angle, pushed again, and felt it enter. His body tightened around the blow. His mouth opened beside Benjamin’s face, but the sound that came out of it had no words.

Benjamin’s grip began to fail.

Sariel pulled the knife free and pushed Garrick away from him with both hands. For a moment all three leaned together, unable to find room for their feet. Then Garrick’s knees bent. He fell against the wall and slid down, taking a strip of Benjamin’s sleeve in his grasp.

The spearman stared at him. So did the man by the cart. Sariel stood with the knife low, breathing through her open mouth, and waited to see which of them would move.

The man at the cart ran first. He abandoned the strap and went north, shouting. The spearman tried to stand, failed, and began dragging himself away along the wall.

Sariel could have reached him.

Benjamin leaned against her shoulder. His weight answered before she had finished considering it.

“I’m here,” she said.

He looked down at his left side. Blood had reached his belt.

“I think—”

“I can see. Put your hand on it.”

He tried with his left. The angle hurt him, and she caught his wrist before he pulled it away.

“Stay there. I’ll find something.”

Her scarf was damp but not dirty enough to matter more than the blood leaving him. She folded it, pressed it against the cut, and put his right hand over it. He swore at her.

“Good,” she said. “Keep doing that.”

“The swearing?”

“Both.”

His sword lay beside the wall. She could see its repaired grip. She bent without taking her shoulder from under his, picked it up, and thrust it through her own belt on the far side. The bare edge lay away from her leg. She would deal with the stupidity of carrying it there when they were out of reach of men trying to kill them.

Garrick lay a few steps away. His head had fallen back against the stone. Sariel looked at him once. His eyes did not turn toward her.

Then she brought Benjamin to the crossing.

He managed the first boards himself. At the third his knee bent, and she had to take enough of his weight that the handrope pressed painfully into her back.

“Help,” she called.

The word carried better than any name. A shape moved on the home bank, then another. Halen came onto the deck with Kestra behind him. They stopped far enough away to keep the boards from filling with people.

“His right side,” Sariel told Halen. “Don’t lift under the left.”

Halen ducked under Benjamin’s right arm. Sariel kept pressure against the scarf while Benjamin used the unhurt side to lean on him. Kestra steadied the handrope and walked backward ahead of them, calling out each board edge.

Halfway across, the pale seam faded. It did not snap or tear. It thinned until the wet wood was only wet wood, with a smear of chalk visible at the home-side mark.

“Keep coming,” Kestra said. “Three boards. Two.”

Someone on the north bank shouted. An arrow struck the mill wall above the shelf, then fell into the water. Sariel lowered her head beside Benjamin’s and kept walking. Stopping would not make the next shot less likely to reach them.

They stepped onto earth.

“All home?” Thane called.

Sariel looked at the waiting men, at the young millworker with one bare foot, at the reserve defenders gathered beside the lane. She had counted each through. Kestra had confirmed the wall. Benjamin was beside her, still leaning heavily against Halen.

“All those we sent forward,” she said. “Benjamin, was there anybody behind you?”

He shook his head, then forced the word out when Thane waited.

“Nobody.”

“Deck away,” Thane said.

The crew moved.

Halen passed Benjamin to another man’s shoulder and went to his rope. The handropes came off their home-side hooks. Two helpers held down the long rear rails while the first pair at the hauling rope leaned backward. A pin caught under the load, and the man beside it reached too quickly.

“Lever,” Thane snapped.

The man took the lever, lifted the frame just enough to release the pressure, and drew the pin by its wooden crossgrip. He kept his fingers out of the joint. The panel rose onto the roller. Across the water its far end slid from the stone seat.

The people holding the tails pressed down. The frame stayed level long enough for the hauling crew to draw its weight back onto the bank. Then it came more easily, the rollers rumbling over the marked ground. The crew kept pulling until the whole panel lay ashore.

The other panel still crossed the channel. Sariel saw two men on the north bank looking at it. One had a sword. The other was the bowman who had shot at the mill wall. He began fitting another arrow.

A village woman raised her own bow beside the mill door. She did not speak. Her shot struck the earth in front of the swordsman. He backed away, and the bowman crouched behind him.

“Second,” Thane said.

The helpers changed to the remaining tail rails. Halen’s crew took the strain. This roller moved properly. The frame slid home, leaving water between the stone seats.

The men across it stayed where they were.

Sariel had expected to feel triumph when the gap opened. She felt only Benjamin sagging as the effort of staying upright became too much for him. She tightened her arm round his waist, above the wound, and told the man supporting his other side that they needed the stretcher.

Kestra had already gone for it.

They laid Benjamin down on the cleared ground beyond the rollers. Sariel moved with him, keeping the scarf pressed against his side. He looked very pale. When she asked whether he could hear her, his eyes moved with impatient recognition.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

“You keep saying that.”

She laughed once, badly, and pressed her forehead against his shoulder for the time it took to breathe. Then the bearers lifted him, and she walked beside the stretcher toward the orchard.

Elena met them at the broad stone. Someone had run ahead.

Sariel began explaining too quickly: left side, sword, he had walked, the blood had not stopped, she had used her scarf because—

“Keep holding it,” Elena said. “You’ve done that right. Tell me when we put him down.”

Sariel went quiet. The lane seemed longer than it had during practice. Benjamin kept his eyes closed until one bearer stumbled, then opened them to complain. Elena asked him two questions and listened to the answers before asking the next. Sariel could not remember what they were. She watched the scarf.

At the orchard they put him beneath the canvas, away from the place where Tom lay covered. Sariel saw the blanket and then Linett, sitting beside it, and the meaning of the two together reached her slowly.

“Oh,” she said.

Elena touched her wrist. “Sariel. I need to look.”

She let the scarf come away.

The cut was longer than she had thought. It ran across the left flank below the edge of Benjamin’s ribs, opening muscle as well as skin. Elena looked at it without giving Sariel a face to read. Vennit came to help. Between them they cut the rest of the cloth clear, checked where the bleeding came from, and packed clean folded linen against it.

“Hold this while I change places,” Elena told Sariel.

She did. It was work she could do without understanding the whole of it. Benjamin gripped her free hand when the pressure hurt.

“Sorry,” he said.

“Stop.”

“I meant your fingers.”

“Those too.”

His mouth moved a little at that. She kept her eyes on it while Vennit supported him through the next change of padding.

There was no great jet of blood, no single thing Elena could shut and finish. It welled from the cut, slowing beneath pressure, returning when the cloth moved. Elena worked in stages. She made Benjamin tell her where it hurt, checked his breathing, touched the uncut part of his belly and watched his face. When Sariel asked whether the blade had gone too deep, Elena answered carefully.

“I can’t find a sign of that now. I need to keep watching.”

“Will he—”

“We have him here. Help me keep him warm.”

Sariel took the blanket Vennit offered. She tucked it round the parts of Benjamin they were not treating, leaving the wounded side clear. His boots were wet. She unlaced them and put them under the canvas where he would be able to find them, because the possibility of his wanting his boots again was something she could arrange with her hands.

When the new dressing stayed only lightly marked, Elena sat back far enough to straighten her spine.

“We’ll move him to Vennit’s when the lane’s quiet. He stays lying down.”

Benjamin opened one eye. “I had no other plan.”

Elena looked at him. “You’ve surprised us before.”

Sariel laughed, properly this time. Benjamin looked pleased for half a moment before the effort hurt and he stopped smiling.

The reports came to the orchard because Thorne had come there to see Tom.

Sariel heard them from beside Benjamin. Garrick was dead; a watcher had seen men try to lift him, then leave him against the wall. Most of the gang had gone north in separate groups. Four remained within calling distance of the channel, two hurt badly enough that they could not keep up. The man with the thigh wound Sariel had given him was among them.

One of the village defenders wanted to leave all four where they were.

“They came to burn us.”

Thorne stood with his hand on the back of Linett’s chair. She looked up at the man.

“Yes,” she said. “They did.”

He waited for the rest. She had nothing else to give him. Her husband was under a blanket beside her.

Thorne asked where the men had put their weapons. On the ground by the north road, the watcher said. They were sitting apart from them.

“Keep them there. Give them water when we can do it safely.”

“And then?”

“We hold them. We take their names. The households hear what happened before anybody decides what they’re owed.”

Elena looked up from washing her hands. “The injured ones come to me.”

The defender began to argue. She let him finish, then asked who else in Millhaven could dress the wounds. He looked toward Vennit and seemed to realize he had not found a different answer.

Sariel stayed beside Benjamin while the afternoon passed. She got up only when Elena made her drink and when Orren’s sword caught against the stool she was trying to sit on. She had forgotten it was still through her belt. Halen took it, cleaned the worst of the blood from the blade, and put it in Benjamin’s own scabbard. Sariel watched him make certain the point was seated before he laid it within reach.

Near evening, Roven returned from checking the north road. He had gone with two watchers and come back by the western fields. The gang had broken into smaller parties farther up the road; none had turned south while he watched. He would not promise that none would return. Thorne asked him to help put one deck panel across for the four waiting prisoners, with guards on both banks and the other panel left ashore. When the prisoners had crossed, they would draw it back again.

The news from the mill was better than Sariel had expected. The drying house roof was gone. The cart shed had lost one corner of its roof and the door nearest the fire. Buckets, wet sacks, and the clear space beside the mill had kept the flames from its main building. The wheel had stopped because Halen had shut the water while men worked near the race. He wanted it running again before morning if the bearings had not been damaged.

The forge stood. Somebody had shut its door, as Linett had told Tom.

Sariel went to Linett while Benjamin slept. She did not have an adequate thing to say. She told her that Tom had made the crossing work.

“I know,” Linett said.

“He showed Halen how to stop the roller catching.”

“He told me about it.”

Sariel sat on the ground beside her chair. For a while they watched Elena and Vennit checking a burned leg at the other end of the canvas. Then Linett asked whether Sariel could fetch the cloth that had fallen beside Tom’s hand. Sariel picked it up and put it in her lap.

The prisoners reached the orchard before the last light went. Two walked between guards. The other two came on stretchers, one of them the spearman Sariel had cut. He recognized her and looked away. Vennit asked a guard to hold the lantern while she examined his thigh. Sariel heard Halen report that the panel was ashore again and the night watch had taken its places.

After dusk they carried Benjamin to Vennit’s cottage. Sariel walked at the stretcher’s head with a covered lantern. The square smelled of wet ashes and somebody’s supper. People moved aside to let them through, then went back to bringing buckets home, fastening shutters, looking for tools they had lent and hoped to see again.

At the cottage Elena checked the dressing once more. The blood had not spread far. Benjamin woke while they settled him on the bed and asked where his sword was.

“Here,” Sariel said.

“And you?”

She pulled a chair close enough that he could see her without turning his head.

“Here too.”

Elena left instructions and a cup of water within reach. Sariel put the lantern where it would not shine in Benjamin’s eyes. She meant to take her boots off. Instead she sat with one hand resting on the edge of the mattress, listening until his breathing settled.

Chapter 29 The work after

By morning Elena could no longer remember who had borrowed the small basin.

She stood beside Benjamin’s bed with a half-full cup of water in her hand, looking at the place where it should have been. Somebody had washed the boards. Somebody else had left a heel of bread beside her bag, wrapped so that she would not put an instrument on it. Benjamin watched her without speaking.

“Water,” she said.

He lifted the cup from her hand. “In this?”

“No. Yes. For you.”

He drank. She went to the door and called for the basin, then waited because she could not remember what she had intended to do while it came.

Under the orchard canvas, Elena had seen the blood before she could discover its source, and for several breaths he had become a body she could not afford to lose. Vennit had put a hand on her shoulder and asked for a lamp. They had worked until the bleeding stayed stopped, then taken turns watching him through the dark.

Now his skin was warm. He knew where he was. He wanted to sit up, which she refused until she had looked beneath the dressing.

“Will it open?” he asked.

“It might. I want it to have less reason.”

He nodded, then caught his lower lip between his teeth as she loosened the cloth. Sariel rose from the chair. She had slept in her boots, her knife laid on the floor within reach. Elena asked her to take the cup instead.

She did. Her fingers shook against its rim.

The dressing had stained but had not filled again. Elena looked, waited, looked once more. There were things the surface could not tell her. She asked Benjamin about pain elsewhere, about sickness, about the long hours after the fighting. He answered impatiently at first, then saw her face and tried to remember properly.

Vennit came in carrying the basin.

“I had it,” she said.

“Oh.”

“You gave it to me.”

Elena began to laugh. It was a poor sound, too close to something else. Vennit waited until it passed, then set the basin down.

“Eat the bread.”

“I need to—”

“I know what needs doing here.”

Elena went outside with the bread. She could hear Sariel asking Benjamin whether the water had been enough, her voice low and careful. He said it had. Then he asked her to come closer.

The sun was on the cottage wall. A man crossed the square carrying the headless shaft of a spear. He stopped by the well to help a woman lift a bucket, tucked the shaft beneath his arm, and went on. Elena sat on the step and ate with both hands. Halfway through she discovered she was hungry enough to feel sick.

They brought her a captive before she had finished.

He was young, though not as young as he wished to look. His left foot had swollen inside his boot. Halen walked beside him with a staff; another villager followed, carrying the prisoner’s belt and empty sheath. The man kept his hands visible even when he stumbled.

“Can’t put weight on it,” Halen said.

“I can see.”

“He says he fell after they gave up.”

The captive looked at her. “I did.”

Elena fetched a stool. When she asked him to sit, he waited for Halen’s nod. She disliked that she noticed. There were other people waiting for her, people who had carried the wounded through smoke, and this man’s coat still bore a strip of Garrick’s colors.

She cut the boot fastening rather than drag the swelling through it. He made no sound until her fingers reached the outside of the ankle. Then he jerked away and apologized.

“Don’t apologize. Tell me where.”

Halen watched while she examined it. His own cheek had healed unevenly from the collector’s blows. When the man asked whether he would walk again, Halen looked toward the well.

“I can’t tell you that this morning,” Elena said. “You can tell whoever moves you that I said you aren’t to march on it.”

“He’ll tell us you said he could go home.”

“Then come back and ask me.”

Halen nodded. She bound the ankle with help from the other guard. The captive stared at her hands.

“I wasn’t at the mill before,” he said.

She thought he meant that morning. Then she understood.

“Say that when they ask you what you did.”

“But you should know.”

“I know your foot hurts.”

She did not say it kindly. His mouth closed. When they had him upright, she made Halen fetch a shorter staff so that he could keep his weight off the foot. They left slowly. Elena went inside and found Vennit changing Benjamin’s linen. She stood beside her for a moment before admitting what she had said.

Vennit smoothed the clean edge beneath his hip.

“Did you leave anything undone?”

“No.”

“Then go and sleep. You can speak to him again when you have.”

Elena slept in the wool-store side room with the window open. Noise from the square came through her dreams: a wheel, a name called twice, somebody splitting wood. When Kestra woke her, there was warm broth beside the bed. She had written down who had asked for Elena, and Vennit had marked the people she had already seen.

Tom’s name was nowhere on the page.

Elena read to the bottom before she allowed herself to stop.

Linett did not want his best coat buried.

“He’d call it a waste,” she said, holding it across her arms. “And then he’d ask whether I’d searched the pockets.”

They were in the forge. Elena had come to see whether Linett needed anything and found her emptying the little cupboard behind the bench. There was more in it than Elena expected: string, a broken knife he had meant to mend, three buttons wrapped in paper, a child’s small iron horse with one leg missing. Linett set the horse on the bench and looked at it for a long time.

“Whose?” Elena asked.

“I’m not sure anymore.”

“Shall I ask?”

“No. Leave it.”

She handed Elena the coat. Its weight surprised her. She had only known Tom in work clothes, sleeves turned back, an impatient question ready before someone had finished explaining what was broken. The coat had a neat patch beneath one arm. Linett had sewn it in a darker thread which showed only when Elena held it near the window.

They searched the pockets. A nail. A folded piece of leather. A scrap with three measurements, the middle one crossed out. Linett took the leather and rubbed it between her fingers.

“He was making a handle cover. For my knife.”

Elena waited.

“I told him not to bother. I said I’d put up with it this long.” Linett sat on the bench. “It pinches here.”

She showed Elena the place at the base of her thumb. Elena took the hand, then let it go when she realized Linett had not asked for treatment.

“Keep the leather,” she said.

“I will.”

They buried Tom above the wet ground, where the lane toward the chapel rose between old trees. People who had not fought came carrying food for those who had. The woman he had helped out of the fire sat with her burned leg supported on a stool. Vennit had brought a blanket for her knees. She kept folding its edge between her fingers.

Thane stood beside Linett. He had asked Elena whether he could carry one end and been furious before she answered. Now his right hand lay in its sling. His left hand twisted the loose end of the sling.

There were words over the grave, some formal, some spoken too quietly for Elena to hear. She remembered Tom lying on the practice stretcher, complaining that he might recover before they reached her. She had laughed. It seemed impossible that she could still remember exactly how much she had enjoyed it.

Linett put the scrap with the measurements into the ground.

“He never did tell me what these were for.”

A man behind Elena gave a small laugh and tried to swallow it. Linett looked toward him.

“He’d remember by supper,” the man said.

“Yes.”

Her face changed. She covered it with one hand. Thane put his left arm around her and stayed when she leaned her weight against him. Elena looked at the newly turned earth until she could see it clearly again.

They walked back slowly. At the forge Linett unlocked the door herself. She did not go inside at once.

“I’ll open it tomorrow,” she said.

Nobody told her she need not.

Elena found Thane there three days later with a strip of leather between his teeth.

“Take that out.”

He looked up, offended by the familiarity of the instruction, then obeyed. Linett had set her knife on the bench. A little wooden frame held it with the handle toward Thane. He was trying to draw a thong through two holes with his left hand.

“Is it pulling?” Elena asked.

“My hand?”

“Yes.”

“Everything pulls.”

She sat beside him. The swelling had eased enough that he could see more of the shape he recognized. Two fingers remained reluctant to straighten. On some touches he felt too much; on others she had to ask whether he felt anything. She warmed her hands before examining him because he had begun to flinch from cold.

“Better?” he asked when she finished.

“Some movement is better.”

He waited.

“I don’t know how much will return.”

“I know you don’t.”

She left his hand resting on the cloth. Linett came in carrying a narrow plank and said the sawyer wanted payment before cutting anything else. Thane began to rise. She put the plank down.

“I didn’t ask you to go.”

“I can talk to him.”

“So can I. Tell me whether that piece will do.”

He examined the end grain, asked her to turn it, and pointed out a split. Linett marked the sound length with chalk. They discussed the doors damaged in the fighting, which repairs could wait, and what work required a smith they could pay. The forge would keep taking ordinary mending. Linett had laid out the winter orders beside the money already paid against them. Two customers wanted their deposits back; she had returned one and arranged a date for the other. The rest wanted the work. She turned each order toward Thane, asking which materials they had and which must still be bought. For the larger work she intended to make an arrangement with a neighboring smith. She had sent a message, and if his price was absurd she would send another somewhere else.

Thane asked whether she wanted him at the bench.

“Yes. When you can work. Tell me what you use and what you finish. Don’t tell people you can do it cheaper because you feel bad.”

His face closed.

Linett waited until he looked at her again.

“I need to know what I can promise them,” she said.

He nodded. “I can make the frames. Show someone how to set the pins. The small fitting takes me longer.”

“Then we allow longer.”

She tried the leather-covered knife. Thane watched her thumb.

“Again,” he said.

She squeezed the handle, turned it, squeezed once more.

“That will do.”

He smiled down at the frame. Elena left them deciding whether he could improve the fastening. By the door she saw the little iron horse. Linett had put it on the highest shelf, where nobody clearing the bench would mistake it for scrap.

The square filled with other people’s belongings.

A saddle claimed by two men. A bundle of clothes found beneath a cart. Tools recovered from a shelter Garrick’s men had abandoned. Halen spent most of a morning stopping people from taking what they recognized until Thorne could hear both claims. Then Thorne sent him to sleep and put somebody else there.

The captives were being fed. Elena saw to their injuries alongside the village wounded. The young man with the swollen ankle told her his name the next time she visited, and she remembered to use it while she changed the binding. She still could not decide how much of his account she believed. When he asked for a second blanket, she passed the request on.

There were arguments about letting any of them go. There were arguments about keeping men through winter when grain remained short. Thorne wrote down the separate accusations, asked for the people who had seen them, and refused to let the whole question be settled by whoever could speak longest without drawing breath. Some men would answer particular claims. Others, whose households could be found and whose accounts witnesses supported, would be allowed to leave. Weapons remained held while ownership was established. Elena knew how often those decisions would be repeated badly by the time they reached the next village. She had begun to ask Kestra to read things back before she signed her name.

The Baroness’s envoy returned after the crossing had been opened to carts again.

He came with a clerk and a case. Elena was returning from the orchard when she saw him wait while a cart used the newly seated deck. He stepped aside to let the wheel clear, then walked into the square. No soldiers followed him.

The meeting took place in the wool store. Elena had been asked to attend because the village’s account included medicine, linen, and care still owed. She brought the list Vennit had checked and discovered that her hands were shaking when she put it on the table. The envoy took off his gloves. She remembered the cheese he had brought before the fighting.

He did not offer grain this time.

Thorne laid out the existing market rights and the copies of the provision accounts. The originals from the lime yard stayed wrapped until the clerk asked to see the numbered entry. Kestra opened the cloth. Nobody hurried while he compared it with the sheet before him.

“Other copies remain with the receiving houses,” Thorne said.

“We know.”

The answer made the room quiet.

The proposed agreement recognized the village’s current management of the mill road and market, with the old payment continuing. No oath of fealty appeared beneath it. The envoy called this a clarification of existing practice. Thorne read that phrase, then asked the clerk to write the practice plainly: the village kept its own watch, chose its own officers, and retained its market proceeds subject to the recorded due.

The clerk looked at the envoy. The envoy nodded.

They argued over the next clause. The Baroness’s household wanted a statement that all claims arising from the recent trouble were concluded. Thorne put Elena’s list beside it.

“Whose claims?”

“The intention is to prevent renewed hostilities.”

“Then say that. I cannot settle her account.” He pointed to Linett, seated near the window. “Or hers. Or the people still asking where their grain went.”

Elena had expected Linett to speak. She remained quiet, her hands folded on her lap, letting Thorne wait for the answer.

The clause was narrowed. They agreed that no immediate collection or entry by armed household men would be sought under the failed protection proposal. Ordinary trade would resume without that proposal being treated as an accepted bargain. The separate claims remained separate. It did not promise that the Baroness would never want the road again.

Thorne asked each witness whether they had heard the wording. Then the clerk made the copies.

Elena signed for the care she could account for. Her name looked small beneath the list. She had no seal. Kestra dried the ink and gave her the sheet to check once more.

When the envoy had gone, Linett asked whether that meant the village had been paid.

“No,” Thorne said.

“Good. I wanted to be certain everyone knew.”

That evening Elena went back to Vennit’s with medicines bought from the village’s own small fund for care.

Benjamin could sit in a chair now. Sariel had taken him to the door, where he watched the square with the concentration he once gave a practice blade. He looked up as Elena passed and asked whether she would eat with them.

“I have dressings to cut.”

“After that?”

“And the man with the foot.”

“After him?”

She stopped. Sariel waited beside his chair, a hand resting on its back.

“Are you hungry?” Elena asked.

“Yes,” Benjamin said. “I meant you.”

Inside, Vennit had the cloth laid out. Elena set down the bottles and began telling her what remained. Vennit listened, handed her a pair of shears, and took the other side. They cut enough for the next changes. The rest stayed folded.

“I’ll see the foot when I go past,” Vennit said.

“You’ve been working all day.”

“So have you. I ate at midday.”

Elena looked toward the open door. Benjamin was laughing at something Sariel had said, then holding his side because laughter still hurt. She wanted to be annoyed with them. Instead she wanted to know what the joke had been.

“I’ll come back before bed.”

“Yes.”

She washed her hands, slowly enough to get the smell of medicine out of the creases, and went to join them. Kestra and Thane were waiting at Mira’s. There was a place between them, a bowl set upside down to keep it clean. Elena turned it over and held it out for supper.

Chapter 30 The next commission

The first snow came through the roof above Benjamin’s bed.

He woke Sariel by putting one cold finger against her forehead. She caught his wrist before she opened her eyes.

“Don’t.”

“I wanted a witness.”

“To what?”

He pointed. A little white heap had formed on the blanket beside his knee. Another flake descended through the dark line between two boards, turned once in the air of the loft, and disappeared against the wool.

Sariel sat up. Across the room Kestra was asleep with her mouth open, one hand still between the pages of her book. Thane had managed to get his stockinged feet outside every layer of bedding. From the side room came the sound of Elena turning over.

“Move your bed,” Sariel whispered.

“I like this bed.”

“Move it with you in it, then.”

He grinned, but when he tried to rise the pull in his left side stopped him. He remained on one elbow, waiting. Sariel put her hand behind his shoulder. They had learned to do that without making it into a question every time.

After a while he sat upright.

“It was better yesterday.”

“You climbed the mill stair six times yesterday.”

“Five.”

“You went back for the whistle.”

He looked at her. “You noticed.”

“I was on the stairs.”

She brought his coat. He put it on slowly, leaving the last fastening for her because twisting to find it hurt. The wound had closed before the cold weather. Some mornings that seemed the least useful thing she knew about it. He could walk the raised lane, carry a modest load, stand through a short drill. Then a movement he had made all his life would leave him white around the mouth.

They shifted the bed together. Sariel went to fetch a bowl for the snow and found that Thane was watching them.

“Don’t climb onto it,” he said.

“The bed?”

“The roof. From outside, yes. Inside you’ll put your knee through the boards.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were looking.”

He rolled over, pulled the blanket over his feet, and resumed sleeping.

At the bakery Mira made Benjamin sit beside the oven while Sariel ate standing up, too restless for a chair. The square had turned pale. Wheel ruts showed black through the snow, and a boy was drawing another boy’s face on the well with a wet finger. Halen came past carrying two poles. He knocked snow from his boots before entering.

“Evening drill’s in the store,” he told Benjamin. “The lower room. Thorne said we can move the benches if we put them back.”

“I’ll come down.”

“You can watch from your stairs.”

Benjamin began to object, then looked at the hand he had pressed against his coat.

“Don’t put the rail across the door.”

“I remember.”

“And warm up before you start hitting things.”

Halen nodded with an expression Sariel had seen on Benjamin when Orren repeated an instruction he already knew. The resemblance hurt, then pleased her. She took another piece of bread.

That afternoon she held the ladder while a village roofer replaced the cracked board. He charged for the board and the work. Thorne arranged payment for the greater part from the village repair money; the five paid for a loose shutter they had wanted fixed since autumn. Sariel stood in the falling snow and listened to the hammering above her. When the man climbed down, she asked him to show her the split. He did, taking her finger and running it along the old grain so she could feel what she had failed to see from beneath.

Upstairs, Benjamin had put his bed back.

She threw the empty bowl at his blanket.

In the depth of winter Sariel learned the sound of Thane’s good days before she saw him.

On good days he argued with Linett. On bad ones he was agreeable, which usually meant something hurt or had fallen from his hand while nobody was watching. The forge had work again: winter repairs, the first tools brought in by people who meant to be ready for spring, a wagon fitting which had occupied half the floor until Linett threatened to charge it rent. A smith from the next market came on agreed days for work that needed his skill. Thane paid for his use of the bench by mending what he could and drawing what other hands would have to make.

Sariel brought him a little lock in a cloth.

“Mine?” he asked.

“The latch on the side room. Elena wants it to work from inside without a key.”

“Does she?”

“She says somebody enters whenever she washes.”

“That’s because somebody always wants her.”

Sariel laid the lock down. “She wants a latch.”
