Thane considered it, then nodded. He trapped the casing in a wooden cradle screwed to the bench. A broad strap held it still while his left hand worked. His right rested beside it, two fingers bent farther than the others. There was more movement now than there had been at the first frost. She had stopped asking him to show her.

He let her take out the spring. She chose the wrong hook, felt it slip, and changed it before he commented. Hest’s case lay open between them. One pocket was empty because the fine pick had cracked during a household repair; its replacement was thicker at the handle and carried the small filed notch Sariel preferred.

Thane watched her set the new latch arm.

“You could make that turn shorter.”

“Then it catches the plate.”

“Only if the plate stays there.”

She looked at him, then at the lock. He smiled.

They had the mechanism working by the time Linett came in with hot cups. She tried it herself, asked whether Elena could lift the catch with wet fingers, and made them broaden it. Sariel grumbled. Thane told Linett she had been about to suggest that.

“I’m sure she was.”

Linett set Tom’s cup at the back of the bench without looking at it, then handed Sariel one of the others. Sariel drank and asked what she owed for the strip of metal.

“Bring back the offcut.”

“That isn’t payment.”

“It is if you bring back the one I asked for.”

They stayed until the light began to fail. Thane talked Sariel through a catch she could release wearing gloves. When she took the finished latch home, Elena left her wash unfinished to try it. She closed the door. The catch clicked.

Sariel stood outside, smiling.

“Does it hold?”

“Yes.”

“Then stop trying it.”

Letters reached them in a bundle tied to Tema’s accounts, passed from one cart to another until the outer paper smelled of apples and damp leather.

Finn’s had been written by Dena. His name at the bottom was his own, pressed so hard into the page that Sariel could feel it through the back. He was still at Alderfold. Fox had recovered the habit of stealing things it could neither eat nor use. Dena had required Finn to return a glove to its owner and explain why the other glove had been found beneath the fox’s bedding. There were three young animals to tend, a mule with a difficult foot, and a shed Finn had helped roof. He wanted to know whether Benjamin had learned to carry less.

Benjamin, seated beneath the repaired roof, protested that this was a malicious question to send so far.

Tavia had paid someone to carry her own letter. The paper was better, and she had left wide margins simply because she could. She copied correspondence for a woman who supplied cloth to households in two market towns. The work included accounts she disliked, letters she enjoyed, and a room which she could shut without waiting for six other people to finish dressing. The shoes had lasted eleven days before she sold them.

Elena laughed until she had to hand the page to Kestra.

Zara’s note was shorter. Tema had allowed her to arrange two loads herself; she had made a mistake about dry storage, paid to correct it, and still earned more than she would have by taking the first price offered. She enclosed a description of a medicine seller for Elena and asked them to write how Thane’s hand was, in his words if possible.

He took the paper when Kestra offered it.

“Can you write for me?” he asked.

Kestra brought the ink. He dictated slowly at first, then made her cross out a whole line because it sounded more cheerful than he meant. Sariel went to the window to give them room. Beyond the glass, Halen was leading the evening watch across the square. Benjamin watched with her, his shoulder warm against hers.

Tema returned while dirty snow still lay beneath the northern hedges.

He found them at the wool store, where Sariel was oiling a buckle and Benjamin was being made to walk with his pack. Elena had put the pack on the scales herself. Thane sat on the table with his boots clear of the papers. Kestra had asked him to move twice and was apparently preparing a third attempt.

“I see you’re ready,” Tema said.

Benjamin stopped. “For what?”

“That is what I hoped to discuss.”

He removed his wet coat and hung it on a peg instead of waiting for someone to take it. Sariel liked him for that more than she liked the folded contract he brought from its pocket.

Seed grain, he told them. Several small consignments gathered into one train, bound toward Valvano Ford as the roads opened. Buyers wanted it before their fields were ready. Some stretches had suffered through winter. Others had acquired men who claimed the right to be paid for standing beside a road.

“How many carts?” Benjamin asked.

“Four. Drivers for each. Two spare hands, one of whom knows harness better than I do. I am coming as far as the Ford.”

“Zara?” Elena asked.

“Stays with the westward trade. She has her own trouble to negotiate.”

“Does she call it that?”

“She calls it mine.”

They laughed. Tema spread the agreement. Sariel brought her chair closer.

He had named a payment for the five together. She worked it out in her head, lost her place, and waited while Kestra divided it on paper. Her share would pay the lodging they intended to keep and leave money toward a better coat. It would also require her to walk roads she did not know beside grain other people badly needed.

“Half before we set out,” Kestra said.

“A third.”

“We have to buy what we carry.”

“I provide road food.”

“Which food?” Elena asked.

Tema looked at her, then took out a second sheet. They moved the cups and read that too. Thane asked about room for tools and a seat when his hand made carrying painful. Benjamin wanted to know whether Tema expected them to guard the grain or collect the buyers’ debts. Sariel asked who decided when a road had become too dangerous for the carts.

By then Tema had taken off his gloves.

“I am paying you to bring them through.”

“Yes,” she said. “Suppose I come back and tell you the bank has fallen. Or there are twelve men at the next turn.”

“You tell me what you saw.”

“And while we discuss it?”

“The carts wait.”

She held his gaze until he wrote that down.

They would scout the next stretch, keep the train together where it needed guarding, help settle obstructions they could reasonably settle, and protect the people with it. They were not being hired to seize seed from an unwilling buyer or take a fortified crossing by force. Tema reserved the right to choose another route after hearing them. The five reserved the right to refuse one they could explain was beyond their strength. If they could not agree, they would settle the work already done and return the unearned part of the advance.

The payment took longer. They settled on half before leaving, the rest at the Ford, and an added daily amount for delays after the agreed span. Ordinary weather days within that span were theirs to endure. Tema’s decision to wait for another load would be his to pay for.

Then Elena placed one finger on the space beneath those terms.

“If someone is hurt doing the work, we don’t put them down at the next house and continue.”

Tema’s expression changed. “I have never proposed that.”

“I know. Put what we do instead.”

They allowed space on a cart for a person who could not walk. Medicine consumed in the work would be replaced from a stated reserve. If care required leaving the train, earned pay would remain due, and the first obligation would be to get the patient to help. Sariel watched Thane read that part with Kestra. He asked her to change incapable to unable to continue, then said either word was probably worse than being the person in question.

Nobody hurried him.

Tema went to Mira’s while they spoke without him. Benjamin laid a hand on the contract.

“I want to go.”

Elena looked at his side.

“I know,” he said. “We test it again. If I can’t walk the distance, we change what I do. But I want work that isn’t waiting for someone to come here.”

“I want to see what the road needs,” Thane said. “There are only so many ways to mend the same mill barrow.”

“Linett says you haven’t found the best one yet,” Sariel told him.

“She is preserving my humility.”

“I want to learn another working,” Kestra said. She touched the bronze disc beneath her collar. “I’ll write to Three Oaks from the Ford. Mareth said I could keep it through this journey. Afterward I must return it or arrange its replacement.”

“And you?” Sariel asked Elena.

“I want the pay.” She looked startled by how blunt it sounded, then smiled. “I want a room with shelves and enough linen. I can’t make that out of people being grateful.”

Sariel ran a finger beneath the edge of her buckle. The leather was good. She had bought it herself.

“I want to come back here afterward,” she said.

Benjamin turned toward her.

“That wasn’t the question,” she added. “I know. But that’s what makes me want to go.”

He put his hand over hers. They left it there while Kestra called Tema back.

The week before departure filled with small pleasures Sariel had been too poor to expect.

Boot soles which did not admit water at the first puddle. A hood that turned with her head. Fine wire stored in a tube instead of bent around a scrap of wood. She tested the buckle on her new field bag so often that Benjamin asked whether she expected it to escape. His own sword had a sound scabbard mouth now, and the leather at the hilt fitted his hand. He still used Orren’s blade. On the last trial walk he had kept pace for the whole morning and still been able to straighten when they put the packs down. When he drew the sword in the lower room, Halen watched his feet before trying the movement himself.

Thane reduced his tool roll, argued with it, and put two things back. Linett fitted a strap to hold it against his hip. He tried the weight, flexed the fingers of his right hand, and asked her to move the fastening where his left could reach it. She did.

Elena and Vennit spent an afternoon packing medicines. Benjamin brought a little flask and asked whether they wanted it filled with drinking water. Vennit smelled it, made a face, and put it among the bottles to be washed. Kestra made a label large enough for him to read from the doorway. He objected to the lettering; nobody altered it.

Kestra fetched her scroll case from Thorne’s sister on the western rise. It smelled faintly of the dried herbs kept on the shelf beside it. Sariel watched her check the account of the Coin, page by page, before placing it with the saved scroll. Her working book went into a separate pocket where she could reach it on the road.

They paid for the loft through their expected return and left money against an additional stretch. Thorne kept a second key. Linett agreed on what use of the workshop would mean when they came back. Sariel had a place for the things she chose to leave.

At their last supper Mira put the cooking pot in the middle of the table.

It had been scrubbed. The bottom still bore the dark mark of a fire made during the first days after the canal. Sariel could remember Thane carrying wet ashes in it, Benjamin trying to carry everything else, Elena sitting down so abruptly beside the road that they had finally stopped.

“Whose?” Mira asked.

“All of ours,” Benjamin said.

“Then one of you can explain why it is in my cupboard.”

They began assigning it to one another. Thane demonstrated that his new fastening could carry it, then objected to the noise it made against his tools. Kestra said she already had the papers. Elena said the papers did not become heavier when mentioned. Benjamin reached for the handle.

Sariel put her hand on it first.

“I’ll carry it to the first halt. Then we change.”

“You scout,” he said.

“After the first halt. Until then I know this road.”

He released the handle.

Mira brought stew. Linett came late with flour on one sleeve from helping carry something she said nobody had warned her would leak. Thorne brought a loaf which Mira inspected without comment, an act of restraint so visible that everyone laughed. They made room for Halen and Roven. The table grew crowded. Sariel found herself telling the story before she had decided to tell it.

“Hest once charged a man twice for opening his own cupboard.”

Kestra lifted her head.

“He’d locked his keys inside. She opened it, and he accused her of taking too little time. He said any child could do what he’d just seen.”

“What did she do?” Halen asked.

“Shut it.”

Benjamin began laughing.

“She told him to fetch a child. He stood there explaining that he’d meant something else, and she waited with her hands folded. I was beside her trying to look as though I’d never found anything funny.”

“Did he fetch one?”

“No. He paid again.” Sariel smiled down at her bowl. “She gave me the second payment. Said I’d earned it by not offering to help.”

She could see Hest’s hands, the fold of skin at one knuckle, the way she had examined the cupboard’s warped door before touching the lock. For a moment the table blurred.

Benjamin’s knee rested against hers.

“I miss her,” Sariel said.

Kestra nodded. “So do I.”

They ate quietly for a little while. Then Benjamin told them how Orren had pretended not to notice a practice sword broken by three people taking turns to see whether it could lift a barrel. He did not improve their stupidity for the story. Sariel laughed with her whole chest, tears still wet beside her nose. Mira demanded to know who had owned the barrel.

When the bowls were empty they stayed. Elena accepted another piece of bread. Thane leaned back with his bad hand warm beneath his coat. Outside, someone shut the forge door, and the sound carried clearly through the quiet square.

Halen had the watch when they left.

He stood at the western road with two people Benjamin had taught, checking a cart whose driver thought being known should save him the trouble of answering. Halen finished before coming over. He clasped Benjamin’s arm carefully, then less carefully when Benjamin pulled him into an embrace.

“Keep the lane clear,” Benjamin said.

“Go away,” Halen told him.

Roven was there to see Tema onto the road. Mira had given them bread wrapped separately so they would not eat it all before the first stop. Sariel carried the pot. It knocked once against her thigh; she adjusted the cord until it stopped.

At the turn she told the others where she would go ahead and where she would wait. Kestra found the place on her copy of the route. Thane asked whether the ground there was dry enough to sit. Elena said they would discover that together.

Benjamin came up beside Sariel. Behind them the village remained visible between the hedges, the mill roof dark against the morning. She looked until the curve of the road began to take it away.

Then she turned to the four beside her and went on toward the carts.
